Plan and implement private endpoints and private links
When referring to a private endpoint in Azure, you are referring to a network interface with a 
private IP address obtained from a virtual network. This network interface is then connected 
privately and securely to an Azure service via a Private Link. In this case, the Azure service can 
be Azure Storage, Azure SQL, an Azure Cosmos DB, or your own service using a Private Link 
service.
When you use private endpoints, the traffic is secured to a Private Link resource. The plat -
form does an access control validation to check the network connections are reaching only 
the specified Private Link resource. You will need extra private endpoints if you need to access 
more resources within the same Azure service. 
 
It is very important to mention that private endpoint enables connectivity between the 
consumers from the same virtual network, regionally peered virtual networks, globally peered 
virtual networks, on-premises using VPN or Express Route, and services powered by Private 
Link. Another important consideration is that network connections will only be allowed to be 
initiated by clients that are connecting to the private endpoint. Service providers don’t have a 
routing configuration to create connections into service consumers.
MORE INFO PRIVATE LINK RESOURCES
For more information about the available Private Link resources, see http://aka.ms/az500plink .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks126
 
An Azure Private Link service is the reference to your service powered by Azure Private Link. 
After you create a Private Link service, Azure will generate a globally unique, named moniker 
called an “alias” based on the name you provide for your service.
 
When configuring your Azure SQL database, you can restrict access to a specific network 
using the server- or database-level firewall rules. These rules can enable or disable client access 
to all the databases within the same SQL Database server. These rules are stored in the master 
database.
If your database is accessible from the Internet and a computer tries to connect to it, the 
firewall first checks the originating IP address of the request against the database-level IP 
firewall rules for the database that the connection requests. If the address isn’t within a range 
in the database-level IP firewall rules, the firewall checks the server-level IP firewall rules.
The server-level firewall rules can be configured via the Azure portal, whereas the database-
level firewall needs to be configured on the database itself by using the 
sp_set_database_ 
firewall_rule SQL command. To configure the server-level firewall, follow these steps:
1. Navigate to the Azure portal at https://portal.azure.com .
2. In the search bar, type database,  and under Services, click SQL Databases.
3. Click the database for which you want to modify the server-level firewall settings.
4. On the Overview  page, click the Set Server Firewall button, as shown in Figure 2-20.
FIGURE 2-20 Selecting the option to configure the server-level firewall
5. The Firewall settings page appears, as shown in Figure 2-21.
6. In the Public Network Access section, you can disable public access or allow it only for 
selected networks.
7.
 In the Virtual Networks  section, you can create your firewall rules to allow only certain 
public IP addresses to access your resources.
8. Once you finish configuring, click the Save button.
NOTE DEPLOYMENT CONSIDERATIONS
Although the Private Link resource can be deployed in a different region than the virtual 
network and private endpoint, the private endpoint must be deployed in the same region and 
subscription as the virtual network.

Azure Key Vault Firewall
Like the previous resources, Azure Key Vault also allows you to create network access restric-
tions by using the Key Vault Firewall, which applies to Key Vault’s data plane. This means that 
firewall rules won’t affect operations such as creating a new vault or deleting or modifying the 
settings. Below are two use-case scenarios for the Azure Key Vault Firewall:
 ■ Contoso needs to implement Azure Key Vault to store application encryption keys.  
Contoso wants to block access to its keys for requests coming from the Internet.
 ■ Fabrikam implemented Azure Key Vault, and now it needs to lock down access to its 
keys and enable access only to Fabrikam’s applications and a shortlist of specific hosts.
To configure Azure Key Vault Firewall, you should first enable the Key Vault Logging using 
the following sequence of PowerShell commands:
$storagea = New-AzStorageAccount -ResourceGroupName ContosoResourceGroup -Name 
fabrikamkeyvaultlogs -Type Standard_LRS -Location 'East US'
$kvault = Get-AzKeyVault -VaultName 'ContosoKeyVault'
Set-AzDiagnosticSetting -ResourceId $kvault.ResourceId -StorageAccountId $storagea.Id 
-Enabled $true -Category AuditEvent
In this sequence, you will create a new storage account to store the logs, obtain the Key 
Vault information, and configure the diagnostic setting for your Key Vault.
After finishing this part, you can go to the Azure portal, open your Key Vault, and in the 
left navigation pane under the Settings  section, click Networking  > Firewalls And Virtual 
Networks , and then click Allow Public Access From Specific Virtual Networks And IP 
Addresses. Figure 2-22 shows the result of this selection.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks128
FIGURE 2-22 Azure Key Vault private endpoint connections
On this page, you can click create rules to allow custom access. Remember that once you 
configure those rules, users can only perform Key Vault data plane operations when their 
requests originate from this list of allowed virtual networks. The same applies when users try to 
perform data plane operations from the portal, such as listing the keys.
 
In Figure 2-22, notice the Allow Trusted Microsoft Services To Bypass This Firewall 
option, which is selected by default. This will allow the following services to have access to your 
Key Vault regardless of the firewall configuration: Azure Virtual Machines deployment service, 
Azure Resource Manager template deployment service, Azure Application Gateway v2 SKU,

Plan and implement network integration for Azure App 
Service and Azure functions
Azure App Service is an HTTP-based service for hosting web applications, REST APIs, and 
mobile backends. Azure App Service Environment (ASE) is an Azure App Service feature that

provides an isolated and dedicated environment for securely running App Service apps in the 
cloud. You can create multiple ASEs to host multiple apps running in Windows, Linux, Docker, 
mobile, and function apps.
 
To configure security for Azure App Service, you need to understand the variety of options 
available. Azure App Service has built-in security controls that can be leveraged to enhance the 
overall security posture of your apps. Essentially, some of these controls are Azure components 
described throughout this chapter. Table 2-3 summarizes the security controls that can be used 
with Azure App Service.
TABLE 2-3 Advantages and limitations
Layer Security control Description
Network Service Endpoint You can use access restrictions to define a priority-ordered

is an important practice to limit exposure to inbound network 
traffic.

Besides the available security controls that are inherited from Azure, you should also ensure 
that you are always developing your apps using the latest versions of supported platforms, 
programming languages, protocols, and frameworks. It is very important that you properly 
configure the authentication for these apps throughout the development lifecycle. Always 
ensure that authentication is required and anonymous access is disabled unless the scenario’s 
description clearly states that it must be enabled. You can also enhance your authentication 
security by requiring clients to use a certificate to authenticate. This practice improves security 
by allowing connections only from clients that can authenticate using certificates that you 
provide.
As part of your secure configuration of App Service, make sure that data in transit is pro -
tected, which means that you should always redirect HTTP to HTTPS traffic and enforce the lat -
est version of the TLS protocol. Communications from your Azure App Service and other Azure 
resources, such as Azure Storage, should also be encrypted. If the scenario description requires 
you to transfer files from your Azure app to another location using FTP, make sure that you are 
utilizing FTPS instead.
Some of the overall security recommendations for Azure A pp Service will also be surfaced in 
Microsoft Defender for Cloud, as shown in Figure 2-23.
Microsoft Defender for Cloud will perform this security assessment on your apps, part of 
the Microsoft Defender for Cloud security posture management. However, if you enable the 
Defender for App Service plan, you will also get threat detection for App Service. Microsoft 
Defender for App Service threat detection includes analytics and machine-learning models 
that cover all interfaces that allow customers to interact with their applications, whether over 
HTTP or through one of the management methods.
To ensure your App Service or Azure Function is secure, you also need to address the 
authentication. By default, authentication and authorization are disabled. Upon enabling it, 
every incoming HTTP request passes through it before your application code handles it. The 
authentication and authorization module runs separately from your application code and is 
configured using app settings.

If the Contoso administrator’s requirement is to securely store and manage the data the 
company’s app uses, Azure AD is the identity provider that addresses this requirement because 
Azure AD allows you to use CDS.
Because App Service is a Platform as a Service (PaaS), the operating system (OS) and appli-
cation stack are managed for you by Azure, which means you don’t need to worry about soft-
ware updates. Azure manages OS patching on two levels: the physical servers and the guest 
VMs that run the App Service resources. Both will follow the regular Microsoft Patch Tuesday 
update cycle once a month unless it is a Zero Day patch, which will be handled with higher 
priority and probably out of band (outside the regular Patch Tuesday cycle). When a new major 
or minor version is added to App Service, it is installed alongside the existing versions.
App Service preserves its Service Level Agreement (SLA) even during the patch updates, 
which means that even if a patch requires a VM to restart, it will not affect App Service  
production because there will always be a capacity buffer.
Access to patches in the registry at

CurrentVersion\Component Based Servicing\Packages  is locked down, though basic  
info regarding OS and runtime updates can be queried using Kudu Console at

Azure App Service Firewall
You might also want to harden the network access for your apps that are deployed via Azure 
App Service. Although the terminology used in this section refers to Azure App Service  
Firewall, what you are really implementing is a network-level access-control list. The access 
restrictions capability in Azure App Service is implemented in the App Service front-end roles. 
These front-end roles are upstream of the worker hosts where your code runs.
A common exam scenario for implementing this capability is when you need to restrict 
access to your app from certain VNets or the Internet. On the AZ-500 exam, make sure to  
carefully read the scenario because, in this case, you are adding restrictions to access the app 
itself, not the host.
To configure access restrictions on your Azure App Services, open the Azure portal and the 
App Services dashboard, click your app service or Azure function, and click Networking  in the 
Settings section. There, you will see options to configure inbound and outbound settings.
Plan and implement network security configurations for an 
App Service Environment (ASE)
An App Service Environment (ASE) is the aggregation of a series of capabilities deployed into a 
subnet in a customer’s Azure Virtual Network instance. There are two deployment types  
for ASE:
 ■ External  Accessible from the Internet
 ■ Internal load balancer  Private to your internal Azure infrastructure
Regardless of the type, the capabilities that comprise an ASE are:
 ■ Front ends using HTTP or HTTPS and terminating in an App Service Environment
 ■ Workers, which comprise resources that host the apps
 ■ Database that holds the data that defines the environment
 ■ Storage that hosts the customer-published apps
You can create multiple ASEs within a single Azure region or across multiple Azure regions. 
The selection will be dictated by the type of application you are building and the business 
requirements. ASEs are appropriate for application workloads that require high scalability, 
isolation, secure network access, high memory utilization, and high requests per second (RPS). 
 
IMPORTANT ASE V1, V2, AND V3

comparison table at https://aka.ms/AZ500ASEv2vsv3 . W hen this chapter was written,  
versions 1 and 2 were scheduled to retire in August 2024.

Plan and implement network security configurations for an 
Azure SQL Managed Instance
When planning your network security configurations for Azure SQL Managed Instance, you 
need to start with the core security baseline available in the Microsoft Cloud Security Bench-
mark (MCSB). MCSB is the default baseline used by Microsoft Defender for Cloud.
MCSB was created with the idea of providing a canonical set of Azure-centric techni-
cal security controls based on controls defined by the Center for Internet Security (CIS), the 
National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), and the Payment Card Industry (PCI). 
MCSB comprises security controls and a service baseline. A security control (also called a com-
pliance control) specifies a feature or activity that needs to be addressed but is not necessarily 
related to a technology or implementation. For example, network security is one of the security 
controls available in MCSB. 
It contains specific actions that must be addressed to help ensure that the network is more 
secure. A service baseline is basically the implementation of the control in individual Azure 
services. For example, an organization needs to improve its database security posture, and 
implementing an Azure SQL security baseline is one action that can be taken.
MCSB contains the Azure security baseline for Azure SQL, which comprises the following 
relevant sections:
 ■ Network Security
 ■ NS-1 Establish network segmentation boundaries
 ■ NS-2 Secure cloud services with network controls
 ■ Identity Management
 ■ IM-1 Use a centralized identity and authentication system
 ■ IM-3 Manage application identities securely and automatically
 ■ IM-7 Restrict resource access based on conditions
 ■ IM-8 Restrict the exposure of credentials and secrets
 ■ Privileged Access
 ■ PA-1 Separate and limit highly privileged/administrative users
 ■ PA-7 Follow just enough administration (least privilege) principle
 ■ PA-8 Determine access process for cloud provider support
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.2: Security for private access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 139
 ■ Data Protection
 ■ DP-1 Discover, classify, and label sensitive data
 ■ DP-2 Monitor anomalies and threats targeting sensitive data
 ■ DP-3 Encrypt sensitive data in transit
 ■ DP-4 Enable data at rest encryption by default
 ■ DP-5 Use the customer-managed key option in data at rest encryption when 
required
 ■ DP-6 Use a secure key management process
 ■ Asset Management
 ■ AM-2 Use only approved services
 ■ Logging and Threat Detection
 ■ LT-1 Enable threat detection capabilities
 ■ LT-3 Enable logging for security investigation
 ■ LT-4 Enable network logging for security investigation
 ■ Backup and Recovery
 ■ BR-1 Ensure regular automated backups
While this is an extensive list, for the purpose of this section of the AZ-500 exam, you should 
focus only on Network Security and the monitoring capabilities for Azure SQL that are surfaced 
in Defender for Cloud in the form of recommendations to increase the security posture of 
Azure SQL workloads.

tices to harden your databases and decrease the likelihood of compromise. Recommendations 
such as disabling public network access on Azure SQL managed instances, enabling private 
endpoint connections to fortify secure communication with Azure SQL Database, and turning 
off the public network access property on Azure SQL Database are security measurements that 
are easy to implement and have a very positive effect on the overall security of your workloads.

Plan and implement Transport Layer Security (TLS) 
to applications, including Azure App Service and API 
Management
To ensure that you always protect the data in transit, you should configure your App Service to 
use an SSL/TLS certificate. To create a TLS bind of your certificate to your app or enable client 
certificates for your App Service app, your
 App Service plan  must be configured to the Basic, 
Standard, Premium, or Isolated tiers.
The App Service enables different scenarios to handle certificates, which includes the  
capability to 
 ■ Buy a certificate.
 ■ Import an existing certificate from the App Service.
 ■ Upload an existing certificate that you might have already.
 ■ Import a certificate from Key Vault (from any subscription on the same tenant).
 ■ Create a free App Service custom certificate. (This option does not provide support for 
naked domains.)
When you open an App Service, you can add a managed certificate, bring your own certifi -
cate (using a PFX file), or use a public key certificate (using a CER file), as shown in Figure 2-31.
FIGURE 2-31 Options to configure a private key certificate for App Service
For the AZ-500 exam, the scenario description is what leads you to choose one option over 
the other. For example, let’s say that a Contoso administrator needs to secure data in transit for 
their App Service, but the administrator needs to save costs, leverage the existing Public Key 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks142
Infrastructure (PKI) on-premises, and support naked domains. In this case, uploading an exist-
ing certificate (PFX) is the most appropriate option. This will save costs because it will leverage 
the existing PKI (which already met the second requirement), and it supports naked domains. 
When uploading an existing certificate, ensure you have the password for the protected PFX 
file; the private key must be at least 2048 bits long and contain all intermediate certificates in 
the certificate chain.
Another important scenario is when you need to respond to requests to a specific hostname 
over HTTPS. In this case, you need to secure a custom domain in a TLS binding. The certificate 
that will be used to bind TLS/SSL needs to contain an 
ExtendedKeyUsage for server authentica -
tion object identifier (OID), which is 1.3.6.1.5.5.7.3.1, and a trusted certificate authority must 
sign it. Also, notice that on this page, you can configure your App Service to only answer to 
HTTPS and configure the TLS version that will be used.
 
Azure API Management security considerations
APIs are everywhere. APIs likely facilitate those digital interactions, whether you’ re reserving 
travel arrangements online, checking the weather through a search engine, or purchasing gro-
ceries via a mobile app. Serving as the core of communication among services, users, and cloud 
data, APIs are becoming increasingly vital as we transition from monolithic to microservices-
based application structures.
Current API security measures fall short. Factors like mobile accessibility, microservice 
design patterns, and hybrid on-premises/cloud utilization add layers of complexity to API secu -
rity, as there’s rarely a single “gateway” point for enforcing security. Your existing toolset, which 
might encompass application gateways, Web Application Firewalls (WAFs), and API gateways, 
offers essential traffic control and rule-based detections. However, they lack API behavioral 
understanding, creating a significant gap in API security. Threats stemming from business logic 
abuse, such as insufficient authorization and authentication of APIs and APIs exposing exces -
sive data, evade these traditional perimeter-based defense mechanisms.
When planning your Azure API Management security, you must consider some security 
principles, including how to secure your frontend APIs. Consider using OAuth 2.0, OpenID 
Connect, and mutual TLS, and consider how you want to protect your backend services 
behind API Management. Azure API Management supports client certificates and OAuth 2.0. 
You should also consider which client and backend protocols/ciphers are required for your 
implementation. 
Make sure to consider API Management validation policies for verifying REST or SOAP API 
requests and responses according to schemas outlined in the API definition or uploaded onto 
TIP CERTIFICATES
For the detailed steps to configure the different types of certificates, see https://  
aka.ms/az500AppCertificates.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Security for public access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 143
the instance. While these policies do not substitute a Web Application Firewall, they can offer 
supplementary defense against certain threats.
When designing your API Management security, make sure to consider the following 
guidelines:
 ■ Deploy a Web Application Firewall (WAF) before API Management to safeguard against 
prevalent web application exploits and vulnerabilities.
 ■ Adopt the use of Azure Key Vault to securely store and manage secrets that will be 
accessible via API Management.
 ■ Use managed identity in API Management to establish trust relationships between the 
service and other resources and leverage Microsoft Entra ID to protect your managed 
identity.
 ■ Ensure that APIs are only accessible over HTTPS to protect data in transit and ensure its 
integrity.
 ■ Use Defender for APIs to improve the security posture of your APIs and to detect  
potential attacks against your APIs.
 ■ Use the latest TLS version when encrypting information in transit.
Plan, implement, and manage an Azure Firewall, including 
Azure Firewall Manager and firewall policies
While NSG provides stateful package flow and custom security rules, you will need a more 
robust solution when you need to protect an entire virtual network. If your company needs 
a fully stateful, centralized network firewall as a service (FWaaS) that provides network and 
application-level protection across different subscriptions and virtual networks, you should 
choose Azure Firewall.
Also, Azure Firewall can be used in scenarios where you need to span multiple availability 
zones for increased availability. Although there’s no additional cost for an Azure Firewall  
deployed in an availability zone, there are additional costs for inbound and outbound 
data transfers associated with Availability Zones. Figure 2-32 shows an Azure Firewall in its 
own VNet and subnet, allowing some traffic and blocking other traffic based on a series of 
evaluations.
As shown in Figure 2-32, the Azure Firewall will perform a series of evaluations before 
allowing or blocking the traffic. Just as with an NSG, the rules in Azure Firewall are processed 
according to the rule type in priority order (lower numbers to higher numbers). A rule col-
lection name may contain only letters, numbers, underscores, periods, or hyphens. You can 
configure NAT, network, and application rules on Azure Firewall. Remember that Azure Firewall 
uses a static public IP address for your virtual network resources, and you need that before 
deploying your firewall. Azure Firewall also supports learning routes via Border Gateway  
Protocol (BGP).
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

FIGURE 2-32 Azure Firewall topology
Azure Firewall will query the network and application rules to evaluate outbound traffic. 
Just as with an NSG, no other rules are processed when a match is found in a network rule. 
Azure Firewall will use the infrastructure rule collection if there is no match. This collection is 
created automatically by Azure Firewall and includes platform-specific fully qualified domain 
names (FQDN). If there is still no match, Azure Firewall denies outgoing traffic.
Azure Firewall uses rules based on Destination Network Address Translation (DNAT) for 
incoming traffic evaluation. These rules are also evaluated in priority and before network rules. 
An implicit corresponding network rule is added to allow the translated traffic if a match is 
found. Although this is the default behavior, you can override this by explicitly adding a  
network rule collection with deny rules that match the translated traffic (if needed).
 
In Figure 2-32, you also saw that Azure Firewall leverages Microsoft Threat Intelligence dur-
ing the traffic evaluation. The Microsoft Threat Intelligence is powered by Intelligent Security 
Graph and is used by many other services in Azure, including Defender for Cloud.
Azure Firewall is available in three tiers: Basic, Standard, and Premium. The Basic tier 
includes the following capabilities:
 ■ Built-in high availability
 ■ Availability Zones
IMPORTANT WEB APPLICATION FIREWALL (WAF)
Application rules aren’t applied for inbound connections. Microsoft recommends using Web 
Application Firewall (WAF) to filter inbound HTTP/S traffic.

 ■ Application FQDN filtering rules
 ■ Network traffic filtering rules
 ■ FQDN tags
 ■ Service tags
 ■ Threat intelligence in alert mode
 ■ Outbound SNAT support
 ■ Inbound DNAT support
 ■ Multiple public IP addresses
 ■ Azure Monitor logging
 ■ Certifications
The Basic tier is recommended for small-medium businesses (SMB) where the necessary 
throughput is around 250 Mbps. For larger organizations that need autoscaling to handle peak 
traffic up to 30 Gbps, the Standard tier is more appropriate. The Standard tier includes the  
following capabilities:
 ■ Built-in high availability

 ■ Outbound SNAT support
 ■ Inbound DNAT support
 ■ Multiple public IP addresses

 ■ Forced tunneling
 ■ Web categories
 ■ Certifications
While these features are enough for many organizations, there will be scenarios where 
the environment is highly sensitive and regulated, which requires advanced threat protection 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks146
capabilities like malware and TLS inspection. These features are part of the Azure Firewall  
Premium tier, which includes:
 ■ TLS inspection  You can decrypt outbound traffic, analyze the data, and encrypt it 
again before sending it to the destination.
 ■ Intrusion detection and prevention system (IDPS)  This is a network-based IDPS 
that enables you to monitor network traffic for malicious activity. In addition, IDPS 
enables you to log information about these activities, report it, and optionally create a 
mechanism to attempt to block it.
 ■ URL filtering This capability enhances the Azure Firewall’s FQDN filtering feature to con-
sider an entire URL. For example, www.fabrikam.com/a/b instead of www.fabrikam.com.
 ■ Web categories Allows you to control user access to websites by categories such as 
gambling websites, social media websites, and others.

your Azure Firewall.

8. In the Region dropdown, select the region where the Azure Firewall will reside.
9. In the Availability Zone  dropdown, select the availability zone where the firewall will 
reside.
10. In the Firewall SKU section, you can select the plan according to your business needs.

sic Firewall rules. Remember that if you use a Firewall Policy, you will need to select an 
existing one or create a new one.

Firewall.

categorize resources and view consolidated billing by applying the same tag to multiple 
resources and resource groups. Once you finish, click Next: Review + Create button.
17. In the last tab, review the selection and click the Create  button. The creation of the 
Azure Firewall will take several minutes. After the deployment is complete, you can click 
the Go To Resource button.
You can also deploy a new Azure Firewall using the 
New-AzFirewall cmdlet, as shown in the 
following example:
New-AzFirewall -Name "azFw" -ResourceGroupName MyRG -Location centralus -VirtualNetwork 
MyVNet -PublicIpAddress MyPubIP
Azure Firewall Manager
Azure Firewall Manager can be used when the organization needs a security management 
solution that enables centralized security policy and route management. Azure Firewall  
Manager can provide this type of benefit for two types of Azure network architecture:
 ■ Secured virtual hub  This type of network is utilized when the organization uses an 
Azure Virtual WAN Hub to create hub-and-spoke architectures. When security and 
routing policies are associated with such a hub, it is called a secured virtual hub.
 ■ Hub virtual network  This type of network is utilized when the organization uses an 
Azure virtual network that they create and manage independently. When security  
policies are associated with such a hub, it is called a hub virtual network. 
When designing your Azure network’s architecture, consider the scenario’s technical 
requirements. If these requirements include one or more of the items shown below, then you 
should use Azure Firewall Manager: 
 ■ Centralized deployment and configuration of multiple Azure Firewall instances that 
span through different Azure regions and subscriptions
 ■ Centralized management of Azure Firewall policies across multiple secured virtual hubs
 ■ Ability to integrate with third-party Security-as-a-Service (SECaaS) providers to obtain 
additional network protection for VNet and branch Internet connections
 ■ Ability to route traffic to a secured hub for filtering and logging purposes without 
manually setting up User Defined Routes (UDR) on spoke virtual networks.
 
TIP AZURE FIREWALL MANAGER PRICING
Azure Firewall Manager has different components, and some of these components have their 
own pricing. Make sure to review the Azure Firewall Manager pricing page for the latest  
information regarding pricing at http://aka.ms/az500fwmanprice . 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Security for public access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 149
One of the main components of Azure Firewall Manager is the Firewall Policy. This policy 
contains NAT, network and application rule collections, and Threat Intelligence settings. A 
Firewall Policy is a global resource that can be used across multiple Azure Firewall instances, 
regions, and subscriptions. You can create a policy using Azure portal, REST API, templates, 
Azure PowerShell, and CLI. To create policies, you can also migrate existing rules from Azure 
Firewall using the portal or Azure PowerShell. 
You can create new policies, or you can create a policy inherited from other existing policies. 
Policies created with non-empty parent policies inherit all rule collections from the parent 
policy. It is important to mention that when you inherit a policy, any changes to the parent 
policy will be automatically applied down to associated firewall child policies.
When taking the AZ-500 exam, make sure to carefully read the scenario description and the 
organization’s requirements. Depending on the organization’s requirements, you will either 
create an Azure Firewall Manager for a virtual hub or a hub virtual network. 
If you need to secure your cloud network traffic destined to private IP addresses, Azure 
PaaS, and the Internet, then you should deploy Azure Firewall Manager to a virtual hub. You 
can create a hub virtual network if you need to connect your on-premises network to an Azure 
virtual network to create a hybrid one. By deploying Azure Firewall Manager to this hub virtual 
network, you are securing your hybrid network traffic destined to private IP addresses, Azure 
PaaS, and the Internet. 
The main use case scenario for Azure Firewall Manager is the centralized management of 
policies across multiple secured virtual hubs. Azure Firewall Manager supports classic rules 
and policies, though when designing your deployment, we recommend using policies. Azure 
Firewall Manager also supports Standard and Premium policies. If your deployment needs any 
of the following components, you should choose the Standard policy:
 ■ NAT rules, Network rules, Application rules
 ■ Custom DNS, DNS proxy
 ■ IP Groups
 ■ Web Categories
 ■ Threat Intelligence
More advanced deployments may require capabilities that will only be available in the  
Premium policies: TLS Inspection, Web Categories, URL Filtering, and IDPS.
Another scenario that Azure Firewall Manager supports is leveraging third-party security as 
a service (SECaaS) offerings to protect Internet access for your users. By using this integration, 
you can secure a hub with a supported security partner. Also, you can route and filter Internet 
traffic from your Virtual Networks (VNets) or branch locations within a region. The supported 
security partners are Zscaler, Check Point, and iboss. 
 
TIP SECURITY PROVIDERS
To see an example of deploying a supported third-party security provider to a new or existing 
hub, visit http://aka.ms/az500FWMSECaaS .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks150
The general deployment steps will also vary according to the deployment selection. If 
you decided to deploy Azure Firewall Manager for hub virtual networks, the overall steps are 
shown below:
1. Create a Firewall Policy.
2. Create a hub-and-spoke architecture.
3. Select the supported provider; in this case, only Azure Firewall is supported.
4. Configure the appropriate routes.
 
Firewall Policy
Azure Firewall Policy is a centralized management tool for governing network traffic in Azure, 
providing a scalable way to enforce and log security rules across multiple Azure Firewall 
instances. Figure 2-34 shows the Azure Firewall Policy lifecyle.
Creation and
configuration
Associating with 
Azure
Firewall instances
Rule evaluation
Centralized
management
Logging and
monitoring Rule actions
FIGURE 2-34 Azure Firewall Policy lifecyle 
 ■ Creation and configuration  You create a Firewall Policy within Azure, defining a set 
of rules and settings that dictate how network traffic is handled. These rules can include 
allowing or denying traffic based on source IP address, destination IP address, port, 
protocol, and other attributes.
 ■ Associating with Azure Firewall instances  Once the policy is configured, you can 
associate it with one or more Azure Firewall instances. This association ensures that 
those firewall instances enforce the rules defined in the policy.
 ■ Rule evaluation  When network traffic passes through an Azure Firewall instance 
associated with a Firewall Policy, the firewall evaluates the traffic against the rules 
defined in the policy. It checks each rule in the policy sequentially until it finds a match.
 ■ Centralized management  One of the key benefits of Azure Firewall Policy is its  
centralized management approach. Instead of configuring rules individually on each 
TIP AZURE FIREWALL MANAGER DEPLOYMENT
To secure a virtual hub using Azure Firewall Manager, follow the steps at http://aka.ms/
az500fwmvhub. To secure a hub virtual network using Azure Firewall Manager, follow the 
steps at http://aka.ms/az500fwhVNet .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Security for public access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 151
Azure Firewall instance, you define them once in a policy and then apply that policy 
across multiple instances. This simplifies management and ensures consistency across 
your Azure network security posture.
 ■ Logging and monitoring  Azure Firewall Policy provides extensive logging capabili-
ties, allowing you to monitor and analyze network traffic patterns, security incidents, 
and compliance with organizational policies. For further analysis, logs can be exported 
to Azure Monitor, Log Analytics, or third-party SIEM (Security Information and Event 
Management) systems.
 ■ Rule actions Depending on the outcome of the rule evaluation, the firewall takes 
appropriate actions specified in the matching rule. These actions can include allow -
ing the traffic to pass, denying it, or logging information about the traffic for auditing 
purposes.
Azure Firewall Policy provides a centralized, scalable, and flexible approach to defining, 
enforcing, and monitoring security rules across multiple Azure Firewall instances. 
Azure Firewall has NAT rules, network rules, and application rules. The rules are processed 
according to the rule type. First, network rules take precedence over application rules, and 
both sets of rules are final. If a network rule matches, application rules are skipped. In the 
absence of a matching network rule and with the packet protocol being HTTP/HTTPS, the 
packet undergoes evaluation by the application rules. The packet proceeds to assessment 
against the infrastructure rule collection should no match be found. If no match is found even 
there, the packet is denied by default.

Plan and implement an Azure Application Gateway
Azure Application Gateway functions as a web traffic load balancer operating at OSI layer 7, 
specifically designed to manage web application traffic. Azure Application Gateway provides 
more advanced capabilities tailored to web application traffic (layer 7), and also includes layer 
4 protection which became available in March 2024.
Application Gateway can make routing decisions based on various attributes of an HTTP 
request, such as the URI path or host headers. For instance, it can direct traffic according to 
the incoming URL. For example, if the URL contains “/images”, the traffic can be directed to a 
designated set of servers, known as a pool, configured specifically for handling images.
For the AZ-500 exam, it is important to understand the use case scenarios where Azure 
Application Gateway is more suitable. Table 2-5 has examples of use case scenarios that lead to 
the use of Azure Application Gateway:

Plan and implement an Azure Front Door, including a 
Content Delivery Network (CDN)
Consider an Azure deployment across different regions that needs to provide a high- 
performance application experience and is resilient to failures. For this type of scenario, Azure 
Front Door is the best solution.
Azure Front Door is Microsoft’s modern cloud Content Delivery Network (CDN), offering 
swift, dependable, and secure access for users to your applications’ dynamic and static web 
content worldwide. Leveraging Microsoft’s extensive global edge network, Azure Front Door 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Security for public access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 161
operates through numerous global and local points of presence (PoPs) strategically dispersed 
globally, ensuring proximity to enterprise and consumer end-users.
Azure Front Door works at layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS) and uses the anycast protocol with split 
TCP and Microsoft’s global network to improve global connectivity. By using a split TCP-based 
anycast protocol, Front Door ensures that your users promptly connect to the nearest Front 
Door POP (point of presence).
Azure Front Door and Azure CDN are two Azure services designed to provide global con-
tent delivery with intelligent routing and caching functionalities at the application layer. They 
both facilitate the optimization and acceleration of your applications by leveraging a globally 
distributed network of points of presence (POP) close to your users. Additionally, both services 
offer a range of features aimed at enhancing application security against malicious attacks and 
aiding in monitoring application health and performance.
 
You can configure Front Door to route your client requests to the fastest and most  
available application backend—any Internet-facing service hosted inside or outside of Azure. 
Some other capabilities included in Front Door are listed here:
 ■ Intelligent Health Probe  Front Door monitors your backends for availability and 
latency. According to its results, it will instant failover when a backend goes down.
 ■ URL-Based Routing  Allows you to route traffic to the backend based on the URL’s 
path of the request. For example, traffic to www.fabrikam.com/hr/* is routed to a specific 
pool, whereas www.fabrikam.com/soc/* goes to another.
 ■ Multiple-Site Hosting  Enables you to configure a more efficient topology for your 
deployments by adding different websites to a single Front Door and redirecting to  
different pools.
 ■ Geo-Filtering  You can use this option to restrict access to your web application by 
country or region.
 ■ TLS Termination Support for TLS termination at the edge.
 ■ Custom Domain, SSL Offloading, and Certificate Management  You can let Front 
Door manage your certificate or upload your own TLS/SSL certificate.
 ■ Application Layer Security  Allows you to author your own custom web application 
firewall (WAF) rules for access control, and it comes with Azure DDoS Basic enabled. 
Front Door is also a layer 7 reverse proxy, which only allows web traffic to pass through 
to backends and blocks other types of traffic by default.
IMPORTANT FRONT DOOR AND CDN
Different scenarios may lead you to select between Front Door and CDN. Visit this page  
to better understand the feature set and comparison between these solutions: https://  
aka.ms/AZ500FDCDN .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks162
 ■ URL Redirection  Allows you to configure different types of redirection, which include 
HTTP to HTTPS redirection, redirection to different hostnames, redirection to different 
paths, or redirections to a new query string in the URL.
 ■ URL Rewrite Allows you to configure a custom forwarding path to construct a request 
to forward traffic to the backend.

Plan and implement a Web Application Firewall (WAF)
The Web Application Firewall (WAF) offers centralized protection for your web applications 
against prevalent exploits and vulnerabilities. The need for robust protection grows increas-
ingly critical as web applications become more susceptible to malicious attacks exploiting 
widely known vulnerabilities, such as SQL injection and cross-site scripting.
Web Application Firewall (WAF) can be used on Front Door. Azure also allows you to deploy 
WAF in other ways, so it is important to understand the design requirements before deciding 
which WAF deployment you should use.
Review the flowchart available at http://aka.ms/wafdecisionflow to better understand WAF’s 
features, which include Azure Load Balancer, Application Gateway, and Azure Front Door. If 
your scenario has the following characteristics, WAF with Front Door is a good choice:
 ■ Your app uses HTTP/HTTPS.
 ■ Your app is Internet-facing.
 ■ Your app is globally distributed across different regions.
 ■ Your app is hosted in PaaS (such as an Azure App Service).
Consider deploying WAF on Front Door for a global and centralized solution. When using 
WAF with Front Door, the web applications will be inspected for every incoming request deliv-
ered by Front Door at the network edge.
 
Create and configure Web Application Firewall (WAF)
In a scenario where you need to protect your web applications from common threats, such as 
SQL injection, cross-site scripting, and other web-based exploits, using Azure Web Applica-
tion Firewall (WAF) on Azure Application Gateway is the most appropriate way to address 
these needs. WAF on Application Gateway is based on Open Web Application Security Project 
(OWASP) core rule set 3.1, 3.0, or 2.2.9. These rules will protect your web apps against the top 10 
OWASP vulnerabilities, which you can find at https://owasp.org/www-project-top-ten.
You can use WAF on Application Gateway to protect multiple web applications. A single 
instance of Application Gateway can host up to 40 websites, and a WAF will protect those 
websites. Even though you have multiple websites behind the WAF, you can still create custom 
policies to address the needs of those sites. The diagram shown in Figure 2-47 has more details 
about the different components of this solution.
IMPORTANT WAF INTEGRATION WITH FRONT DOOR
If your deployment requires TLS offloading and package inspection, WAF natively integrates 
with Front Door, which allows you to inspect a request after it’s decrypted.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 2  Secure Azure Networks166

Logs

Web Server 1

Azure Monitor
WAF on

FIGURE 2-47 Different integration components for WAF on Application Gateway
In the example shown in Figure 2-47, a WAF Policy has been configured for the back-end 
site. This policy is where you define all rules, custom rules, exclusions, and other customiza -
tions, such as a file upload limit.
WAF on Application Gateway supports Transport Layer Security (TLS) termination, cookie-
based session affinity, round-robin load distribution, and content-based routing. Azure Moni-
tor receives all logs related to potential attacks against your web applications. WAV v1 alerts 
will also be streamed to Defender for Cloud and appear in the Security Alert dashboard.
Depending on the scenario requirement, you can configure WAF on the Application  
Gateway to operate in two different modes:
 ■ Detection Mode  This mode will not interfere with traffic when suspicious activity 
occurs. Rather than blocking suspicious activity, this mode only detects and logs all 
threat alerts. Diagnostic logging and the WAF log must be enabled for this mode to 
work properly.
 ■ Prevention Mode  As the name implies, this mode blocks traffic that matches the 
rules. Requests that get blocked by this setting will generate a 403 Unauthorized Access 
message. The connection is closed at that point, and a record is created in the WAF logs.
When reviewing the WAF log for a request that was blocked, you will see a message that 
contains some fields that are similar to this example:
Mandatory rule. Cannot be disabled. Inbound Anomaly Score Exceeded (Total Inbound Score: 
5 - SQLI=0,XSS=0,RFI=0,LFI=0,RCE=0,PHPI=0,HTTP=0,SESS=0): Missing User Agent Header; 
individual paranoia level scores: 3, 2, 0, 0
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Security for public access to Azure resources   CHAPTER 2 167
The anomaly score comes from the OWASP 3.x rules, which have a specific severity: Criti -
cal (5), Error (4), Warning (3), or Notice (2). The previous message indicates that the total 
inbound score is 5, which translates to a severity equal to Critical . It is important to emphasize 
that the traffic will not be blocked until it reaches the threshold of 5. This means that if traffic 
matches the block rule but has an anomaly score of 3, it will not be blocked, though the mes-
sage that you will see in the WAF log says that it is blocked.
 
Recommend when to use Azure DDoS Protection Standard
Azure offers built-in infrastructure-level DDoS protection to safeguard against DDoS attacks 
directed at the Azure platform and Microsoft online services. This protection extends to all 
resources within Azure, including customer applications. Its primary goal is to ensure that there 
is no disruption to other customers within the same data center and region in the event of an 
attack on a customer application. 
However, it’s important to note that infra-level protection focuses on preserving the integ-
rity of the Azure platform, so its detection policies and thresholds are intentionally set high. 
Consequently, application-targeted attacks often have lower throughput and may circumvent 
this protection. As a result, even medium-sized attacks could potentially impact customer 
applications. Figure 2-48 visually represents the difference between the built-in Azure DDoS 
Infrastructure protection and the Azure DDoS protection plan to protect your applications.
DDos infrastructure-level protection
protects Azure platform
Azure DDos protection
protects your applicationsProtected resource
FIGURE 2-48  Multiple layers of protection
Azure DDoS Protection and optimal application design strategies offer advanced DDoS 
mitigation capabilities to safeguard against DDoS attacks. It is inherently configured to fortify 
your Azure resources within a virtual network. Enabling protection is straightforward for  
both new and existing virtual networks, requiring no alterations to applications or resources.

Thought experiment
In this thought experiment, demonstrate your skills and knowledge of the topics covered in this 
chapter. You can find answers to this thought experiment in the next section.
Advanced security for compute at Tailwind Traders
You are one of the Azure administrators for Tailwind Traders, a medium-sized online store 
specializing in various home products. Tailwind Traders is enabling their employees to work 
remotely and need to implement a VPN that fits their business needs. The VPN solution needs 
to support a variety of operating systems, including Android, iOS (versions 11.0 and above), 
Windows, Linux, and Mac devices (OSX versions 10.13 and above). Tailwind Traders also needs 
to enable secure communication over the Internet with a new branch office they are opening in 
Dallas. Tailwind Traders has moved most of their workloads to the cloud and is now concerned 
about potential attacks that can disrupt their business, mainly around the availability of their 
main website that sells their products. However, Tailwind needs to adopt a more cost-effective 
solution since they don’t have enough budget this fiscal year to spend on security enhance -
ments. With this information in mind, answer the following questions:
1.
 W
hat solution would you recommend to protect Tailwind’s website against  
an availability type of attack? 
2.
 W
hat VPN protocol would you recommend to be implemented to enable remote users 
to connect to Tailwind enterprise resources?
3.
 W
hat technology would you recommend enabling Tailwind to connect with the new 
branch office in Dallas?
Thought experiment answers
This section contains the solution to the thought experiment. Each answer explains why the 
answer choice is correct.
1. Azure DDoS IP Protection. By definition, Tailwind is a medium-business company that 
doesn’t have enough budget to afford more enterprise-grade DDoS protection.
2.
 OpenVPN Protocol is the option that supports the variety of clients described in this 
scenario.
3.
 A site-to-site (S2S) VPN is this scenario’s most appropriate option.

Chapter summary   CHAPTER 2 171
Chapter summary
 ■ Different types of Azure VPNs will be selected according to the organization’s require-
ments, including site-to-site VPN, point-to-site VPN, VNet-to-VNet, and multi-site VPN.
 ■ Consider using ExpressRoute if your connectivity scenario requires higher reliability, 
faster speeds, consistent latencies, and higher security than typical Internet connections.
 ■ Network security group (NSG) in Azure allows you to filter network traffic by creating 
rules.
 ■ Azure App Service is an HTTP-based service for hosting web applications, REST APIs, 
and mobile backends. Azure App Service Environment (ASE) is an Azure App Service 
feature that provides an isolated and dedicated environment for securely running App 
Service apps in the cloud.
 ■ To ensure that you are always protecting the data in transit, you should configure your 
App Service to use an SSL/TLS certificate. To create a TLS bind of your certificate to your 
app or enable client certificates for your App Service app, your App Service plan must 
be configured to the Basic, Standard, Premium, or Isolated tiers.
 ■ Consider using Azure Firewall when your organization requires a fully stateful firewall 
and centralized management with network- and application-level protection.
 ■ Azure Application Gateway functions as a web traffic load balancer operating at OSI  
layers 4 and 7, specifically designed to manage web application traffic.
 ■ Consider using Azure Front Door when your organization’s requirements include  
Azure deployment across different regions with a high-performance experience for 
applications and that is resilient to failures.
 ■ Use a resource-level firewall when you need resource-level filtering to enhance your 
workload’s security.
 ■ Enable Azure DDoS Standard when you need to tune application traffic volume, and you 
want to ensure an SLA level that provides application guarantee and cost protection.

CHAPTER 3
Secure Azure Compute
Many organizations host their primary cloud workloads in services that fall into the category 
of Azure Compute. Azure Compute includes not only Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) virtual 
machines but different container and container orchestration technologies and published 
APIs. In this chapter, you’ll learn how to secure virtual machine workloads, container  
workloads, container orchestration workloads, and APIs you publish to Azure.
Skills covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 3.1: Secure Virtual Machine Workloads
 ■ Skill 3.2: Secure Kubernetes and container workloads
 ■ Skill 3.3: Secure APIs published in Azure
Skill 3.1: Secure Virtual Machine Workloads
While Platform as a Service (PaaS) gets a lot of attention when people consider what they can 
do with Azure, most organizational workloads running in the cloud are hosted within humble 
Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) virtual machines. As a security professional responsible for 
protecting cloud workloads, you must know how to protect virtual machines in the Azure 
management plane and ensure that the virtual machine’s internal contents are secure. 
Microsoft Defender for Servers
Microsoft Defender for Cloud is a product suite that allows you to protect your on-premises 
and cloud workloads. From the perspective of someone having to protect IaaS virtual 
machines running in Azure, the most useful element of Defender for Cloud is Microsoft 
Defender for Servers. Defender for Servers is an umbrella product that includes the following 
tools:
 ■ Azure security baselines
 ■ Threat and vulnerability management
 ■ Just-in-time virtual machine access
 ■ File integrity monitoring
 ■ Adaptive application controls
 ■ Adaptive network hardening
173
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 3  Secure Azure Compute174
 
IaaS VM OS hardening baselines
Azure security baselines allow you to assess the configuration of a managed Azure IaaS virtual 
machine instance against a best practices security baseline. Assessing the IaaS VM operating 
system instance requires the Guest Configuration extension and that the IaaS VM has a system-
assigned managed identity.
Once the IaaS VM is assessed, you will be provided with a set of recommendations that 
describe the identified security issue, the potential impact of that security issue, how the vul -
nerability might be exploited, and steps you can take to remediate that vulnerability. In some 
cases, the vulnerability can be resolved from the Azure portal. 
 
Threat and vulnerability management
Threat and vulnerability management is a Microsoft Defender for Endpoint module that allows 
you to discover and prioritize vulnerabilities and misconfigurations. You can use the thread 
and vulnerability management module for Microsoft Defender for Endpoint for both Windows 
Server IaaS VMs and Azure Arc–enabled on-premises machines.
Vulnerability management includes assessments beyond those detected by assessing 
a server against an Azure security baseline. For example, the vulnerability assessment may 
determine where the software needs to be updated or the configuration modified and links 
to appropriate CVE documentation. As with Azure security baseline assessment, you will be 
provided with a set of remediation steps for each detected vulnerability.
 
Just-in-time virtual machine access
Just-in-time (JIT) VM access allows you to open a specific management port to the Internet 
for a limited duration of time to a small range of IP addresses. You only need to use JIT if you 
require management port access to an Azure IaaS VM from a host on the Internet. JIT requires 
Microsoft Defender for Servers Plan 2 to be enabled on the Azure subscription that hosts the 
IaaS VM.

To enable JIT in an IaaS VM, perform the following steps:
1. In the Azure portal, type Defender for Cloud  in the Search bar and select Microsoft 
Defender For Cloud  in the list of services.
2. In the Defender For Cloud  menu, select Workload Protections  under Cloud 
Security. 
3. In the Advanced Protection  section of the Workload Protections  page, shown in 
Figure 3-1, select Just-In-Time VM Access .
FIGURE 3-1 Just-In-Time VM Access in Workload Protections
4. On the Not Configured  tab, select the IaaS VMs that you want to protect with JIT and 
choose Enable JIT on VMs. Defender for Cloud will provide a list of ports recommended 
for protection. Enable the ports appropriate for the IaaS VM’s operating system. Ports 
that you can protect include:
 ■ 22 — SSH
 ■ 3389 — RDP
 ■ 5985 — WinRM
 ■ 5986 — WinRM
You can also choose to add specific ports for JIT access. When you do this, you need to 
specify the following:
 ■ Port Specifies which port will be opened when the request is approved.
 ■ Protocol The protocol allowed on the port when the request is approved.
 ■ Allowed Source IPs  These are the IP address ranges allowed to access the specified 
port on the specified protocol. These can be a static address, an address range, or the 
address from which the JIT request is issued when connected to the Azure portal.

File integrity monitoring allows you to monitor critical files, including operating system files, 
the Windows Registry, Linux system files, and application software, for modifications that

Adaptive application controls
Most servers run a consistent set of processes. Because this list of processes is so consistent, 
any deviation from this list of commonly run software can be evidence of a compromise or 
breach. Adaptive application controls use machine learning to determine which applications 
and processes regularly run on a Windows Server instance and create a behavior-based allow 
list to allow those applications to run while blocking any that don’t normally run. 
Adaptive application controls are more sophisticated than Windows Defender Application 
Control technologies because WDAC requires you to manually define which applications can 
run, whereas adaptive application controls will generate that list based on observing a healthy 
system. You can modify a generated allow list to add or remove programs as necessary. Allow 
lists can be applied on a per-system basis or to a collection of systems. Using adaptive appli-
cation controls requires an IaaS virtual machine to be monitored for at least 14 days before a 
profile of running applications will be reliably generated. 
Follow these steps to configure adaptive application controls on a collection of virtual 
machines that consistently run a specific set of applications:
1.
 In the Azure portal, type Defender for Cloud  in the Search bar and select Microsoft 
Defender For Cloud  in the list of services.
2. In the Defender For Cloud menu, select Workload Protections under Cloud Security. 
NEED MORE REVIEW?  FILE INTEGRITY MONITORING
You can learn more about file integrity monitoring at https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/
defender-for-cloud/file-integrity-monitoring-overview .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 3  Secure Azure Compute178
3. On the Workload Protections  page, shown in Figure 3-2, select Adaptive Application 
Control.
FIGURE 3-2 Adaptive Application Control in Workload Protections
4. The Adaptive Application Controls page will display IaaS VMs in one of three groups:
 ■ Configured These are IaaS VMs that are already configured with an application 
allowlist.
 ■ Recommended  These are IaaS VMs with data collected and running a consistent 
set of applications but do not have an allow list configured.
 ■ No Recommendation  These are IaaS VMs that either haven’t generated two 
weeks of data or deployed an existing application control technology.
To create a new rule, select which IaaS VMs the rule will apply to, select which of the 
detected applications you want to allow to run, and then apply the rule. When using adaptive 
application control for the first time, you should configure rules to run in audit mode because 
this will allow you to determine if any workload-critical applications are not presently included 
in the rule. When you are confident that the rule functions as intended, you can apply the rule.

Adaptive network hardening
Adaptive network hardening monitors traffic flowing through existing network security 
groups and recommends how the rules in those groups could be further tightened to improve 
security. Adaptive network hardening uses machine learning to analyze actual traffic patterns, 
known trusted configurations, threat intelligence, and other intrusion indicators to provide 
recommendations on limiting traffic to specific IP addresses and ports. Rather than open a port 
to the public Internet for all IP address ranges, the adaptive network hardening process might 
determine that traffic on that port only occurs from a specific public set of IP addresses.  
A recommended network security group rule would restrict traffic only to that set of IP 
addresses rather than allowing traffic from a broader range of hosts.
Perform the following steps to view information about adaptive network hardening:
1.
 In the Azure portal, type Defender for Cloud  in the Search bar and select Microsoft 
Defender For Cloud  in the list of services.
2. In the Defender For Cloud  menu, select Workload Protections under Cloud 
Security. 
3. On the Workload Protections  page, select Adaptive Network Hardening  under 
Advanced Protection .
4. Virtual machines will be grouped into one of three tabs.
 ■ Unhealthy Resources  These VMs have Adaptive Network Hardening  recom-
mendations based on an assessment performed by the Adaptive Network Hardening 
algorithm.
 ■ Healthy Resources  These VMs have no alerts or recommendations.
 ■ Unscanned Resources  These VMs either haven’t yet generated at least 30 days of 
traffic data or are not protected by Microsoft Defender for Servers.
To remediate servers listed in the Unhealthy Resources  group, review and then apply the 
recommended rules to the VM, as shown in Figure 3-3.
FIGURE 3-3 Adaptive Network Hardening recommendations

Azure Update Manager
Azure Update Manager allows you to automate the deployment of updates to computers run-
ning both the Windows and Linux operating systems. Azure Update Manager is the successor 
to Azure Automation Update Management, which is a successor to another update service in 
Azure. Azure Update Manager exists because Azure Automation Update Management has a 
dependency on the Log Analytics Agent, which was deprecated in mid-2024. Azure Update 
Management automatically detects all virtual machines in a subscription, allowing you to apply 
updates to those virtual machines on a scheduled or ad hoc basis. 
To configure and use Azure Update Management, perform the following steps:
1.
 In the Azure portal, type Update Management  into the Search bar and select Update 
Manager, as shown in Figure 3-4.

On-Demand Assessment And Updates .
4. On the Select Resources And Check For Updates page, shown in Figure 3-6, you can 
see a list of virtual machines in the subscription and select the ones you want to check 
for updates.
5.
 Choose Check For Updates to assess each of the selected IaaS VMs.

Azure Automanage
Azure Automanage is a service that automates the deployment of a number of existing services 
used to secure and monitor Azure IaaS VMs running both the Windows Server and supported 
Linux operating systems. Automanage will onboard and configure the following services:
 ■ Boot diagnostics
 ■ Security Center
 ■ Azure monitoring
 ■ Update Manager
 ■ Azure Backup
 ■ Azure Monitor
 ■ Azure configuration management
 ■ Change tracking and inventory
 ■ Azure Automation accounts
While each of these Azure services can be enabled and configured individually for an IaaS 
VM or Azure Arc—enabled server, the advantage of Automanage is that it simplifies the  
process of deploying and configuring each of them for you.

space is used to store data-enabled Automanage services. You can select an existing 
workspace or create a new workspace associated with the Automanage profile.
 ■ Boot Diagnostics  Allows you to configure boot diagnostics, allowing you to 
troubleshoot IaaS VM boot problems.
 ■ Windows Admin Center  Enabling this option for Windows Server IaaS VMs  
configures the VM so that it can be managed from the Azure portal 
 
Manage disk encryption keys for IaaS virtual machines
Most server operating systems support disk encryption, whether the server runs in a cloud 
environment, as a virtual machine on-premises, or installed on traditional hardware. Azure Disk 
Encryption supports BitLocker disk encryption for Windows Server VMs and DM-Crypt encryp-
tion for Linux VMs, where the appropriate cryptographic keys are stored in an Azure Key Vault 
and accessible only to the VM through the VM’s managed identity. 
Disk encryption for Windows Server IaaS VMs
To support IaaS VM disk encryption, the Windows Server IaaS VM must be able to do the  
following. (By default, IaaS VM meets these requirements unless you remove the default  
network security group rules.)
 ■ The server must be able to connect to the Key Vault endpoint so that the Windows 
Server IaaS VM can store and retrieve encryption keys.
 ■ The server must be able to connect to an Entra ID endpoint at http://  
login.microsoftonline.com  to retrieve a token that allows it to connect to the Key  
Vault that holds the encryption keys. 
 ■ The server must be able to connect to an Azure storage endpoint that hosts the Azure 
extension repository and the Azure storage account that stores the VM’s virtual hard 
disks.
 ■ If the Windows Server IaaS VM is domain joined, do not configure BitLocker-related 
Group Policies other than the Configure User Storage Of BitLocker Recovery Informa -
tion: Allow 256-Bit Recovery Key policy. This policy is usually configured automatically 
during the encryption process, and Azure Disk Encryption will fail if a Group Policy 
conflict in any TPM or BitLocker-related policies exists.
 ■ Azure Disk Encryption supports encrypting both the boot and data volumes.
 ■ Azure Disk Encryption can only encrypt mounted volumes.
NEED MORE REVIEW?  AZURE AUTOMANAGE 
You can learn more about Azure Automanage at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/
automanage/.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 3.1: Secure Virtual Machine Workloads   CHAPTER 3 187
Perform the following steps to encrypt the hard disk drives of a Windows Server IaaS VM:
1. Create an Azure Key Vault to store the encryption key used to encrypt the hard disk of 
the VM. You can do this with the following code (substituting appropriate Key Vault 
names, resource group names, and regions):
az keyvault create --name "KeyVaultName" --resource-group "VMResourceGroup" 
--location AzureRegion --enabled-for-disk-encryption
2. You can create a key yourself and use that or automatically have a key created and 
placed in the specified Azure Key Vault by the encryption process. 
3. To encrypt a VM using Azure CLI, issue the following command, identifying the Azure 
Key Vault you created in step 1:
az vm encryption enable -g VMResourceGroup --name VMName --disk-encryption-
keyvault KeyVaultName
You can rotate encryption keys using the same command you used to enable disk encryp-
tion but specifying a different Key Vault. If you want to add an encryption key, run the  
az vm encryption enable command and pass the --key-encryption-key parameter. You can 
remove the key-encryption key by running the command above again but omitting the  
--key-encryption-key parameter. Windows Server 2022 can use RSA 3072 or RSA 4096-bit 
encryption keys but cannot use an RSA 2048-bit encryption key. Versions of Windows Server 
before Windows Server 2022 should use RSA 2048-bit encryption keys.
Azure Backup includes a mechanism to back up and restore an Azure Disk Encryption—
encrypted VM as long as the Recovery Services Vault is in the same subscription and region as 
the encrypted IaaS VM.
 
Disk encryption for Linux IaaS VMs
Linux IaaS VMs also support Azure Disk Encryption. Instead of using BitLocker, Linux IaaS VMs 
leverage DM-Crypt to encrypt the operation system and data disks. Similar to Windows Server 
VMs, Linux VMs also use Azure Key Vault to control and manage disk encryption keys. Azure 
Disk Encryption is not supported for Basic or A-Series IaaS VMs running a Linux operating 
system. If you only want to encrypt data volumes, the Linux IaaS VM must be configured with 
a minimum of 2 GB of memory. If you want to encrypt the operating system volume and data 
volumes, you’ll need to configure the Linux IaaS VM with a minimum of 8 GB of memory. 
If the root file system requires more than 4GB of storage, the amount of memory is equiva -
lent to the size of the root file system multiplied by two. For example, an 8 GB root file system 
will require 16 GB of RAM allocated to the Linux IaaS VM. Once you have encrypted the OS disk, 
you can reduce the amount of RAM allocated to the Linux IaaS VM because the encryption 
process requires this memory, not the day-to-day functionality of an encrypted OS volume. 
NEED MORE REVIEW?  AZURE DISK ENCRYPTION FOR WINDOWS VMS
You can learn more about Azure Disk Encryption for Windows VMs at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/azure/virtual-machines/windows/disk-encryption-overview .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 3  Secure Azure Compute188
Each data disk you want to encrypt will need to be properly configured in the Linux IaaS 
VM’s /etc/fstab file. Ensure you use the nofail option when you set up entries and select a 
persistent block device name. You can ensure that the /etc/fstab settings are configured prop -
erly using the mount -a command or by restarting the IaaS VM, triggering a remount operation. 
When you do this, issue the lsblk command and verify that the storage is still mounted. Azure 
Disk encryption moves mount information from /etc/fstab into its own separate configuration 
file during storage encryption, meaning that once you encrypt storage, the entry for the stor -
age will no longer be present in /etc/fstab. 
Encrypted disks are not immediately available after reboot because there is a delay while 
the start, unlock, and mounting operations occur. This process may take several minutes, 
depending on how the Linux IaaS VM is configured. 
Linux IaaS VMs that use Azure Disk Encryption have the following networking requirements:
 ■ Must be able to connect to a Microsoft Entra endpoint at login.microsoftonline.com .
 ■ Must be able to connect to the Key Vault endpoint that will store the encryption keys 
used by DM-Crypt for Azure Disk Encryption. This Key Vault must reside in the same 
Azure region and subscription as the Linux IaaS VM. 
 ■ Must be able to connect to the Azure storage endpoint that hosts the Azure extension 
repository.
 ■ Must be able to connect to the Azure storage account that hosts the virtual hard disk 
files.
To create a Key Vault that will support encryption keys for a Linux IaaS VM, create a Key 
Vault in the same region and subscription as the VM with the 
--enabled-for-disk-encryption 
command option. Each Key Vault must have a unique name across Azure, and a single Key 
Vault can hold encryption keys for multiple IaaS VMs and IaaS VMs running different operating 
systems. For example, to create an Azure Key Vault to store the encryption key used to encrypt 
the hard disk of the VM, use the following command from the Cloud Shell, substituting appro-
priate Key Vault names, resource group names, and regions:
az keyvault create --name "KeyVaultName" --resource-group "VMResourceGroup" --location 
AzureRegion --enabled-for-disk-encryption
Once the Key Vault is enabled, you can then encrypt the VM from Cloud Shell using the  
following command:
az vm encryption enable -g VMResourceGroup --name VMName --disk-encryption-keyvault 
KeyVaultName
Not every Linux distribution that can be deployed on Azure in an IaaS VM is supported 
for Azure Disk Encryption. While you should check the supported distributions using the link 
provided in the Need More Review sidebar below, when this book was written, the supported 
distributions included the following:
 ■ Ubuntu 18.04, 20.04, and 22.04.
 ■ CBL-Mariner-2.
 ■ CentOS 6.8, 7.4, 7.5, 7.6, 7.7, 7_8, 7_9, 7-LVM, 8_2, 8_3, 8_4, and 8-LVM.

 ■ Oracle Linux 8.5, 8.5 Gen 2, 8.6, and 8.6 Gen 2.
 ■ RedHat RHEL 6.7, 6.8, 7.4, 7.5, 7.6, 7.7, 7.8, 7_9, 7-LVM, 8.1, 8.2, 8-lvm-gen2, 8-LVM, 8.3,

Skill 3.2: Secure Kubernetes and container workloads
This objective deals with securing container workloads and container orchestration workloads. 
To master this objective, you’ll need to understand how to configure security monitoring for 
Container Apps and container registries and configure security for Azure Kubernetes services.
Configure security monitoring for Azure Container Apps
Azure Container Apps allow you to configure several different methods for monitoring perfor -
mance and security. These include the following:
 ■ Log streaming  You can view system and console logs from a running container as 
they are generated. Suitable for the development and test phase of an Azure Container 
App’s lifecycle.
 ■ Container console  Allows you to connect to the Linux console of a running container 
to debug an application from within the container. Suitable for the development and 
test phase of an Azure Container App’s lifecycle.
 ■ Azure Monitor metrics  Use Azure Monitor to collect and analyze a Container App’s 
compute and network usage. Suitable for the deployment and maintenance phases of 
an Azure Container App’s lifecycle.
 ■ Application logging  Allows you to monitor, analyze, and debug an Azure Container 
App using log data.
 ■ Azure Monitor Log Analytics  Allows you to view and analyze an Azure Container 
App’s system and application logs. Suitable for the production deployment phase of an 
Azure Container App’s lifecycle. Suitable for the deployment and maintenance phases of 
an Azure Container App’s lifecycle.
 ■ Azure Monitor alerts  Allows you to configure alerts related to your Azure Container 
App based on metrics and log data. Suitable for the deployment and maintenance 
phases of an Azure Container App’s lifecycle.

Configure Azure Container App Managed Identity
Managed identities allow you to assign an Entra identity so that an application or service can 
be granted access to another resource within Azure. An Azure Container App can be granted 
one of the following managed identity types:
 ■ System-assigned identity  This identity is linked to the Container App and will be 
removed from Entra when the Container App is deleted. You can only configure a single 
system assigned to an Azure Container App.
 ■ User-assigned identity  This identity exists independently of the Container App and 
can be assigned to a Container App and other Azure resources. You can assign mul-
tiple user-assigned identities to a single Container App. You must manually remove 
user-assigned identities from Entra, which will not be removed if you delete the Azure 
Container App. 
Managed identities are covered in more detail in Chapter 1, “Microsoft Entra Security.”  
Perform the following steps to add a system-assigned identity to an Azure Container App:
1.
 In the Azure portal, navigate to your Container App.
2. In the left-hand menu of the Container Apps  page, select Identity .
3. On the System Assigned tab shown in Figure 3-12, set the Status  selector to On.
FIGURE 3-12 Azure Container App system assigned identity
To assign a user-assigned identity to an Azure Container App, you will first need to have a 
user-assigned identity configured within Entra. You can do this by performing the following 
steps: 
1.
 In the Azure Search  bar, search for Managed Identities  and select Managed  
Identities from the results.

Manage access to Azure Container Registry
Azure Container Registry is an Azure service based on Docker Registry 2.0 that allows you to 
store and manage container images and related artifacts. You can sign into an Azure Container 
Registry instance using Azure CLI or the Docker login commands. Azure Container Regis-
try allows you to transfer container images over HTTPS. To pull or push images to the Azure 
Container Registry, a client must interact over HTTPS with two endpoints: the Registry REST API 
and the storage endpoints. By default, an Azure Container Registry accepts connections over 
the Internet from hosts on any network. 
The premium service tier of Azure Container Registry allows for image tag signing as  
well as firewalls and virtual networks that allow you to limit which clients can access the service. 
You can also integrate Microsoft Defender for Cloud with Azure Container Registry to ensure 
that container images are regularly scanned for malware when an image is pushed to the 
Registry.
