Azure Container Registry roles and permissions
To manage access to your Azure Container Registry, you must add a user to a specific role that 
will allow the user to perform container-related tasks. Table 3-1 provides the mapping of the 
roles for the allowed tasks that can be executed in the Azure Container Registry instance:
TABLE 3-1 Azure Container Registry RBAC roles
Role Tasks that can be executed
Owner Access resource manager, create and delete the Registry, push images, pull images, 
delete image data, and change policies
Contributor Access resource manager, create and delete the Registry, push images, pull images, 
delete image data, and change policies
Reader Access resource manager and pull images
ArcPush Push and pull images
ArcPull Pull image
ArcDelete Delete image data
ArcImageSigner Sign images
For CI/CD automation scenarios, you need Docker push capabilities. For this type of sce-
nario, Microsoft recommends that you assign the AcrPush role. This recommendation comes 
from applying the principle of least privilege because this role, unlike the broader Contributor 
role, prevents the user from performing other Registry operations or accessing Azure Resource 
Manager. Using the same rationale, nodes running containers need the AcrPull role but 
shouldn’t require Reader capabilities.
 
Scan Azure Container Registry images
Microsoft Defender for Container Registries, part of Microsoft Defender for Cloud, can be 
configured to analyze container images that are
 ■ Pushed to an Azure Container Registry instance
 ■ Imported into the Registry instance
 ■ Pulled in the last 30 days by container technologies, including Docker, Azure Container 
Apps, or Kubernetes.

Network isolation for Azure Kubernetes Service
Azure Kubernetes Service supports two different methods for deploying AKS clusters to Azure 
Virtual Networks:
 ■ Azure CNI (Container Network Interface) networking  When you use Azure CNI 
networking with AKS, pods are allocated individual IP addresses that can route traffic to 
other network resources in Azure or hosted on on-premises networks. AKS includes an 
Azure CNI plugin to support this functionality.
 ■ Kubernetes networking  Azure will manage the virtual network resources during 
cluster deployment and use the Kubernetes plugin.
MORE INFO MICROSOFT DEFENDER FOR CONTAINER REGISTRIES
You can learn more about Microsoft Defender for container registries at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/defender-for-cloud/defender-for-containers-enable .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 3  Secure Azure Compute196
From a security perspective, Microsoft recommends choosing Azure CNI networking as this 
network model provides greater isolation of resources and controls. With Azure CNI network-
ing, virtual network resources are stored in a separate Azure resource group to the AKS cluster, 
and you delegate permissions to the AKS cluster identity to manage those resources. The CKS 
cluster identity requires the Network Contributor permission to access the subnet used by the 
virtual network. The advantage of having separate resource groups for AKS and virtual  
network resources is that you can more easily delegate separate permissions.
By default, all traffic is permitted between a cluster’s pods. To make this configuration 
more secure, use Network Policy to control traffic flow between AKS pods. You can enable the 
Network Policy feature when you create a new AKS cluster but you are presently unable to 
enable Network Policy on already deployed AKS clusters. Microsoft recommends you only use 
Network Policy with Linux-based nodes and not those that use Windows Server nodes. Net-
work policies are Kubernetes resources that use YAML manifest with ingress and egress rules 
managing traffic flow.
You should ensure that you do not expose AKS nodes directly to remote networks. Traffic 
served by the AKS cluster to hosts on remote networks should transit a web application firewall 
or Azure Application Gateway. Management tasks on nodes that require remote access should 
transit a bastion host located on a specially configured management virtual network. The man -
agement virtual network should be configured so that it is securely peered with the AKS cluster 
virtual network. Access to the management network and the bastion host should be restricted 
to connections transiting a known Azure VPN gateway or ExpressRoute connection. 
 
Monitor Azure Kubernetes Services
There are a variety of tools that integrate with Azure Monitor that you can use to monitor 
Azure Kubernetes Services. Which tool you use depends on which element of the AKS  
deployment you want to monitor. Monitoring tools include the following:
 ■ Network Watcher  Use this set of tools to observe the virtual networks used by AKS 
clusters.
 ■ Traffic analytics  This feature of Network Watcher allows you to examine flow logs to 
understand traffic flow for AKS. Traffic analytics allows you to determine if any traffic 
in the AKS deployment is transiting unexpected ports or interacting with unexpected 
public IP addresses. You can use traffic analytics to determine if you need to harden 
your network rules.
 ■ Network insights  Generates a visual representation of the performance and health 
of different network elements used by AKS.
MORE INFO AKS NETWORK CONNECTIVITY AND SECURITY

learn.microsoft.com/azure/aks/operator-best-practices-network .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 3.2: Secure Kubernetes and container workloads   CHAPTER 3 197
 ■ Network Observability add-on for AKS  Allows you to observe and monitor how 
different services in the AKS deployment interact. This is also termed monitoring  
east-west traffic.
 ■ Container Insights  Leverages a containerized version of the Azure Monitor agent 
to collect stdout/stderr logs, performance metrics, and Kubernetes events at the node 
level. Container Insights also allows you to collect metrics from the Kubernetes control 
plane.
 ■ Azure Monitor managed service for Prometheus  This is a cloud-native metrics 
solution from the Cloud Native Compute Foundation. This fully managed service works 
with the Prometheus query language and alerts. 
 ■ Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes  When the Arc agent is installed, you can manage, 
configure, and monitor Kubernetes clusters running in other clouds and on-premises. 
This includes using Container Insights and Prometheus. 
 ■ Azure Managed Grafana  Allows you to use a managed implementation of Grafana, 
an open-source data visualization platform used to represent Prometheus data. 
 ■ Application Insights  Enables application performance monitoring for applications 
running on an AKS cluster. Allows you to diagnose latency and reliability issues through 
analysis of distributed traces.
 
Secure Azure Kubernetes Services
One of the most direct methods that you can use to secure Azure Kubernetes Services is to 
enable Defender for Containers as part of Microsoft Defender for Cloud. Defender for  
Containers can
 ■ Assess AKS cluster configurations and provide security recommendations
 ■ Perform vulnerability scans
 ■ Provide real-time protection and alerting for Kubernetes nodes and clusters
You should use the principle of least privilege when it comes to users and groups and apply 
this reasoning to the resources containers can access. Use built-in Kubernetes pod security 
contexts to define which user or group account to run as or which Linux capabilities to expose. 
Pods should run as a defined user or group and should never be configured to run with or have 
access to root permissions. You configure which user or group a pod or container can leverage 
using the 
securityContext for a pod. 
When configuring a container to run as a non-root user, containers cannot bind to privi-
leged ports under 1024. If you run Linux nodes and pods, you can also control container 
actions using built-in Linux security features such as seccomp and AppArmor. Seccomp 
limits container process calls; AppArmor restricts read, write, and execute actions or system 
MORE INFO MONITOR AKS
You can learn more about monitoring AKS at https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/aks/monitor-aks.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 3  Secure Azure Compute198
functions, such as mounting file systems. AppArmor works with any application that runs on 
Linux and is not limited to Kubernetes pods.
Another important element of keeping AKS secure is regularly upgrading your AKS cluster. 
You can use the az aks get-upgrade command to view the versions available for your cluster 
and the az aks get-upgrade command to upgrade the cluster safely. When you use the az aks 
get-upgrade upgrade command, the following occurs:
 ■ Nodes are cordoned and drained one at a time
 ■ Pods are scheduled on the remaining nodes
 ■ A new node is deployed running an updated OS and Kubernetes version.
 
Azure Kubernetes Services authentication
Azure Kubernetes Services can be deployed with Microsoft Entra Integration. You can enable 
managed identity authentication for Kubernetes clusters with 
kubelogin. Integrating with 
Entra allows you to configure Kubernetes role-based access control based on a user’s Entra ID 
account or group membership. Entra authentication integrates with Azure Kubernetes Services 
clusters through OpenID Connect. 
To install the Azure Kubernetes Service add-on for managed identity, you need to meet the 
following prerequisites: 
 ■ You need Kubectl version 1.18.1 or later. If you are using Azure CLI and the Azure Power-
Shell module, these commands will automatically be at a supported level. You also need 
to ensure that the difference between the minor version of Kubernetes and Kubectl is 
not more than one, as this may cause authentication problems.
 ■ If you use Helm, it needs to be a minimum of version 3.3.
 ■ You need a Microsoft Entra group for the cluster, which will be registered as an admin 
group to grant admin permissions. 
When you deploy an AKS cluster, a system-assigned managed identity related to the cluster 
can be automatically provisioned in Entra ID. You can ensure that an AKS cluster uses managed 
identity using the 
az aks create command with the --enable-managed-identity parameter. 
You can configure a system-assigned managed identity on an existing AKS cluster that uses 
a service principal by using the az aks update command with the  
--enable-managed-identity 
parameter.
You create an AKS cluster and enable administrative access for a Microsoft Entra group by 
using the az aks create command with the --enable-aad and --aad-admin-group-objects-id

TABLE 3-2 AKS built-in roles
Role Description
Azure Kubernetes Service 
RBAC Reader
Allows read only access to see most objects in the namespace. Does not  
provide access to view roles, role bindings, or Secrets.
Azure Kubernetes Service 
RBAC Writer
Allows read/write access to most objects in the namespaces. Does not  
provide access to view roles or role bindings. Does allow access to Secrets  
and running Pods as any ServiceAccount in the namespace.
Azure Kubernetes Service 
RBAC Admin
Allows admin access to most resources in a namespace and is able to create 
roles and role bindings within the namespace. Does not allow write access to 
the resource quota or the namespace itself.
Azure Kubernetes Service 
RBAC Cluster Admin
This provides super-user access and allows any action to be performed on 
any resource in the cluster and all namespaces.

Skill 2.3: Secure APIs published in Azure
This objective deals with securing APIs published in Azure. Organizations often publish APIs in 
Azure and as is the case with any resource you make available publicly, the moment you expose 
the resource, people of nefarious intent will try to exploit that resource. An exploited API can-
not only be used to gain access to other resources in the cloud but may also be attacked in 
such a way as to increase your organization’s Azure bill substantively. In this section, you will 
learn about Azure API Management and the recommended security baseline for API manage-
ment in Azure.
Azure API Management
Azure API Management is an Azure service that allows you to securely expose Azure and 
specially configured external services as APIs. APIs are standards-based integration tools for 
exposing and accessing data, processes, and applications. APIs allow an organization to create 
an abstraction layer for legacy services, making those services more easily interact with  
modern cloud services and apps. 
Azure API Management includes the following components that run as managed services in 
Azure:
 ■ API Gateway The API gateway is the interface through which client applications inter-
act. The API gateway processes client traffic and forwards it to the appropriate services. 
The API gateway is responsible for verifying API keys and other credentials, enforcing 
usage quotas and rate limits, and transforming requests and responses as specified by 
policy statements. The API Gateway also caches responses to reduce latency and gener-
ates logs, metrics, and traces used for monitoring, reporting, and troubleshooting. The 
self-hosted API gateway allows you to deploy a Linux-based Docker container in your 
on-premises environment connected to the API Management service in Azure. 
 ■ Management plane  The management plane is where you, as the API publisher, provi-
sion and configure API Management service settings, configure API schemas, package 
APIs into consumable products, define policies including quotas and rate limits, view 
analytics information, and manage user access.
 ■ Developer portal  The developer portal is a website that provides documentation 
for the APIs you publish. The developer portal allows you to configure an API to be 
published through an interactive console, allows those that will consume APIs to create 
accounts and subscribe to access API keys, manage API keys, view analytics related to 
their own usage, and download API definitions.
API Management has the following built-in groups:
 ■ Administrators  Can manage API Management service instances and create APIs and 
the operations and products that developers access. Subscription Global Administrators 
are members of this group.
 ■ Developers These are developers you grant access to the developer portal. They build 
applications that call the functionality of the APIs that you publish. Developers can sign up 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 2.3: Secure APIs published in Azure   CHAPTER 3 201
on the developer portal or be invited by an Administrator. When a developer subscribes to 
a published API, they are granted the primary and secondary keys to the API. 
 ■ Guests Developer portal users who have not authenticated. They can be granted 
read-only access to view but not call APIs.
 
API authentication and authorization
Azure API Management supports the OAuth 2.0 authorization protocol between the client 
and API Gateway and between the gateway and the backend API or service. The management 
plane instance uses Entra ID and Azure RBAC to secure and restrict access.

steps:

Defender for APIs
Defender for APIs is an element of Microsoft Defender for Cloud, which provides security  
protection for APIs managed by Azure API management. Defender for APIs provides the  
following functionality:
 ■ Identifies external, unused, and unauthenticated APIs
 ■ Identifies APIs that receive or respond to sensitive data
 ■ Can apply configuration recommendations to harden APIs and API management 
services
 ■ Detect suspicious API traffic patterns, including OSAWSP API, to 10 vulnerability exploits
 ■ Integrates with Defender for Cloud Security Posture management and SIEM systems
To enable Defender for APIs for a subscription, perform the following steps:
1. Open the Azure API Management instance in your subscription.
2. In the left menu under Security , select Defender For Cloud. 
3. On the Microsoft Defender For APIs  page shown in Figure 3-21, choose Enable 
Defender On The Subscription .

4. On the Defender Plan  page, choose On The APIs Plan and select Save .

the API management instance to view recommendations and security insights about the API 
instance.

Security Baseline for API Management
The Azure security baseline for API Management recommends Microsoft’s best practice 
security advice for managing API security. The Azure Security Baseline for API management 
includes the following recommendations:
 ■ Deploy Azure API Management Inside An Azure Virtual Network  Use network 
security groups to restrict traffic by port, protocol, source IP address, and destination 
IP address so that only authorized hosts can access the API management plane and 
developer portal. You should deploy a private endpoint if you cannot deploy API man-
agement within an Azure virtual network. You can only enable private endpoints for API 
management if the instance has not been configured with an external or internal virtual 
network.
 ■ Deploy Application Gateway As A Reverse Proxy, Load Balancer, And Web  
Application Firewall When Exposing APIs To Both Internal And External  
Consumers
 This acts as a reverse proxy and provides load balancing, routing, web 
application firewall, and other protections.
 ■ Configure Azure API Management Developer Portal To Authenticate  
Developer Accounts Using Entra ID
 Ensure that Entra is used to authenticate  
developer accounts and configure security with those accounts.
 ■ Configure Azure API Management Instance To Protect APIs Using OAuth 2.0 
With Entra ID
 Use OAtuth 2.0 with Entra as the authorization protocol to configure 
authorization.
 ■ Use An Entra ID Managed Service Identity To Allow The API Management 
Instance To Connect To Other Resources In Azure, Such As Azure Key Vault
 Use 
managed service identity rather than API keys to connect to other resources in Azure.
 ■ Ensure That Secrets For API Management (Named Values) Are Stored In Azure 
Key Vault
 Use best practice for secrets by storing them in Azure Key Vault rather than 
in other locations.
 ■ Use RBAC And Principle Of Least Privilege To Control Access To Azure API 
Management  Minimize the privileges assigned when granting access to Azure API 
management.
 ■ Enable Resource Logs For API Management So That You Have A Record About 
Operations And Errors For Auditing And Troubleshooting
 Resource logs for API 
Management include GatewayLogs and WebSocketConnectionLogs.
You can monitor the Azure security baseline for API management and recommendations 
using Microsoft Defender for Cloud.

api-management/protect-with-defender-for-apis .

Thought experiment
In this thought experiment, demonstrate your skills and knowledge of the topics covered in this 
chapter. You can find answers to this thought experiment in the next section.
Advanced security for compute at Tailwind Traders
You are one of the Azure administrators for Tailwind Traders, an online general store special-
izing in various home products. Tailwind Traders is deploying new VMs in Azure to increase 
the compute capacity because the company is forecasting an increase in online store shopping 
during the upcoming holiday season. Before releasing those VMs for use, they need to ensure 
that these VMs are configured to use security best practices, which include secure configu-
rations, endpoint protection installation, and ensuring that the operating system is fully up 
to date.Currently, Tailwind Traders does not have any cloud security posture management 
in place, but the company is interested in trying Microsoft Defender for Cloud. To improve 
security, they also need to continuously monitor those servers to identify potential attacks, and 
they want to receive an alert in case there are suspicious activities or indications of an attack 
against those servers. Another goal of Tailwind Traders is to ensure that Linux and Windows 
Server IaaS VMs are always kept up to date with the latest software updates. You also want to 
audit application activity on Linux and Windows Server IaaS VMs and then block any appli-
cation outside those that normally run. With this information in mind, answer the following 
questions:
1.
 Will Microsoft Defender for Cloud meet those requirements?
2. What Azure service allows Linux and Windows Server IaaS VMs to remain up to date 
with software updates?
3.
 What Azure service allows you to block applications that don’t normally run on an  
IaaS VM?

Chapter summary   CHAPTER 3 207
Thought experiment answers
This section contains the solution to the thought experiment. Each answer explains why the 
answer choice is correct.
1. Microsoft Defender for Cloud will only achieve partial results for the desired require-
ments. It will enable the administrator to see security recommendations and improve 
the security posture of the workloads. However, the administrator must enable Micro-
soft Defender for Servers to monitor threat detection continuously.
2.
 Azure Update Manager allows Linux and Windows Server IaaS VMs to remain up to date 
with software updates.
3.
 Adaptive application controls allow you to create a list of applications that commonly 
execute on an IaaS VM based on monitoring that VM and block any that attempt to 
execute that are not on the list.
Chapter summary
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Servers includes several tools to secure Azure IaaS virtual 
machines, including security baselines, threat and vulnerability management, just- 
in-time VM access, file integrity monitoring, adaptive application controls, and adaptive 
network hardening.
 ■  Azure Update Manager automates the deployment of updates to Windows and Linux 
IaaS VMs on a scheduled or ad-hoc basis.
 ■ Azure Disk Encryption supports BitLocker for encrypting Windows VM disks and  
DM-Crypt for encrypting Linux VM disks using keys stored in Azure Key Vault.
 ■ Azure Automanage automates the deployment and configuration of several Azure 
services to secure and monitor IaaS VMs.
 ■ Azure Container Apps support managed identities, storing secrets in Azure Key Vault, 
and security monitoring through logs, metrics, and alerts.
 ■ Azure Container Registry has roles and permissions to control access and can be inte-
grated with Microsoft Defender for Cloud to scan images for vulnerabilities.
 ■ Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) supports Azure CNI networking for pod isolation, 
network policies to control traffic, integration with Azure Monitor for observability, and 
Entra ID for authentication and RBAC.
 ■ Azure API Management allows securely publishing APIs, supports OAuth 2.0 authentica-
tion with Azure AD, and can be protected using Microsoft Defender for APIs.
 ■ The Azure security baseline for API Management provides best practice recommenda-
tions for securely deploying and configuring API Management.

CHAPTER 4
Secure Azure Storage
Unsecured data storage containers are the source of many data breaches in the cloud. These 
breaches occur because storage containers that administrators believe are only accessible 
to a select group of authorized people are, in fact, configured so that they are accessible to 
everyone in the world who knows the storage container’s address. This objective deals with 
securing storage in Azure, from configuring access control for storage accounts to managing 
storage account keys. You’ll learn about shared access signatures, storage service encryp-
tion, shared access policies, and how to use Azure AD to authenticate user access to storage 
resources in Azure.
Skills covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 4.1:  Microsoft Defender for Storage
 ■ Skill 4.2: Storage account access control
 ■ Skill 4.3: Storage service encryption
 ■ Skill 4.4: Storage account keys
 ■ Skill 4.5: Using Entra with storage
Skill 4.1: Microsoft Defender for Storage
This objective deals with Microsoft Defender for Storage, an element of Microsoft Defender 
for Cloud that is specifically tailored towards protecting storage resources in Azure. Poorly 
configured cloud storage accounts have caused many large data breaches. Implementing 
Microsoft Defender for Storage can help minimize the chances your organization will suffer a 
similar breach. 
Defender for Storage 
Microsoft Defender for Storage allows you to detect unusual and malicious attempts to inter-
act with Azure Storage accounts. When you enable Microsoft Defender for Storage, security 
alerts will trigger when Azure detects anomalous storage account activity. These detections 
are based on existing recognized patterns of malicious activity identified by Microsoft  
security researchers. 
209
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage210
Microsoft Defender for Storage includes the following functionality:
 ■ Scans and detects malware in storage, including archives of uploaded blobs. This allows 
you to limit the chance that your storage accounts are used to store and distribute 
malware.
 ■ Allows you to assess and respond to threats based on data sensitivity. 
 ■ Detection of suspicious activities performed by entities without identities, such as those 
accessing data through Shared Access Signatures (SAS tokens)
 ■ Allows for data plane and control plane telemetry analysis related to Azure Blob  
Storage, Azure Files, and Azure Data Lake Storage without configuring diagnostic logs.
Microsoft Defender for Storage is available for Blob Storage, Azure Files, and Azure Data 
Lake Storage Gen2. General-Purpose V2, Block Blob, and Blob Storage accounts support this 
service.
 
Malware Scanning
Attackers often try to upload malicious files to Azure Blob Storage to distribute malware when 
attempting to compromise an organization. The Malware Scanning functionality of Defender 
for Storage protects Azure Blob Storage by examining files for malicious characteristics as they 
are accessed. Malware scanning has the following features:
 ■ Uses Microsoft Defender Antivirus to find polymorphic and metamorphic malware
 ■ Scans all files under 2GB, including archive files
 ■ Can be configured to quarantine or delete suspicious files
 ■ Scans all content uploaded to storage and can be configured to log all scan results
Malware scans are instigated by any operation that results in a BlobCreated event in Azure 
Blob Storage. This includes uploading new blobs, overwriting existing blobs,  and modifying 
existing blobs.
Defender for storage requires the following permissions:
 ■ Activity monitoring  Requires the Security Admin role or equivalent at the  
subscription level.
 ■ Malware scanning  Requires the Storage Account Owner role or equivalent at the 
storage account level.
 ■ Sensitive data threat detection  Requires the Storage Account Owner role or  
equivalent at the storage account level.
MORE INFO MICROSOFT DEFENDER FOR STORAGE
You can learn more about Microsoft Defender for Storage at https://learn.microsoft.com/
azure/defender-for-cloud/defender-for-storage-introduction .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.1: Microsoft Defender for Storage   CHAPTER 4 211
When you enable malware scanning, the following actions occur in your Azure subscription:
 ■ An Event Grid System Topic resource is created in the same resource group for each 
storage account on which you enable malware scanning. This resource is used by the 
malware scanning service to listen for blob upload triggers.
 ■ A Data Scanner resource called StorageDataScanner is created in the subscription and  
is configured with a system-assigned managed identity. This resource will be assigned 
the Storage Blob Data Owner role assignment and is used to access data for malware 
scanning and sensitive data recovery.
Scan results are available through four different channels:
 ■ Blob index tags  These are metadata fields on each blob. Scan results are present in 
the blob metadata.
 ■ Defender for Cloud security alerts  Alerts are generated each time a malicious file is 
detected. Navigating to the Security Alerts  section of Microsoft Defender for Cloud 
Event Grid event makes these visible. Scan results can automatically be sent to an Event 
Grid topic, allowing you to trigger a Function App, Webhook, Event Hub, or Service Bus 
Queue.
 ■ Log Analytics  Defender for Storage can be configured to log scan results to a Log 
Analytics Workspace destination.
At the time of writing, malware scanning isn’t supported for Azure Files. It is also not  
possible for malware scanning to examine client-side encrypted blobs while data encrypted at 
rest by customer-managed keys can be examined.
 
Sensitive data threat detection
The sensitive data threat detection functionality of Microsoft Defender for Cloud allows you to 
prioritize security alerts based on the sensitivity classification of the data subject to the alert. 
Sensitive data threat detection is integrated with Microsoft Purview’s sensitive information 
types and classification labels, allowing you to ensure that detecting what constitutes sensitive 
data aligns with your organization’s existing data classification policies.
By prioritizing threat detection based on the sensitivity of the data, you are likely to reduce 
the likelihood of exposure events related to sensitive data. Sensitive data threat detection is a 
configurable feature in Defender for Storage that can be enabled without additional cost.
Sensitive data discovery can be used with the following:
 ■ Block blob storage accounts in Azure Storage
 ■ Azure Data Lake Storage Gen2
 ■ Storage accounts behind private networks
MORE INFO MICROSOFT DEFENDER FOR STORAGE MALWARE SCANNING
You can learn more about Microsoft Defender for Storage malware scanning at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/azure/defender-for-cloud/defender-for-storage-malware-scan .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage212
 ■ Storage accounts encrypted with customer-managed server-side keys are supported
 ■ Databases
 ■ Azure SQL Databases
Sensitive data threat detection will not only classify data but will report if the following 
conditions exist:
 ■ Data is stored in a location exposed to the Internet  This alert will be raised if the 
storage account has network access enabled from all networks or if access is enabled 
from selected virtual networks and IP addresses. 
 ■ Storage account allows public access  This alert will be raised if the storage 
account’s Allow Blob Public Access setting is enabled and the Public Access Level 
set to Blob (anonymous read access for blobs only) is also enabled. This alert will also 
be raised if the Public Access Level is set to Container  (anonymous read access for 
containers and blobs).
Sensitive data discovery is supported on the following file types: 
.doc, .docm, .docx, .dot, 
.gz, .odp, .ods, .odt, .pdf, .pot, .pps, .ppsx, .ppt, .pptm, .pptx, .xlc, .xls, .xlsb, 
.xlsm, .xlsx, .xlt, .csv, .json, .psv, .ssv, .tsv, .txt., .xml, .parquet, .avro, and 
.orc. Sensitive data threat detection requires Subscription Owner permission over the storage 
account. To view and edit data sensitivity settings, you need access to an account with either 
the Global Administrator, Compliance Administrator, Compliance Data Administrator, Security 
Administrator, or Security Operator roles.
 
Skill 4.2: Storage account access control
This objective deals with controlling access to different sorts of Azure storage. To master this 
objective, you’ll need to understand the different sorts of storage available in the cloud and 
the Azure RBAC roles you configure when assigning rights to those storage entities.
Storage account access control
Storage accounts are containers for Azure Storage data objects, such as blobs, files, queues, 
tables, and disks. Azure supports the following types of storage accounts:
 ■ General-Purpose V2 accounts  Stores blobs, files, queues, and tables. They are rec -
ommended for the majority of storage scenarios. General-Purpose V2 accounts replace 
General-Purpose V1 accounts, which you should not use for new deployments and 
should be migrated away from if used in existing deployments.
MORE INFO SENSITIVE DATA THREAT DETECTION
You can learn more about detecting threats to sensitive data at https://learn.microsoft.com/
azure/defender-for-cloud/defender-for-storage-data-sensitivity .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.2: Storage account access control   CHAPTER 4 213
 ■ BlockBlobStorage accounts  Storage accounts are recommended for scenarios with 
high transaction rates for block blobs and append blobs. Also, they are recommended 
for scenarios that require smaller objects or consistently low storage latency.
 ■ FileStorage accounts  High-performance, files-only storage accounts. Recommended 
for high-performance applications.
 ■ BlobStorage accounts Legacy storage account type that you should not use for new 
deployments and should be migrated away from if they are used in existing deployments.
The recommended method of managing access control for storage accounts in the manage-
ment plane is to use RBAC roles. RBAC roles for storage can be assigned at the following levels:
 ■ Individual container  Role assignments at this scope apply to all blobs in the con-
tainer. Role assignments also apply to container properties and metadata when the 
container is accessed at the management plane.
 ■ Individual queue  Role assignments at this scope apply to all messages in the queue. 
Role assignments also apply to queue properties and metadata when the queue is 
accessed at the management plane.
 ■ Storage account Role assignments at this scope apply to all containers, all blobs 
within those containers, all queues, and all messages.
 ■ Resource group  Role assignments at this scope apply to all storage accounts in the 
resource group and all items within those storage accounts.
 ■ Subscription  Role assignments at this scope apply to all storage accounts in the  
subscription and all items within those storage accounts.
 ■ Management group Role assignments at this scope apply to all storage accounts and 
all items within those storage accounts within all subscriptions in the management group.
When assigning an RBAC role, remember the rule of least privilege and assign the role with 
the narrowest possible scope. This means that if an individual user or application only requires 
access to a specific storage account and multiple storage accounts in a resource group, you 
should only assign the role at the storage account level. In addition to the rule of least privi-
lege, remember to assign roles to groups rather than individual users. This way, role assign-
ment becomes a matter of adding and removing user accounts from a specific group. Rather 
than assigning roles to individual users or applications, you should assign the role to a group 
and then add the user and application accounts to that group to manage the role assignments. 
Table 4-1 lists the RBAC roles that are appropriate for storage accounts:
TABLE 4-1 Storage account RBAC roles
Storage-related RBAC Role RBAC Role Description
Storage Account Contributor Allows management of storage accounts. Has access to 
the account key and can access data using Shared Key 
authorization.
Storage Account Key Operator Service Role Can list and regenerate storage account access keys.
Storage Blob Data Contributor Can read, write, and delete Azure Storage containers and blobs.

Configure access for storage blobs
To assign specific rights to blob storage, you can configure RBAC permissions against the 
appropriate container or storage account. You determine what access the user or application 
requires, create an Entra ID group, assign the group the appropriate RBAC permission, and 
then add the user account or service principal to the Entra ID group.
Azure includes the following built-in roles for authorizing access to blob data:
 ■ Storage Blob Data Owner  Allows the security principal to set ownership and manage 
POSIX access control for Azure Data Lake Storage Gen2.
 ■ Storage Blob Data Contributor  Grants the security principal read/write/delete  
permissions to Blob Storage resources.
 ■ Storage Blob Data Reader  Allows the security principal to view items in Blob Storage.
 ■ Storage Blob Delegator Allows the security principal to acquire the user delegation 
key, which, in turn, can be used to create a shared access signature for a container or blob. 
This shared access signature is signed with the security principal’s Entra ID credentials.
 ■ Storage Queue Data Contributor  Grants the security principal read/write and delete 
permissions to Azure Storage queues.
 ■ Storage Queue Data Reader  Allows the security principal to view the messages in 
Azure Storage queues.
 ■ Storage Queue Data Message Processor  Allows the security principal to peek, 
retrieve, and delete messages in Azure Storage queues.
 ■ Storage Queue Data Message Sender  Allows the security principal to add messages 
in Azure Storage queues.
 
Configure access for queue storage
The method you use to assign specific rights to queue storage is to configure RBAC permis -
sions against the appropriate queue or storage account. You determine what access the user or 
application requires, create an Entra ID group, assign the group the appropriate RBAC permis-
sion, and then add the user account or service principal to the Entra ID group.
Azure includes the following built-in roles for authorizing access queue data:
 ■ Storage Queue Data Contributor  Grants the security principal read/write and delete 
permissions to Azure Storage queues.

Configure access for table storage
The method you use to assign specific rights to table storage is to configure RBAC permissions 
against the appropriate storage table or storage account. You determine what access the user 
or application requires, create an Entra ID group, assign the group the appropriate RBAC per-
mission, and then add the user account or service principal to the Entra ID group.
Azure includes the following built-in roles for authorizing access queue data:
 ■ Storage Table Data Contributor  Allows you to grant read/write/delete permissions 
to Table storage resources.
 ■ Storage Table Data Reader  Allows you to grant read-only permission to Table  
storage resources.
 
Storage account data protection
Azure Storage provides data protection options for recovering data that may have been 
deleted or overwritten. You can configure data protection options for storage accounts in the 
Data Protection  section of a storage account’s properties, as shown in Figure 4-4.
Data protection strategies for storage accounts include:
 ■ Configuring Azure Resource Manager locks on storage accounts to protect them from 
configuration changes or deletions.
 ■ Enabling the soft delete option on storage accounts allows you to recover deleted  
containers and their contents for a limited time. The default is 7 days.
 ■ Enable blob versioning, which automatically saves data each time a blob is overwritten.
 ■ Configure an immutability policy to block a container and its blobs from being deleted 
or modified.
 ■ Perform blob snapshots to manually save a blob’s state at a given point in time.

Skill 4.3: Storage service encryption
This objective deals with storage service encryption. Storage service encryption automatically 
encrypts data stored in Azure. Data stored in Azure is transparently encrypted and decrypted 
using 256-bit AES encryption. Any block blobs, append blobs, or page blobs written to Azure 
Storage is subject to Azure Storage encryption. You cannot disable Azure Storage encryp-
tion, and altering code or applications is unnecessary to take advantage of Azure Storage 
encryption.
Configure Storage Service Encryption
Azure Storage encryption is enabled by default for all storage accounts regardless of perfor-
mance or access tiers. This means you don’t have to modify code or applications for Azure 
Storage Encryption to be enabled.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage220
 
Encryption Key Management
By default, Azure Storage accounts encrypt data stored using an encryption key managed by 
Microsoft. If having Microsoft managing Azure Storage account encryption keys is considered 
undesirable, you can manage encryption using your own keys, as shown in Figure 4-6.
FIGURE 4-6  Configure encryption type
When you choose to manage encryption with keys that you provide, you have the following 
options:
 ■ Use a customer-managed key with Azure Key Vault  In this scenario, you upload 
your encryption key to an Azure Key Vault or use Azure Key Vault APIs to generate keys. 
The storage account and the Key Vault need to be in the same Azure region and associ-
ated with the same Entra ID tenancy. The storage account and Key Vault do not need to 
be in the same subscription.
 ■ Use a customer-provided key on Blob Storage operations  In this scenario, encryp-
tion keys are provided per request. Customer-provided keys can be stored in Azure Key 
Vault or in an alternate key store.
Double encryption
Azure Storage automatically encrypts all data in an Azure Storage account using 265-bit AES 
encryption. When you enable infrastructure encryption, the data in the storage account will be 
encrypted twice. This is termed double encryption. Data is first encrypted using one encryption 
algorithm and one key at the service level and then is encrypted at the infrastructure level using a 
separate encryption algorithm and encryption key. This double encryption protects data if one of 
MORE INFO STORAGE SERVICE ENCRYPTION
You can learn more about Storage Service Encryption at https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/
storage/common/storage-service-encryption .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.3: Storage service encryption   CHAPTER 4 221
the encryption algorithms or keys becomes compromised. While service-level encryption allows 
you to use either Microsoft-managed or customer-managed keys, infrastructure-level encryption 
only uses a Microsoft-managed key. Infrastructure encryption must be enabled during storage 
account creation. It is not possible to convert an existing storage account to support infrastruc-
ture encryption if it was not created with that option enabled.
 
Encryption Scopes
Azure Storage accounts use a single encryption key for all encryption operations across the 
storage account. Encryption scopes allow you to configure separate encryption keys at the 
container and blob levels. This allows for scenarios such as storing customer data from differ-
ent customers in the same storage account while having each customer’s data protected by a 
different encryption key.
To create a new encryption scope, perform the following steps:
1.
 In the Azure portal, open the storage account on which you want to configure  
encryption scopes.
2.
 On the storage account’s page, select Encryption , as shown in Figure 4-7, and then 
select Encryption Scopes .
FIGURE 4-7 Encryption Scopes
3. On the Encryption Scopes  page, click Add.
4. On the Create Encryption Scope  page, provide an encryption scope name and then 
specify whether the encryption scope will use Microsoft-Managed Keys  or Customer-
Managed Keys, as shown in Figure 4-8.
MORE INFO STORAGE ACCOUNT WITH INFRASTRUCTURE ENCRYPTION

Skill 4.4: Storage account keys
This objective deals with storage account keys. Storage account access keys allow you to 
authorize access to storage account data. Each Azure Storage account has an associated pair 
of 512-bit storage account access keys. If someone has access to an Azure Storage account key, 
they have access to the associated storage account. 
Configure storage account access keys
The best practice is only to use the first key and to keep the second key in reserve. You then 
switch to using the second key when you perform key rotation. This allows you to generate a 
new primary key, which you will switch to when you perform key rotation in the future. The 
recommended location for storing storage account access keys is Azure Key Vault.
Because only a single pair of access keys is associated with a storage account, you should 
rotate and regenerate access keys periodically. Rotating storage account access keys ensures 
that if a storage account key is leaked, the leak will be automatically remediated when existing 
storage account keys reach their end of life. For example, if you rotate keys every six weeks, 
the maximum amount of time a leaked key remains valid is six weeks. Even if you don’t have 
reason to believe that a storage account key has leaked, the best practice is to rotate them 
MORE INFO STORAGE ACCOUNT ENCRYPTION SCOPES
You can learn more about storage account encryption scopes at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/blobs/encryption-scope-manage .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage224
periodically. Just because you don’t have reason to believe that a storage account key hasn’t 
leaked doesn’t mean it isn’t accessible to someone who shouldn’t have access to it.
View storage account access keys
Viewing a storage account access key requires either Service Administrator, Owner, Contribu-
tor, or storage account Key Operator Service roles on the storage account the key is associated 
with. You can also access the key if you have been assigned an RBAC role that includes the 
Microsoft.Storage/storageAccounts/listkeys/action permission on a scope that includes the 
storage account.
To view a storage account’s storage account keys in the Azure portal, perform the following 
steps:
1. In the Azure portal, navigate to the storage account for which you are interested in 
learning the storage account access key details.
2.
 On the Storage Account page, select Access Keys under Settings , as shown in  
Figure 4-11.
FIGURE 4-11 Access Keys in the Storage Account Keys menu
3. You can view and copy the first and second keys on the Access Keys page shown in 
Figure 4-12.

Manually rotating storage account access keys
The best practice is to rotate storage account access keys periodically. You should only use one 
storage account key at a time. Using only one key at a time will allow you to switch any applica-
tion to the second storage account key of the pair before rotating the first. As discussed earlier, 
after some time has passed, you repeat the process, switching the application to the newly

Skill 4.5: Using Entra with storage
Entra ID authenticates a security principal’s identity and then returns an OAuth 2.0 token. The 
client includes this token in the request to the Blob or Queue Storage being accessed by the 
security principal. You need to register an application with an Entra ID tenant before tokens can 
be issued in this manner. You can use Entra ID to authorize access to Blob and Queue Storage.
Configuring access to Azure Files
Azure Files allows you to deploy fully managed file shares in the cloud that are accessible using 
the Server Message Block (SMB) protocol, Network File System (NFS) protocol, as well as via 
Azure Files REST API without having to deploy and manage an IaaS VM configured as a file 
server.

azure/storage/common/shared-key-authorization-prevent .

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage228
You deploy Azure file shares into storage accounts. When deploying an Azure Files  
deployment, you can choose one of the following storage account options:
 ■ General purpose version 2 (GPv2) storage accounts  These storage accounts allow 
you to deploy onto traditional hard disk-based hardware. GPv2 storage accounts can 
also host blob containers, queues, or tables.
 ■ FileStorage storage accounts  FileStorage storage accounts allow you to deploy 
Azure File shares on premium/solid-state disk-based hardware. You cannot deploy 
other storage resources, such as blob containers, tables, and queues, in a FileStorage 
storage account.
Microsoft recommends the following when deploying Azure file shares:
 ■ You should only deploy Azure Files shares into storage accounts with other file 
shares
 While it is possible to deploy queues and tables into the same storage account, 
this may make troubleshooting performance issues more challenging.
 ■ Consider storage account IOPS limitations  If deploying multiple file shares to a sin -
gle storage account, remember that all file shares will contribute to a total IOPS metric. 
If you deploy multiple active file shares in the same storage account, their performance 
may cause throttling to occur. 
Azure Files supports the following authentication methods:
 ■ Azure Storage account key  You can mount Azure file shares with a storage account 
key. When you do this, you use the storage account name as the username and the 
storage account key as the password. The user that mounts an Azure file share using this 
method will have administrator access to all files, folders, and ACLs will be ignored.
 ■ Microsoft Entra Kerberos for hybrid identities  This configuration uses Microsoft 
Entra ID to issue Kerberos tickets to clients that access Azure file shares over the SMB 
protocol. Allows access to Azure File shares without requiring connectivity to an AD 
DS domain controller from Microsoft Entra hybrid joined and Microsoft Entra joined 
computers. 
 ■ Active Directory authentication over SMB for Linux clients  Identity-based 
authentication over SMBE for Linux clients using the Kerberos authentication protocol 
with either Microsoft Entra Domain Services or Active Directory Domain Services.
 ■ Microsoft Entra Domain Services  Entra Domain Services is a managed AD DS 
instance that is hosted in Azure. You can domain join a storage account to an Entra 
Domain Services instance. Using Entra Domain Services authentication for Azure Files is 
explored later in this chapter.
 ■ On-premises Active Directory Domain Services  You can domain join a storage 
account to an on-premises Active Directory Domain Services instance. This configura -
tion is explored later in this chapter.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.5: Using Entra with storage   CHAPTER 4 229
When planning Azure Files deployment, consider that many organizations block  
port 445 outbound and use this port for SMB communication. Similarly, NFS file shares rely 
on network-level authentication, which also means you’ll have to consider how your clients 
will connect to Azure File shares. You can consider the following options to allow secure 
access to Azure Files:
 ■ Provide clients with VPN or ExpressRoute connections to an Azure network that is  
permitted to access the Azure file share when using SMB. 
 ■ Use service endpoints or private endpoints when using NFS.
Azure Files supports the following encryption types:
 ■ Encryption in transit  By default, Azure Files only allows connections that are made 
with the SMB 3.x protocol or later. Any clients that do not support SMB encryption  
cannot mount Azure Files shares unless encryption in transit is disabled.
 ■ Encryption at rest  All files stored in Azure Files will be encrypted using storage  
service encryption. You learned about storage service encryption earlier in this  
chapter.
 
SMB file shares
SMB file shares are accessible from Windows, Linux and macOS clients. SMB file shares can be 
integrated with Azure File Sync, allowing on-premises file servers to be synchronized with file 
shares accessible through Azure. SMB file shares support the following features:
 ■ AD DS domain join
 ■ Discretionary access control lists (DACLs)
 ■ Serverless backup with Azure Backup
 ■ Network isolation with Azure private endpoints
 ■ SMB Multichannel high network throughput
 ■ SMB channel encryption using AES-256-GCM, AES-128-GCM, and AES-128-CCM
 ■ Previous versions of files support through Volume Shadow Copy Services integrated 
share snapshots
 ■ Automatic soft delete support
 ■ Internet-accessible file shares with SMB 3.0+
MORE INFO CONFIGURE ACCESS TO AZURE FILES
You can learn more about configuring access to Azure Files at https://learn.microsoft.com/
azure/storage/files/files-smb-protocol.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage230
 
NFS file shares
NFS file shares are traditionally used with Linux and UNIX clients. NFS file shares are used in the 
following scenarios:
 ■ Storage for Linux/UNIX-based applications. This includes line-of-business applications 
written using Linux or POSIX file system APIs
 ■ Workloads that require POSIX-compliant file shares, case sensitivity, or UNIX-style  
permissions, user ID, and group ID
 ■ Storage for applications or services that require random I/O and hierarchical storage
NFS file shares support the following features:
 ■ POSIX-compliant file system
 ■ Hard link support
 ■ Symbolic link support
NFS file shares do not support delegations, callbacks, Kerberos authentication, and  
encryption-in-transit. NFS file shares in Azure don’t support user-based authentication. You 
configure authentication using network security rules. When using NFS file shares, you must 
set up a service endpoint or private endpoint for the storage account that hosts the file share.
 
Entra Domain Services authentication
Entra Domain Services is an Azure service that works with Entra ID to provide the functionality 
of domain controllers on an Azure subnet. When you enable Entra DS, you can domain join a 
Windows client or server VM hosted on an Azure subnet without deploying VMs that function 
as domain controllers. You can’t use on-premises Active Directory authentication and Entra DS 
authentication on the same storage account or file shares.
Once you have enabled Entra DS on a subscription, you can enable identity-based access  
by selecting File Shares  under Data Storage  in the Storage Account blade, as shown in  
Figure 4-15, and selecting Identity-Based Access .
When you enable Identity-Based Access , you need to enable an identity source, as shown 
in Figure 4-16.

FIGURE 4-15 Identity-Based Access
FIGURE 4-16 Enable An Identity Source
Once an identity source has been enabled on the storage account, you can use the Access 
Control (IAM) page of the storage account’s properties to assign one of the following Storage 
File Share RBAC roles:
 ■ Storage File Data SMB Share Reader  This role provides read access to Azure file 
shares over SMB to users with this role.
 ■ Storage File Data SMB Share Contributor  Users with this role can read, write, and 
delete access to the Azure Storage file shares over SMB.
 ■ Storage File Data SMB Share Elevated Contributor  This role allows read, write, and 
delete access and the ability to modify Windows Access Control Lists (ACLs) of Azure 
Storage File shares over SMB.
When multiple roles are assigned, permissions are cumulative. The exception to this rule is 
when a deny permission applies; in this case, the deny permission overrides any allow permis-
sions. While it is possible to assign RBAC roles and configure share-level permissions at the 
storage account level, you should assign RBAC roles at the individual file share level instead. 
Full administrative control of file shares, which includes the ability to take ownership of files,

Using AD DS authentication with Azure Files
When you enable AD DS authentication for Azure Files, your Active Directory Domain Services 
(AD DS) domain-joined computers can mount Azure File shares using AD DS user credentials. 
Access occurs over an encrypted Server Message Block (SMB) protocol connection. You can 
secure Azure Files using identity-based authentication over Server Message Block (SMB), 
where Entra DS or an on-premises Active Directory Domain Services Domain (AD DS) functions 
as the identity provider. Entra Domain Services authentication for Azure Files currently sup-
ports the following scenarios:
 ■ If you use AD DS as your identity provider, you must use Entra ID Connect to synchro-
nize identities to Entra ID.
 ■ If you are using AD DS as your identity provider, you can access the file share using a 
computer that is a member of an AD DS domain. You cannot access the file share using a 
computer joined to the Entra DS domain.
 ■ If you are using Entra DS as an identity provider, you will need to access the file share 
using a computer that is a member of the Entra DS domain.
MORE INFO ENTRA DS AUTHENTICATION
You can learn more about Entra DS authentication for Azure Files at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/files/storage-files-identity-auth-domain-services-enable .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.5: Using Entra with storage   CHAPTER 4 233
 ■ When enabled, this form of authentication supports Azure file shares integrated with 
Azure File Sync.
 ■ This form of authentication supports single sign-on.
 ■ This authentication only supports access from accounts in the AD DS forest where the 
storage account is registered unless a specially configured forest trust is present.
When enabling AD authentication for Azure file shares, your first step is to create a storage 
account in a proximate region for the users who will access the files stored in the file share on 
that storage account. You should do this simply because accessing a storage account closer 
to you will provide a much better user experience than trying to open and save files to a file 
share located on the other side of the world. You won’t need to create any file shares from the 
storage account at the start of the process. Before creating the file shares, you’ll need to enable 
Active Directory authentication at the storage account level rather than at the individual file 
shares level.
Enabling AD DS authentication 
When enabling AD DS authentication, the first step is creating an identity representing the 
storage account in your on-premises Active Directory instance. First, create a new Kerberos key 
for the storage account using the following Azure PowerShell commands from Cloud Shell:
$ResourceGroupName = "<resource-group-name-here>"
$StorageAccountName = "<storage-account-name-here>"
New-AzStorageAccountKey -ResourceGroupName $ResourceGroupName  
-Name $StorageAccountName -KeyName kerb1
Get-AzStorageAccountKey -ResourceGroupName $ResourceGroupName  
-Name $StorageAccountName -ListKerbKey | where-object{$_.Keyname -contains "kerb1"}
Once the key has been generated, create a service account in your on-premises domain and 
configure the account with the following service principal name (SPN):"cifs/your-storage-
account-name-here.file.core.windows.net" using the setspn.exe command. Set the account 
password to the Kerberos key, configure the account’s password to never expire, and note the 
account security identifier (SID). You can use the Get-AdUser PowerShell cmdlet to determine 
the SID of a user account.
The next step is to use Azure PowerShell to enable Active Directory authentication. You can 
do this with the following command, substituting the appropriate values:
Set-AzStorageAccount `
        -ResourceGroupName "<your-resource-group-name-here>" `
        -Name "<your-storage-account-name-here>" `
        -EnableActiveDirectoryDomainServicesForFile $true `
        -ActiveDirectoryDomainName "<your-domain-name-here>" `
        -ActiveDirectoryNetBiosDomainName "<your-netbios-domain-name-here>" `
        -ActiveDirectoryForestName "<your-forest-name-here>" `
        -ActiveDirectoryDomainGuid "<your-guid-here>" `
        -ActiveDirectoryDomainsid "<your-domain-sid-here>" `
        -ActiveDirectoryAzureStorageSid "<your-storage-account-sid>"
You also have the option of using the AzFilesHybrid PowerShell module to perform steps 
similar to these. Using the AzFilesHybrid PowerShell module involves downloading the most 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

recent version of the module from Microsoft’s website, installing it on a domain-joined

Set-ExecutionPolicy -ExecutionPolicy Unrestricted -Scope CurrentUser
2. Switch to the directory where AzFilesHybrid has been decompressed and copy the files

.\CopyToPSPath.ps1

If you don't provide the OU name as an input parameter, the AD identity that 
represents the storage account is created under the root directory.
The Debug-AzStorageAccountAuth cmdlet allows you to conduct a set of basic checks 
on your AD configuration with the logged-in AD user once you have performed account 
registration:
Debug-AzStorageAccountAuth -StorageAccountName $StorageAccountName -ResourceGroupName 
$ResourceGroupName -Verbose
If you are unable to configure the on-premises service account so that its password does 
not expire, you’ll need to use the Update-AzStorageAccountADOjbectPassword cmdlet to update 
the Azure Storage account each time your on-premises service account password changes. 
This cmdlet is a part of the AzFilesHybrid module and must be run on a computer in the 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

on-premises AD DS-joined environment with an account that has permissions within AD DS 
and owner permissions to the storage account. The following command with appropriate

# Update the password of the AD DS account registered for the storage account

Configuring file and folder permissions
Once you have assigned share-level permissions by assigning the appropriate Storage File Data 
SMB Share RBAC role to an Azure File share, you should then configure file and folder permis -
sions on the share's contents. When reading the Azure documentation, most Windows Server 
administrators will recognize that NTFS permissions are called Windows ACLs.
You can configure file and folder permissions using the 
Set-ACL PowerShell cmdlet,  
icacls.exe command, or Windows File Explorer if you have mounted the shared folder on a 
computer running a Windows Client or Windows Server operating system.
 
Skill 4.6: Configure delegated access
Shared Access Signatures (SAS) allow you to provide secure, granular, and delegated access to 
storage accounts. Using an SAS, you can control what resources a client can access, the client’s 
permissions to those resources, and the length of time that access will persist. Stored access 
policies allow you to specifically control service-level shared access signatures. You can config -
ure stored access policies for blob containers, file shares, queues, and tables.
Shared Access Signatures
An SAS is a signed Uniform Resource Identifier (URI) that provides the address of one or more 
storage resources and includes a token that determines how the client may access the resource. 
MORE INFO ENABLE AD DS AUTHENTICATION
You can learn more about configuring AD DS authentication for Azure File Shares at https://
learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/files/storage-files-identity-ad-ds-enable .
MORE INFO FILE AND FOLDER PERMISSIONS FOR AZURE FILE SHARES
You can learn more about file and folder permissions for Azure file shares at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/azure/storage/files/storage-files-identity-ad-ds-configure-permissions.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage236
Azure Storage supports the following types of SAS:
 ■ User delegation SAS  User delegation SAS can only be used with Blob Storage. Entra 
ID secures user delegation SAS and the permissions configured for the SAS. 
 ■ Service SAS  Service SAS is secured with storage account keys. This SAS delegates 
access to one type of storage resource. Service SAS can be configured for Azure Files, 
Blob Storage, Queue Storage, or Table Storage.
 ■ Account SAS Account SAS is secured with the storage account keys. These keys can 
be used to delegate access. In addition to all the operations that can be made available 
with user delegation SAS or service SAS, account SAS allows you to delegate access to 
operations that apply at the service level, such as getting and setting service properties. 
Account SAS also allows you to delegate access to read, write, and delete operations on 
blob containers, file shares, tables, and queues that are not possible with a service SAS.
SAS comes in the following two forms:
 ■ Ad hoc SAS An ad hoc SAS includes the start time, expiry time, and resource  
permissions within the SAS URI. All SAS types can be ad hoc SAS.
 ■ Service SAS with stored access policy  Stored access policies are configured on 
resource containers, which include blob containers, tables, queues, or file shares. A ser -
vice SAS with stored access policies inheritss the start time, expiry time, and permissions 
configured for the stored access policy.
As is the case with storage account access keys, if an SAS is leaked, anyone who has access 
to the SAS has access to the storage resources to which the SAS mediates access. Application 
developers should also remember that SASes periodically expire, and if the application is not 
configured to automatically obtain a new SAS, the application will lose access to the storage 
resources to which the SAS mediates. 
Microsoft’s best practices for using SAS include the following:
 ■ Use user delegation SAS when possible  This type of SAS has the best security 
because it is secured through a user’s Entra ID credentials, meaning account keys will 
not be stored with the application code.
 ■ Be ready to revoke an SAS when necessary  If you determine that an SAS has been 
compromised, ensure that you can quickly revoke the SAS and replace it with one that is 
not compromised. 
 ■ Configure stored access policies for service SAS  An advantage of stored access 
policies is that you can revoke permissions for a service SAS without regenerating stor-
age account access keys. 
 ■ Configure short expiration times for ad-hoc SAS  If an ad hoc SAS is compromised, 
the short expiration time will ensure that the compromised SAS isn’t valid for a long 
time.
 ■ If necessary, ensure clients renew SAS  If clients regularly make requests to storage 
using SAS, configure the application so that the client can request SAS renewal before 
the SAS expires.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.6: Configure delegated access   CHAPTER 4 237
 
Create user delegation SAS
To create a user delegation SAS for a storage container using PowerShell, first, create a storage 
context object by substituting the appropriate values into the following PowerShell code:
$ctx = New-AzStorageContext -StorageAccountName <storage-account> -UseConnectedAccount
Create a user delegation SAS token by substituting the appropriate values in the following 
PowerShell code:
New-AzStorageContainerSASToken -Context $ctx `
    -Name <container> `
    -Permission racwdl `
    -ExpiryTime <date-time>
To create a user delegation SAS for a blob, substitute the appropriate values in the following 
PowerShell code:
New-AzStorageBlobSASToken -Context $ctx `
    -Container <container> `
    -Blob <blob> `
    -Permission racwd `
    -ExpiryTime <date-time>
    -FullUri
You can revoke a user delegation SAS using the Revoke-AzStorageAccountUserDelegationKeys 
command. For example, use the following PowerShell code, substituting the appropriate values 
where necessary:
Revoke-AzStorageAccountUserDelegationKeys -ResourceGroupName <resource-group> `
    -StorageAccountName <storage-account>
To create a user delegation SAS for a storage container using Azure CLI, run the following 
Azure CLI command, substituting the appropriate values where necessary:
az storage container generate-sas \
    --account-name <storage-account> \
    --name <container> \
    --permissions acdlrw \
    --expiry <date-time> \
    --auth-mode login \
    --as-user
To create a user delegation SAS for a blob using Azure CLI, run the following Azure CLI  
command, substituting the appropriate values where necessary:
az storage blob generate-sas \
    --account-name <storage-account> \
    --container-name <container> \
    --name <blob> \

azure/storage/common/storage-sas-overview .

    --permissions acdrw \
    --expiry <date-time> \
    --auth-mode login \
    --as-user
    --full-uri
To revoke a user delegation SAS using Azure CLI, run the following command, substituting 
the appropriate values where necessary:
az storage account revoke-delegation-keys \

It is important to note that because Azure Storage caches user delegation keys and Azure 
role assignments, the revocation process might not occur immediately.
 
Create an account SAS
The first step when creating an account SAS is to create an account SAS URI. The account SAS 
URI includes the storage resource URI to which the SAS provides access and the SAS token. SAS 
tokens are special query strings that include the data used to authorize resource requests and 
determine the service, resource, and access permissions. SAS tokens also include the period for 
which the signature will be valid. 
Table 4-2 lists the required and optional parameters for the SAS token:
TABLE 4-2 SAS token parameters.
SAS Query Parameter Description
Api-version Optional  Allows you to specify the storage service version to use when  
executing the request.
SignedVersion (sv) Required  Specify the signed storage service version to authorize requests. 
Must be configured to 2015-04-05 or later.
SignedServices (ss) Required  Allows you to specify the services accessible with the account SAS. 
Options include
 ■ Blob
 ■ Queue
 ■ Table
 ■ File
SignedResourceTypes 
(srt)
Required  Allows you to specify which resource types the SAS provides  
access to
 ■ Service  Access to service-level APIs.
 ■ Container  Access to container-level APIs.
 ■ Object Access to object-level APIs.
MORE INFO CREATE A USER DELEGATION SAS
You can learn more about creating a user delegation SAS at https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/
rest/api/storageservices/create-user-delegation-sas .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 4.6: Configure delegated access   CHAPTER 4 239
SAS Query Parameter Description
SignedPermission (sp) Required  Permissions for the account SAS. Permissions include
 ■ Read Valid for all resource types.
 ■ Write Valid for all resource types.
 ■ Delete Valid for container and object resource types, not including 
queue messages.
 ■ List Valid for service and container resource types.
 ■ Add Valid for queue messages, table entities, and append blobs.
 ■ Create Valid for blobs and files.
 ■ Update Valid for queue messages and table entities.
 ■ Process Only valid for queue messages.
SignedStart (st) Optional  The time the SAS becomes valid.
SignedExpiry (se) Required  The time the SAS becomes invalid.
SignedIP (sip) Optional  Allows you to specify an allowed range of IP addresses.
SignedProtocol (spr) Optional  Determines which protocols can be used for requests made with the 
account SAS. Options are both HTTPS and HTTP or HTTPS only. 
Signature (sig) Required  Used to authorize the request made with the SAS. Signatures are 
hash-based message authentication codes calculated over the signed string and 
the storage account access key using the SHA256 algorithm. This signature is 
then encoded using Base64 encoding. 
To construct the signature string, you need to encode the string as UTF-8 that you want 
to sign from the fields included in the request and compute the signature using the HMAC-
SHA256 algorithm. 
 
Stored Access Policies
Stored access policies consist of an SAS’s start time, expiry time, and permissions. Each param-
eter can be specified on the signature URI rather than in a stored access policy. You can also 
specify all these parameters on the stored access policy or combine the two. It is important to 
note that it is impossible to specify the same parameter on the SAS token and the stored access 
policy without problems.
Azure allows you to set a maximum of five concurrent access policies on individual con -
tainers, tables, queues, or shares. To create or modify a stored access policy, you need to call 
the 
Set ACL operation for the resource you want to protect with the request body of the call 
that lists the terms of the access policy. Following is a template you can use for the request 
MORE INFO CREATE AN ACCOUNT SAS
You can learn more about creating an account SAS at https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/rest/
api/storageservices/create-account-sas .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 4  Secure Azure Storage240
body where you substitute your own start time, expiry time, abbreviated permission list, and a 
unique signed identifier of your choosing:
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>  
<SignedIdentifiers>  
  <SignedIdentifier>
    <Id>unique-64-char-value</Id>  
    <AccessPolicy>  
      <Start>start-time</Start>  
      <Expiry>expiry-time</Expiry>  
      <Permission>abbreviated-permission-list</Permission>  
    </AccessPolicy>  
  </SignedIdentifier>  
</SignedIdentifiers>
To change the existing stored access policy parameters, call the access control list opera-
tion for the resource type and specify new parameters while ensuring that the unique ID field 
remains the same. Call the Set ACL operation with an empty request policy to remove all access 
policies from a storage resource.
 
EXAM TIP
Remember that an account or user delegation SAS will always be an ad-hoc SAS. You can’t 
use stored access policies for the account or user delegation SAS types.

review their storage configuration and access controls. The auditing firm has requested access 
to the company’s table storage resources to assess the security settings and identify any poten -
tial vulnerabilities. Additionally, some of Contoso’s clients have reported issues accessing the

company’s Azure file shares using the SMB protocol. With this information in mind, answer the

This section contains the solution to the thought experiment. Each answer explains why the

1.
 A storage account will be flagged as Allows Public Access if the storage account with

Chapter summary
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Storage helps detect unusual and malicious attempts to access 
Azure Storage accounts through malware scanning and sensitive data threat detection.
 ■ Azure Storage account access is controlled using Azure RBAC roles assigned at various 
scopes like the storage account, resource group, or subscription level.
 ■ Storage Service Encryption automatically encrypts data stored in Azure using 256-bit AES 
encryption and supports customer-managed and Microsoft-managed encryption keys.
 ■ Storage account access keys provide full access to a storage account and should be 
carefully managed and rotated regularly.
 ■ Azure AD authentication can be enabled for Azure file shares to allow access using  
AD user credentials instead of storage account keys.
 ■ Shared Access Signatures (SAS) provide granular, time-limited access to storage 
resources without sharing the full storage account keys.
 ■ Stored access policies allow centrally managing the start time, expiry time, and  
permissions for service-level shared access signatures.

CHAPTER 5
Secure Azure SQL
This chapter deals with the steps you can take to secure Azure SQL database instances.  
To master this objective, you’ll need to understand how to configure database authentica -
tion, the options for database auditing, the benefits of Microsoft Defender for SQL, how to 
configure database encryption, and how to enable Azure SQL Database Always Encrypted on 
specific database table columns.
Skills covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 5.1: Azure SQL server authentication
 ■ Skill 5.2: Auditing Azure SQL
 ■ Skill 5.3: Implement database encryption and masking
 ■ Skill 5.4: Database governance
 ■ Skill 5.5: Microsoft Defender for SQL
 ■ Skill 5.6: Azure SQL Firewall
Skill 5.1: Azure SQL server authentication
When configuring authentication for Azure SQL server instances, you can choose between 
traditional SQL-hosted authentication, Entra authentication, or a mix of both. Even when 
using Entra authentication, it is necessary to understand traditional SQL Server identity  
concepts such as logins and users. 
Azure SQL authentication and authorization
When you create an Azure SQL database server instance, you can configure the following 
authentication options, shown in Figure 5-1.
 ■ Use Microsoft Entra-Only Authentication
 ■ Use Both SQL and Microsoft Entra Authentication 
 ■ Use SQL Authentication
243
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL244
FIGURE 5-1 Server Admin Login
SQL authentication allows a user to submit a user account name and password when creat-
ing a connection. Account passwords are stored in the Azure SQL instance’s master database 
for user accounts linked to logins. Passwords are stored in the database associated with the 
user accounts if not associated with a login. The difference between a login and a user is as 
follows:
 ■ Login A login is an individual account in the master database. User accounts in one 
or more user databases associated with the Azure SQL instance can be linked to a 
login. Credential information for the user account is stored with a login in the master 
database.
 ■ User Account A user account is an individual account in a user database. User 
accounts might or might not be linked to a login. If the user account is not linked to a 
login, credential information is stored with the user account in the user database.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.1: Azure SQL server authentication   CHAPTER 5 245
When Entra authentication is used, a connecting user submits a user account name, and 
credentials are validated against Microsoft Entra ID before they are granted or denied access to 
Azure SQL.
Azure SQL administrators
This administrative account granted full administrative permissions on all databases hosted off 
the Azure SQL instance as a server-level principal. This login is granted all possible permissions 
on the Azure SQL instance. In line with the least-privilege principle, this account should not be 
used except to delegate more constrained permissions to other accounts that will be utilized to 
perform typical interactions with the instance or the databases it hosts. 
A separate user account called 
dbo is created for the administrator login for each user data-
base. The dbo user has all database permissions and is mapped to the db_owner database role. 
You can determine the identity of the administrator account for an Azure SQL database on the 
database Properties  page in the Azure portal, as shown in Figure 5-2.
FIGURE 5-2 Server Admin login
The admin login identifier cannot be changed once the database is created. You can 
reset the password of this account by selecting the Azure SQL server in the Azure portal and 
selecting Reset Password  from the Overview  page, as shown in Figure 5-3. This changes the 
administrator account password on the Azure SQL instance, not the password of any account  
in Entra.

You can configure conditional access for Azure SQL. For example, you might want to require a 
user to perform multi-factor authentication before being able to connect to a database. Condi-
