search for Azure SQL Database . The first-party application Azure SQL Database is  
registered in a tenant when the first Azure SQL instance is created.
3. Select Azure SQL Database  and then choose Conditional Access  under Security, as 
shown in Figure 5-6.
4. On the Conditional Access page, select New Policy . This opens the New Conditional 
Access Policy page, as shown in Figure 5-7.

Skill 5.1: Azure SQL server authentication   CHAPTER 5 251
Contained database users
Contained databases are isolated from other databases and from the hosting Azure SQL 
instance. User accounts for contained databases are stored in each database. Contained  
databases support both SQL Server authentication and Entra ID authentication. Contained 
databases can be moved between Azure SQL instances 
You need to enable the 
contained setting on Azure SQL instances to leverage contained 
database users. You can do this by running the following Transact-SQL code:
sp_configure 'contained database authentication', 1;  
GO  
RECONFIGURE;  
GO
Contained databases have the following limitations:
 ■ The Azure SQL Database server admin account can never be configured as a contained 
database user.
 ■ The name of a contained database user cannot be the same as the Azure SQL server 
admin account name.
 ■ Identities of contained database users are confined to the database. If you want to allow 
access to multiple databases while using contained users, you must create separate 
accounts in each user database.
You should limit granting the 
ALTER ANY USER permission to contained database users. 
Doing this allows users with this permission to provide access to the database to other users. If 
you are using contained databases, periodically audit users in the database for this permission.
Contained database users can access other databases hosted on the same Azure SQL 
instance if other databases hosted on the instance have enabled the guest account. If you want 
to ensure that users remain partitioned in environments with multiple contained databases on 
the same Azure SQL instance, ensure that you have disabled the guest account. You can disable 
a guest account by executing the following Transact-SQL code from within the target database:
REVOKE CONNECT FROM GUEST
 
EXAM TIP
Remember the difference between SQL authentication and Entra authentication and that 
Azure SQL can use both at the same time.

relational-databases/security/contained-database-users-making-your-database-portable .

Skill 5.2: Auditing Azure SQL
Auditing allows you to track database events, such as adding or dropping tables. Audit logs for 
Azure SQL databases can be stored in an Azure Storage account, Log Analytics workspace, or 
Event Hubs. 
Azure SQL auditing
Auditing for Azure SQL can be defined at both the server and database levels. The differences 
are as follows:
 ■ Configuring a server policy will apply to all existing and newly created databases on the 
Azure SQL server instance.
 ■ When server auditing is enabled at the instance level, it will always apply to the 
database.
 ■ Enabling auditing on individual Azure SQL databases will not override any server  
auditing settings; both audits will exist in parallel.
 ■ Microsoft recommends against enabling both server auditing and database blob  
auditing unless you want to use a different storage account, retention period, or Log 
Analytics Workspace for a specific database or if you want to audit a separate set of 
event types of categories for a specific database. 
Perform the following steps to enable auditing for an SQL instance:
1.
 In the Azure portal, open the Azure SQL instance on which you want to configure 
auditing.
2.
 Under the Security  node, select Auditing , as shown in Figure 5-9.
FIGURE 5-9 Auditing in an Azure SQL Server’s properties page
3. Set the Auditing slider to On, as shown in Figure 5-10. Specify the audit log destination. 
You can choose between Storage , Log Analytics , or Event Hub. Click Save. 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.2: Auditing Azure SQL   CHAPTER 5 253
FIGURE 5-10 Azure SQL auditing settings
You can configure audit logs to be written to Azure Storage accounts, Event Hubs, and Log 
Analytics workspaces. (Azure Monitor logs can consume Log Analytics workspaces.) You can 
choose to have data written to multiple locations should you so choose. When auditing to a 
storage destination, the retention period is unlimited. You can modify retention settings to 
keep audit logs for a shorter amount of time. Figure 5-11 shows the Retention (Days)  setting 
configured to 14 days.

FIGURE 5-11 Auditing storage retention
You can choose between a managed identity and a storage access key when configuring 
authentication for the storage account used for auditing Azure SQL databases. When you 
configure auditing to use a managed identity, Entra will manage the identity used to authorize 
the necessary access to the storage account. Using managed identity is more secure than using 
storage access keys because when you use storage access keys, you must regularly rotate the 
keys to keep them secure. If a storage account key leaks, untrusted third parties may get access 
to the storage account.

Skill 5.3: Implement database encryption and masking
Encryption allows you to encipher data stored in a database so that if a nefarious third party 
got access to the database files, they wouldn’t be able to access the contents of those files 
because that content would be encrypted. Masking allows you to configure Azure SQL so that 
only a limited amount of sensitive data, such as the last four digits of a credit card number, is  
returned by a query.
Azure SQL database encryption
Transparent data encryption (TDE) allows you to protect Azure SQL databases by encrypting 
data at rest. When you enable TDS, the databases, associated backups, and transaction log files 
are automatically encrypted and decrypted as necessary. TDE is enabled by default for all new 
Azure SQL Databases. TDE is configured at the server level and is inherited by all databases 
hosted on the Azure SQL Server instance. 
You cannot use TDE to encrypt the master database as this database stores objects that are 
used to perform TDE operations on user databases. The tempdb database is always encrypted 
when TDE is enabled. Secure best practice is to avoid storing sensitive data in any system database.
MORE INFO A UDITING FOR AZURE SQL DATABASE
You can learn more about audit logs and reports for Azure SQL Database at https://  
learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/auditing-analyze-audit-logs.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL256
Azure SQL TDE has a database encryption key (DEK) protected by a built-in server certificate 
unique to each Azure SQL instance and leverages the AES 256 encryption algorithm. Microsoft 
automatically rotates these security certificates.
Customer-managed TDE, also known as Bring Your Own Key (BYOK), is supported in Azure 
SQL. When you configure BYOK, the TDE protection key is stored within Azure Key Vault. When 
you configure BYOK, you configure an Azure Key Vault with permissions so that the Azure SQL 
instance can interact with the Key Vault to retrieve the key. The database will be inaccessible 
if the Key Vault is removed or the Azure SQL instance loses permissions to the Key Vault in a 
BYOK scenario.
You can verify that TDE is enabled for an Azure SQL instance by selecting the Transparent 
Data Encryption  section of a database server instance’s properties page in the Azure portal, 
as shown in Figure 5-14.
FIGURE 5-14 Transparent Data Encryption
If you want to switch to a customer-managed key for an Azure SQL instance, you should 
first create and configure an Azure Key Vault in the same region as the Azure SQL instance. You 
can then use the portal to create a key in the Key Vault and configure the Azure SQL instance 
with the appropriate permissions. To switch a database to a customer-managed key, perform 
the following steps:
1.
 On the Transparent Data Encryption  page of the Azure SQL database instance, select 
Customer Managed Key .
2. The Key Selection Method  setting offers two choices: Choose Enter A Key Identifier 
or Select A Key and then click the Change Key link, as shown in Figure 5-15.
3. On the Select Key From Azure Key Vault page, select the subscription; now, the Key 
Vault will host the key.
4.
 If no suitable key is present in the Key Vault, you can click Create New , which will allow 
you to create a key, as shown in Figure 5-16.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.3: Implement database encryption and masking   CHAPTER 5 257
FIGURE 5-15  Customer-Managed Key
FIGURE 5-16 Create a key for BYOK
5. On the Select Key From Azure Key Vault page, select the key Version , as shown in 
Figure 5-17. If you’ve just created the key, only the most recent version will be available.

Implement Azure SQL Database Always Encrypted
Always Encrypted allows you to protect specific types of sensitive data with a known, recogniz -
able pattern, such as a passport, tax file identification, and credit card numbers. When Always 
Encrypted is enabled, clients interacting with the database server will encrypt the sensitive data 
inside the client applications and will not forward the encryption keys used to decrypt that 
data to the database server that will store that data. This ensures that administrators who man-
age Azure SQL servers cannot view sensitive data protected by Always Encrypted. 
SQL Always Encrypted differs from TDE in scope, visibility, and key management. TDE 
encrypts entire databases at rest, and is transparent to users, using database-managed keys. 
SQL Always Encrypted targets specific columns, keeping data encrypted in memory and during 
processing, with keys managed externally
Deterministic or randomized encryption
Always Encrypted supports two forms of encryption: deterministic and randomized:
 ■ Deterministic encryption  When you use deterministic encryption, the same 
encrypted value will always be generated for the same plain text value, though each 
database will be unique. Implementing deterministic encryption will allow you to 
perform point lookups, equality joins, grouping, and indexing on encrypted columns. 
It may, however, allow unauthorized users to guess information about encrypted values 
by looking for patterns in encrypted columns. This is especially true if there is a small  
set of possible values. Deterministic encryption requires that the column collation is 
configured with a binary2 sort order for character columns.
MORE INFO A ZURE SQL DATABASE ENCRYPTION
You can learn more about Azure SQL Database encryption at https://learn.microsoft.com/ sql/
relational-databases/security/encryption/sql-server-encryption
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.3: Implement database encryption and masking   CHAPTER 5 259
 ■ Randomized encryption  When configuring randomized encryption, data is 
encrypted less predictably. While randomized encryption is more secure than  
deterministic encryption, enabling randomized encryption prevents searching,  
grouping, indexing, and performing joins on encrypted columns.
In general, you should plan to use deterministic encryption if columns will be used for 
searching or where you will be grouping parameters, such as searching for a specific passport 
number. The client will be able to perform the hash of the query value and then locate values 
within the database that match that encrypted hash. You should use randomized encryption 
if your database has information that isn’t grouped with other records and isn’t used to join 
tables, such as medical notes.
Configuring Always Encrypted
Configuring Always Encrypted is an activity that requires the use of client-side tools. You can’t 
use Transact SQL statements to configure Always Encrypted; instead, you must configure 
Always Encrypted using SQL Server Management Studio or PowerShell. You must perform the 
following tasks when configuring Always Encrypted:
 ■ Provisioning column master keys, column encryption keys, and encrypted column 
encryption keys with corresponding column master keys
 ■ Creating key metadata in the database
 ■ Creating new tables with encrypted columns
 ■ Encrypting existing data in selected database columns
Always Encrypted is not supported for columns that have the following characteristics:
 ■ Columns with xml, timestamp/rowversion, image, ntext, text, sql_variant, hierarchyid, 
geography, geometry, alias, and types or user-defined types
 ■ FILESTREAM columns
 ■ Columns with the IDENTITY property
 ■ Columns with ROWGUIDCOL property
 ■ String columns with non-bin2 collections
 ■ Columns that are keys for clustered and nonclustered indexes (if you are using  
randomized encryption)
 ■ Columns that are keys for full-text indexes (if you are using randomized encryption)
 ■ Computed columns
 ■ Columns referenced by computed columns
 ■ Sparse columns set
 ■ Columns referenced by statistics (if you are using randomized encryption)
 ■ Columns using alias types
 ■ Partitioning of columns
 ■ Columns with default constraints 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL260
 ■ Columns referenced by unique constraints (if you are using randomized encryption)
 ■ Primary key columns (if you are using randomized encryption)
 ■ Referencing columns in foreign key constraints
 ■ Columns referenced by check constraints
 ■ Columns tracked using change data capture
 ■ Primary key columns on tables that have change tracking enabled
 ■ Columns masked using Dynamic Data Masking
 ■ Columns in Stretch Database Tables
Perform the following steps to configure Always Encrypted on an Azure SQL database using 
SQL Server Management Studio:
1. Connect to the database that hosts the tables with columns you want to encrypt using 
Object Explorer in SQL Server Management Studio. If the database does not already 
exist, you can create the database and then create the tables that you will configure to 
use Always Encrypted.
2.
 Right-click the database and select Tasks  > Encrypt Columns  to open the Always 
Encrypted Wizard. Click Next .
3. On the Column Selection  page, expand the database tables, and then select the  
columns you want to encrypt.
4. For each column selected, you will need to set the Encryption Type  attribute to  
Deterministic  or Randomized.
5. For each column selected, you will need to choose an Encryption Key . If you do not 
already have an encryption key, you can have one automatically generated.
6. On the Master Key Configuration page, choose a location to store the key. You will 
then need to select a master key source.
7.
 On the Validation page, select whether you want to run the script immediately or use a 
PowerShell script later.
8. On the Summary  page, review the selected option and click Finish .
 
Dynamic masking
Dynamic masking allows you to configure SQL Server to hide sensitive data stored in the data -
base from users who don’t have the appropriate privileges. For example, a query run against 
a table in a database that stores credit card information by an unprivileged user might only 
MORE INFO A ZURE SQL DATABASE ALWAYS ENCRYPTED

reveal the final four digits of the credit card, with the rest of the credit card number hidden 
through dynamic masking.
Dynamic data masking can be configured through the policies available under Security  in 
the SQL Database Configuration pane. Currently, dynamic data masking cannot be config -
ured in the Azure portal for an SQL Managed Instance. Dynamic data masking policies have 
the following elements:
 ■ SQL users excluded from masking  This is the set of SQL users or Entra identities who 
can retrieve unmasked data when performing SQL queries. Users with administrative 
privileges on the database will always be able to view complete data without applying 
masks.
 ■ Masking rules  These are the rules that determine which fields will be masked and 
how the masks will be applied. You can identify fields using database schema, table, and 
column names.
 ■ Masking functions  These are a set of functions that manage data display for  
different scenarios.
The masking functions available for Azure SQL are listed in Table 5-1.
TABLE 5-1 Masking Functions
Masking Function Masking Logic
Default  ■ Use XXX (or fewer Xs if the size of the field is less than four characters) for string 
data types (nchar, ntext, nvarchar).
 ■ Use a zero value for numeric data types ( bigint, bit, decimal, int, money, 
numeric, smallint, smallmoney, tinyint, float, and real).
 ■ Use 01-01-1990 for date/time data types (date, datetime2, datetime,  
datetimeoffset, smalldate, time).
 ■ The default value of the current type is used for an SQL variant.
 ■ <masked/> document is used for SQL.
 ■ Use an empty value for special data types ( timestamp table, hierarchyid, 
GUID, binary, image, varbinary special types)
Credit card Masking method that exposes only the final four digits of a credit card and substitutes 
a constant string (such as XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-1234) for the masked parts of the result.
Email This masking method displays only the first letter of an email address and replaces the 
email domain with 
XXX.com. 
Random number Masks data using random numbers.
Custom text Exposes the first and last characters of the data and substitutes a custom string in the 
middle in the form 
prefix[padding]suffix.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL262
Skill 5.4: Database governance
This objective deals with discovering and managing access to sensitive data that may be stored 
in your organization’s Azure SQL instances. 
Data discovery and classification
Data discovery and classification is a capability built into Azure SQL Database that can discover, 
classify, label, and report on sensitive data stored within Azure SQL databases. Data discovery 
and classification includes the following capabilities:
 ■ Discovery and recommendations  The classification engine examines databases 
and identifies columns in tables that contain potentially sensitive data based on the 
patterns within that data. For example, patterns that might indicate passport numbers, 
credit card details, tax identification numbers, and other known sensitive data patterns. 
Classification includes two metadata attributes: Labels and Information types. A label 
defines the sensitivity level of the data in the column, and the information type explains 
the type of data stored in the column. 
 ■ Labeling Apply persistent sensitivity-classification labels to columns using metadata 
attributes in the SQL Server database engine. You can use these metadata attributes and 
classification labels when performing sensitivity-based audits.
 ■ Query result-set sensitivity  Determine the sensitivity of a query result in real time 
and record it for auditing purposes.
 ■ Visibility  Determine the classification state of a database in the Azure portal or 
export a report in Excel format for compliance and auditing purposes.
 
Information protection policies
Azure SQL provides two separate information protection policies that you can use when per-
forming data classification. A user needs to have the Security Admin permission to configure 
the information protection policy. These policies are as follows:
 ■ SQL Information Protection Policy  A built-in set of sensitivity labels, information 
types, and discovery logic native to the Azure SQL Server. 
 ■ Microsoft Purview Information Protection Policy  Allows you to create and man-
age Microsoft Purview Information Protection sensitivity labels in the Microsoft Purview 
compliance portal. This allows you to manage classification across all data assets, not 
just those hosted in Azure SQL.
MORE INFO D ATA DISCOVERY AND CLASSIFICATION.
You can learn more about data discovery and classification at https://learn.microsoft.com/ 
azure/azure-sql/database/data-discovery-and-classification-overview.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.4: Database governance   CHAPTER 5 263
SQL information protection policy
The SQL information protection policy is an existing technology that allows you to manage all 
information classification and discovery for Azure SQL Server. You are able to use protection 
labels in the default policy file or configure your own taxonomy. It is also possible to define a 
set and ranking of classifications for your organization’s environment. 
To classify a database using SQL Information Protection policy mode, perform the following 
steps:
1.
 In the Azure portal, navigate to the Azure SQL database for which you want to configure 
SQL Information Protection.
2.
 Open the database’s Data Discovery & Classification page. The Overview  tab will 
provide a summary of the classification state of the database. This summary includes 
a list of classified columns you can filter to view schema parts, information types, and 
labels.
3.
 To classify columns, select the Classification  tab. The classification engine will examine 
the database for columns with information that appears to be sensitive and will provide 
a set of recommended column classifications.
4.
 You can review the classifications, select the ones you want to accept, and then apply 
the recommendations.
5.
 You can manually classify columns by selecting Add Classification , selecting the 
schema, table, and column you want to classify, and choosing the appropriate  
information type and sensitivity label.
Microsoft Purview Information Policy
Microsoft Purview Information Protection Policies allow you to uniformly classify sensitive data 
across many data sources connected to Microsoft’s cloud. You manage sensitivity labels in the 
Microsoft Purview compliance portal.
To classify a database using Microsoft Purview Information Protection policy mode,  
perform the following steps:
1.
 In the Azure portal, navigate to the Azure SQL database for which you want to  
configure Microsoft Purview Information Protection.
2.
 Open the database’s Data Discovery & Classification page. On the Overview  tab, 
select Configure .
3. Select Microsoft Information Protection Policy on the Information Protection  
page shown in Figure 5-18 and choose Save.
4. On the Classification  tab, you will now see the sensitivity labels used by Purview.

be trying to attack your organization’s Azure SQL databases. When you enable Microsoft

SQL Vulnerability Assessment
Vulnerability assessment is an element of Defender for Azure SQL, which allows you to  
discover, track, and remediate likely vulnerabilities in your Azure SQL deployment. SQL  
vulnerability assessment can flag the following issues and provide remediation steps:
 ■ Deviations from best practice
 ■ Database server and database misconfigurations
 ■ Overly generous permissions
 ■ Unprotected sensitive data
You can customize vulnerability assessments based on the following categories:
 ■ Permission configurations
 ■ Feature configurations
 ■ Database settings
When setting up a vulnerability assessment, you choose between the following options 
based on when baseline and scan result data will be stored. These options are:
 ■ Express configuration  Perform a vulnerability assessment without managing an 
Azure Storage account to store scan and baseline data. The express configuration can 
be used at the subscription and server scope, not the individual database scope. Scan-
ning is always active, and the schedule cannot be configured. Email notifications occur 
via Logic Apps. Scans can be exported to Azure Resource Graph. Baseline settings can 
use batch assessments, single rule assessments, and assessments configured by the 
results of the most recent scan.
 ■ Classic configuration  Requires you to manage a storage account that stores baseline 
and scan result data. The classic configuration can be used at the subscription, server, 
and database levels. You can enable or disable scanning but cannot configure a sched -
ule. Baseline settings use a single rule, and email notifications can occur through inter -
nal scheduler or logic apps. You can export data in Excel format or to Azure Resource 
Graph.
Vulnerability assessment is automatically enabled when you enable Defender for Azure SQL. 
 
Azure SQL security baseline
The Azure SQL security baseline is developed from guidance published in the Microsoft Cloud 
security benchmark for Azure SQL. You can monitor this baseline and its recommendations 
through Microsoft Defender for Cloud. Azure policy definitions are listed in the Regulatory 
Compliance section of the Microsoft Defender for Cloud page in the Azure portal, as shown in 
Figure 5-28.

azure/defender-for-cloud/sql-azure-vulnerability-assessment-overview .

Skill 5.6: Azure SQL Firewall
Securing Azure SQL isn’t simply a matter of restricting access on the identity plane and ensur-
ing that data is classified, encrypted, and masked. One of the oldest methods of securing 
data, restricting access based on network traffic properties, also plays a part. Firewalls are 
fairly straightforward when deploying SQL on a virtual machine or bare metal server. Even 
though Azure SQL follows the same basic principles, there are several differences to consider 
when understanding firewall rules when a SQL instance runs as a Platform as a Service (PaaS) 
offering. 
Understanding Azure SQL firewalls
Azure SQL firewall rules allow you to block or allow traffic using the IP address of the client 
attempting to interact with the Azure SQL instance or database. Azure SQL uses firewalls at two 
different levels, the Azure SQL instance level and the Azure SQL database-level. Network traffic 
is processed by firewall rules at the database-level before being processed by the firewall rules 
at the server-level.
You should configure firewall rules at the database level as a priority because this allows you 
to move databases between Azure SQL instances whilst ensuring the same IP address restric-
tions and access rules remain in force. You should configure firewall rules at the server-level 
MORE INFO A ZURE SQL SECURITY BASELINE
You can learn more about Azure SQL security baseline at https://learn.microsoft.com/security/
benchmark/azure/baselines/azure-sql-security-baseline .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL272
when you have to apply the same set of firewall rules to a large number of databases and don’t 
expect to move databases to other Azure SQL instances.
Firewall rules function in the following way when a client attempts to access an Azure SQL 
database from the Internet.
1. The client’s IP address is checked against the database-level IP firewall rules on the  
database that the client connection is querying.
2.
 If the address is within the range allowed by the database-level IP firewall rule, the  
connection is granted to the database.
3.
 If the address is not within the allowed range of addresses, a check occurs against the 
server-level firewall rules. If the address is within the range granted access by the  
server-level IP firewall rules, the connection is allowed.
4.
 The connection is denied if the IP address isn’t within the ranges allowed by either the 
database-level or server-level IP firewall rules.
To allow connections to an Azure SQL instance from a host within Azure, you must enable 
Azure connections. You do this by creating a firewall rule starting with and ending with the IP 
address 
0.0.0.0. You can also do this by enabling the Allow Azure Services And Resources 
To Access This Server option on the Firewalls And Virtual Networks Settings  page shown 
in Figure 5-29, which is . disabled by default.
FIGURE 5-29 Allow Azure Services And Resources To Access This Server
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 5.6: Azure SQL Firewall   CHAPTER 5 273
Server-level firewall rules
Server-level rules control access for database administrators and access to all databases man-
aged by the Azure SQL database server instance. Server-level IP firewall rules are stored in 
the Azure SQL database server instance’s master database. You can create a maximum of 256 
server-level IP firewall rules. You can also use server-level rules when a database server hosts 
several databases with the same network access restriction requirements. The Allow Azure 
Services and resources to access this server rule is only counted as one of the  
256 allowed server-level IP firewall rules.
To create a SQL server-level firewall rule, perform the following steps.
1.
 In the Azure portal, open the Azure SQL server instance for which you want to create a 
server-level firewall rule.
2.
 Under Security  in the side menu, select Networking.
3. On the Public Access page on the Networking  blade, choose Selected Networks ,  
as shown in Figure 5-30 and choose Save .
FIGURE 5-30 Enable firewall rules for selected networks.
4. Return to this page, and under Firewall Rules , choose Add A Firewall rule. On the Add 
A Firewall rule dialog shown in Figure 5-31, provide a Rule Name , Start IP, and End IP. 
Click OK.
FIGURE 5-31 Add A Firewall Rule
5. You can also choose the Add A Virtual Network Rule option on this page and select 
an existing Azure virtual network within a subscription to which you have access.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL274
You can use the Azure CLI commands in Table 5-2 to manage firewall rules:
TABLE 5-2 Azure CLI firewall commands
Command Description
az sql server firewall-rule create Create a server-level IP firewall rule
az sql server firewall-rule list Lists all server-level IP firewall rules
az sql server firewall-rule show Shows the details of a specific server-level IP firewall rule
az sql server firewall-rule update Modify a server-level IP firewall rule
az sql server firewall-rule delete Remove a server-level IP firewall rule
Database-level firewall rules
Database-level firewall rules are configured at the individual database-level and are processed 
before server-level firewall rules. You can only configure database-level firewall rules using 
Transact-SQL statements. You do not have to configure database-level firewall rules and can 
allow and deny access only at the server-level. You configure database-level firewall rules when 
you want to isolate users of one database from another. For example, you want to allow users 
from one set of IP addresses access to one database and users from another set of IP addresses 
access to another database. Remember that you can also isolate users based on identities 
stored in Entra and SQL logins. Before creating database-level firewall rules, you need to 
configure at least one server-level firewall rule. Database-level firewall rules have the following 
properties:
 ■ Each user database can have up to 256 database-level IP firewall rules.
 ■ Database-level firewall rules are processed before server-level firewall rules. 
 ■ If you configure an IP address range in a database-level IP firewall rule that is not within 
the range of the allowed ranges at the server-levels, only clients with IP addresses con-
figured in database-level rules will be able to access the database.
You use the following catalog views or stored procedures to manage database-level firewall 
rules:
 ■ sys.database_firewall_rules Lists the current database-level IP firewall rules.
 ■ sp_set_database_firewall_rule Create or update a database-level IP firewall rule.
 ■ sp_delete_database_firewall_rule Delete a database-level firewall rule.
 
MORE INFO A ZURE SQL FIREWALL RULES
You can learn more about Azure SQL firewall rules at https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/
azure-sql/database/firewall-configure.

Chapter summary   CHAPTER 5 275
Thought experiment
In this thought experiment, demonstrate your skills and knowledge of the topics covered in this 
chapter. You can find answers to this thought experiment in the next section.
Securing SQL at Tailwind Traders
Tailwind Traders is storing sensitive customer financial data, including credit card numbers, in 
an Azure SQL database. When verifying customer identity, the service desk needs to be able to 
query the database and not have the full credit card numbers; instead, they want just the last 
four digits. As part of hardening security, the database audit logs written to a storage account 
should avoid using storage keys for authentication.You also want to protect the database from 
common attacks such as data exfiltration and SQL injection.With this information in mind, 
answer the following questions. 
1.
 What technology would you implement to ensure that only the last four credit card 
digits could be returned to users on the service desk? 
2.
 What option should you configure to avoid using storage keys when configuring audit -
ing data to be written to the storage account? 
3. Which component of Defender for SQL can detect data exfiltration? 
Thought experiment answers
This section contains the solution to the thought experiment. Each answer explains why the 
answer is correct.
1.
 You would use dynamic masking to ensure that queries from the service desk only 
returned the last four digits of a credit card.
2.
 Use managed identity to allow auditing data to be written to the storage account with-
out using storage account keys.
3.
 SQL Advanced Threat Protection can detect data exfiltration attacks.
Chapter summary
 ■ Azure SQL supports both SQL authentication and Microsoft Entra authentication. SQL 
authentication uses logins stored in the master database, while Entra authentication 
validates credentials against Entra ID.
 ■ Auditing for Azure SQL can be defined at both the server and database-levels. Audit logs 
can be stored in an Azure Storage account, Log Analytics workspace, or Event Hubs.
 ■ Transparent Data Encryption (TDE) encrypts data at rest for Azure SQL databases,  
backups, and transaction logs. Customers can manage their own TDE keys using Azure 
Key Vault (Bring Your Own Key).
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 5  Secure Azure SQL276
 ■ Dynamic Data Masking allows sensitive data to be hidden from users without appropri-
ate privileges. Different masking functions are available based on the data type and 
masking requirements.
 ■ Data Discovery & Classification enables discovering, classifying, labeling, and reporting 
sensitive data in Azure SQL databases. It uses built-in or custom information protection 
policies.
 ■ Microsoft Defender for SQL (formerly Advanced Threat Protection) detects unusual 
activities indicating potential attacks like SQL injection, brute force, and data exfiltra -
tion. It sends alerts to configured email addresses.
 ■ SQL Vulnerability Assessment, part of Defender for SQL, discovers, tracks, and helps 
remediate potential database vulnerabilities. It can be customized based on various 
categories and settings.
 ■ Azure SQL Firewall rules allow restricting access based on client IP address at the server 
and database-levels. Server-level rules apply to all databases, while database-level rules 
are specific to individual databases.
 ■ Server-level firewall rules are managed in the Azure portal or via Azure CLI commands. 
Database-level firewall rules are configured using T-SQL and take precedence over 
server-level rules.
 ■ Configuring secure Azure SQL involves various features like Entra authentication,  
auditing, encryption (TDE and Always Encrypted), data masking, data discovery and 
classification, Defender for SQL, vulnerability assessment, and firewall rules at the 
appropriate levels.

CHAPTER 6
Security governance
Governance helps ensure that the planned security controls and assurances are implemented 
consistently across the technical state and processes of the organization. Without gover-
nance, security assurances and controls often rapidly drift as other priorities cause them to 
be neglected, resulting in a degraded security posture and a false sense of security.
To manage governance practices for security in Azure, you will need to understand how 
to enforce security policies, manage cryptographic keys securely, and implement backup 
and recovery measures. These actions will drive security posture enhancement and overall 
compliance of your Azure environment.
Skill covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security
Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance  
for security
Management encompasses the essential tasks and procedures for upholding your business 
applications and the underlying resources. Azure offers a plethora of services and tools that 
synergize to deliver comprehensive management solutions. These resources extend beyond 
Azure, encompassing other cloud platforms and on-premises environments. Familiarizing 
oneself with the diverse array of tools and their collaborative functionalities is paramount to 
crafting a holistic management ecosystem. This section of the chapter covers the skills neces-
sary to plan, implement, and manage governance for security according to the Exam AZ-500 
outline.
Create, assign, and interpret security policies and 
initiatives in Azure Policy
Azure Policy allows you to create, assign, and manage a variety of policy definitions. Policy 
definitions can be compared with your current configuration, and any resources that do not 
meet the requirements of your policy can then be determined to be out of compliance. You 
can then focus on the out-of-compliance assets and bring them into compliance. 
A policy assignment is a policy definition within a specific scope . A scope might range 
from an Azure management group to a resource group. A management group enables you 
277
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance278
to manage access, policy, costs, and compliance across subscriptions. A scope refers to all 
the resource groups, subscriptions, or management groups to which the policy definition is 
assigned. Policy assignments are inherited by all child resources. 
An initiative definition is a collection of policy definitions tailored to achieve a singular 
overarching goal. Initiative definitions simplify managing and assigning policy definitions. They 
simplify policy definition assignments by grouping a set of policies into a single item called an 
initiative definition. Policy definitions have parameters, as shown in Figure 6-1.
FIGURE 6-1 Azure Policy components
You should use Azure Policy when specific rules need enforcement, while Azure Initiatives is 
more recommended for a single policy because it simplifies management. An initiative allows 
you to manage multiple policies as a cohesive unit. Example: Instead of handling 50 separate 
policies for ISO 27001 compliance, use an initiative that evaluates them all simultaneously.
You can use custom and built-in policy definitions within your custom initiative definition. 
If you choose to use built-in policies, they are still maintained by Microsoft, whereas custom 
policy definitions are not automatically updated. So, if you are using custom policies in a 
custom initiative, you need to establish a process that helps you keep track of backend changes 
related to the policies’ intent and update your custom policies accordingly.
A policy definition can have different effects (listed below) on the scope it is assigned to:
 ■ append The append mode adds fields to a resource when it is created or updated. For 
example, you can use append to add a list of allowed IP addresses to a storage resource. 
 ■ audit A policy definition in audit mode will report resources that are noncompliant 
with your definition settings. For example, if you have an internal agreement stating that 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security   CHAPTER 6 279
organizational resources are only deployed to Azure regions in Europe, you can use an 
audit policy to report resources deployed in a US region. 
 ■ auditifnotexists Similar to the audit mode,  auditifnotexists will report resources 
that do not have a particular configuration or setting. For example, you could use 
auditifnotexists if you want to see resources that do not have a particular tag 
configured. 
 ■ deployifnotexists (DINE) If you configure a definition in deployifnotexists (DINE) 
mode, it will make sure that, once you deploy a resource, a particular setting or configu -
ration is automatically remediated if it has not already been defined when configuring 
the resource to be deployed. For example, you can use a DINE policy to ensure that the 
Azure Monitoring Agent is installed on all VMs created within your Azure environment.
 ■ deny A definition that is configured in deny mode will prevent the deployment of 
resources that are noncompliant with a particular setting. In the audit mode example 
with the Azure regions, you can use a deny policy to not only audit but prevent the 
deployment of resources to a US region.
 ■ modify Finally, the modify mode is used to add, update, or remove properties or tags 
on a resource when it is created or updated. This effect is commonly used to update 
tags on resources. Also, 
modify allows you to remediate existing resources using reme-
diation tasks. 
In addition to these modes, the following effects are currently supported in a policy 
definition:
 ■ append append adds additional fields to the requested resource during creation or 
update. For example, you could use append to specify a list of allowed IPs during the 
storage creation.
 ■ disabled  used to effectively turn off the policy without deleting it. This means that 
the policy definition will still exist in the environment, but it will not be enforced, and no 
resources will be evaluated against it 
 ■ modify modify adds, updates, or removes properties or tags on a subscription or 
resource during creation or an update.
The first step to achieving governance in Azure is to ensure you leverage Azure Policy for 
policy enforcement. You can also enforce data residency and sovereignty using Azure Policy. 
For example, if you need to enforce that all new resources be created to use a specific region, 
you will use Azure Policy. As mentioned earlier in this chapter, from the centralized manage-
ment perspective, it’s always recommended that you assign a policy to a management group 
and move the subscriptions that you want to inherit that policy to that management group.
Many built-in roles grant permission to Azure Policy resources. You can use the Resource 
Policy Contributor role, which includes most Azure Policy operations. The Owner role has full 
rights to perform all actions, and both Contributor and Reader roles have access to all Azure 
Policy read operations. You can use the Contributor role to trigger resource remediation, but 
you can’t use it to create definitions or assignments.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance280
When enforcing policies, you need to ensure that your policy initiative uses the right type of 
effect. If the scenario requires that you avoid provisioning certain workloads if certain attri-
butes are not set, you should use the deny policy effect. The deny attribute prevents a resource 
request that doesn’t match defined standards through a policy definition and fails the request.
If your scenario requires changing parameters if they were not set during provision time, 
you should use DeployIfNotExists. For example, if a Contoso administrator wants to deploy 
Azure Network Watcher when creating a virtual network, the administrator should enforce 
the 
DeployIfNotExists effect for that policy. DeployIfNotExists runs about 15 minutes after a 
resource provider has handled a create or update resource request and has returned a success 
status code. When you configure a policy with this type of effect, you also create a remediation 
task, and the goal is to configure the resource with the parameter you want.
Another common scenario is to update tags on a resource during creation or update. For 
example, the Contoso administrator needs to update the cost center for all resources during 
the creation. For this scenario, you need to use the 
Modify effect. Just like the DeployIfNotExists 
effect, you also need to configure a remediation task to run the desired change. Remember 
that you must select the Create A Managed Identity option when creating this remediation 
task for both effects.
Follow the steps below to configure policy enforcement using Azure Policy:
1.

3. On the Policy page, click Assignments  under Authoring  in the left pane. Figure 6-2 
shows an example of the Assignments  page.
FIGURE 6-2 Policy Assignments page
4. Notice that you can assign an initiative or policy on this page. For this example, click the 
Assign Policy  button. The Assign Policy  page appears (see Figure 6-3).
5.
 On the Basics tab, you can select the Scope  in which this policy should be assigned. 
If your scenario requires centralized management, you can assign it to a management 
group. The default setting is subscription level, so if the scenario requires that you assign 
only to the subscription level, you can leave this setting as is.

Configure security settings by using Azure Blueprints
Similar to how a blueprint facilitates engineers or architects in delineating a project’s design 
parameters, Azure Blueprints empowers cloud architects and central IT teams to establish a 
consistent set of Azure resources that embody and comply with an organization’s standards, 
patterns, and needs. With Azure Blueprints, development teams can swiftly create and deploy 
new environments with confidence, ensuring adherence to organizational compliance through 
a suite of preconfigured components, including networking, thereby expediting development 
and delivery processes.
The Azure Blueprints service leverages the globally distributed Azure Cosmos DB for robust 
support. Blueprints objects are replicated across multiple Azure regions, ensuring minimal 
latency, maximum availability, and seamless access to your blueprint objects, irrespective of the 
region to which Azure Blueprints deploys your resources. Most deployment needs within Azure 
Blueprints can be met using an Azure Resource Manager template. However, it’s important to 
note that an Azure Resource Manager template is a standalone document not inherently inte-
grated into Azure; each template is stored locally, in source control, or in Templates (preview). 
While the template serves for deploying one or more Azure resources, once deployed, there’s 
no ongoing connection or dependency on the template.
A blueprint serves as a consolidated framework for assembling targeted collections of stan-
dards, patterns, and requirements pertaining to Azure cloud service implementation, security, 
and design. It facilitates consistency and compliance through reusable configurations. On the 
other hand, a policy operates on a default allow and explicit deny mechanism, concentrating 
on resource properties both during deployment and for existing resources. It reinforces cloud 
governance by ensuring that resources within a subscription align with specified requirements 
and standards. A visual representation of the blueprint’s components, also known as artifacts , 
is shown in Figure 6-6.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance284
Azure Blueprint
Resource groups Policy assignment
Azure Resource
Manager templates Role assignment
FIGURE 6-6  Blueprints components
When creating a blueprint definition, you’ll specify its storage location. Blueprints can be 
stored within a management group or subscription to which you have Contributor access. If the 
blueprint is stored in a management group, it becomes accessible for assignment to any child 
subscription associated with that management group. 
 
Deploy secure infrastructures by using a landing zone
An Azure Landing Zone is an environment to host Azure workloads pre-provisioned through 
code. It uses a set of defined cloud services and best practices to add foundational capability  
while providing cloud adoption teams with a well-managed environment to run their 
workloads. 
Everything starts with a subscription that can be considered a unit of management not only 
for new application development but also to accelerate the migration of existing applications. 
It is important to align the subscription with your business needs. Even if you have multiple 
subscriptions, landing zones should be provided to support existing and new workloads. 
When necessary, you may also aggregate multiple subscriptions into a single management 
group (MG), allowing you to easily scale while keeping centralized management for multiple 
subscriptions.
Azure Landing Zone’s conceptual architecture design presumes that a specific MG and sub -
scription hierarchy are available for all operations management subscriptions, which may not 
be aligned with your operating model. As your organization grows and continues to evolve, 
your operational model may change, leading to another resource migration into separate sub-
scriptions and technical migration challenges. There are two types of Azure landing zones:
 ■ Platform landing zone  A subscription that securely shares services such as identity, 
connectivity, and management to applications within application landing zones. Typi-
cally, centralizing these services enhances operational efficiency.
 ■ Application landing zone  A subscription designated for hosting an application. 
These landing zones are pre-provisioned via code, and policy controls are assigned to 
them through management groups.
TIP T o create a blueprint, visit https://aka.ms/AZ500Blueprint .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security   CHAPTER 6 285
 
The best approach to implementing Azure Landing Zones is to start by leveraging Azure 
Landing Zone accelerators, which use IaC as the deployment method. Through IaC, you have 
the flexibility to adjust and configure environments to align precisely with your requirements. 
To effectively implement IaC, your organization will need proficiency in Azure Resource 
Manager templates and GitHub. You can use the Azure Landing Zone accelerator via portal by 
accessing https://aka.ms/caf/ready/accelerator, which will redirect you to the Azure Landing 
Zone accelerator page, as shown in Figure 6-7.
FIGURE 6-7 Initial page of an Azure Landing Zone accelerator
The accelerator page has the following tabs to enable you to customize your deployment 
settings:
 ■ Deployment Settings  This page enables you to select the template you want to 
use for your deployment, select the cloud environment that you want to perform your 
deployment, and select the region that should be used during your deployment. 
 ■ Azure Core Setup  This page enables you to customize subscription options, deploy -
ment of classic resources, and virtual machine settings. Azure Landing Zones will create 
the management group hierarchy under the Tenant Root Group with the prefix  
provided here.
 ■ Platform Management, Security, And Governance  This page allows you to config-
ure Key Vault enforcement, Log Analytics deployment settings, and the Azure solutions 
enabled in the Log Analytics workspace.
TIP A zure Landing Zone accelerators such as Bicep and Terraform are infrastructure-as-code 
(IaC) implementations that can assist you with correctly deploying an Azure Landing Zone in a 
more guided manner.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance286
 ■ Network Topology And Connectivity  This page allows you to select the preferred 
network topology. 
 ■ Identity This page allows you to select the recommended policies that should be 
assigned to your identity management group.
 ■ Landing Zone Configuration  This page allows you to select which of the  
recommended policies you will assign to your landing zone management group.
When planning your landing zone, you should also use Azure Security Benchmark (ASB) 
best practices and Zero Trust (ZT) principles. ASB includes a collection of security recommen-
dations that can be used to help secure Azure workloads. ASB is usually utilized in the following 
scenarios:
 ■ New Azure deployments (greenfield)  When organizations are first adopting cloud 
computing and using Azure as their cloud platform and need to follow security best 
practices to ensure a secure deployment of their Azure workloads.
 ■ Improve security posture of existing workloads in Azure (brownfield)  When 
organizations already have workloads deployed in Azure and are looking to improve 
the security posture by prioritizing top risks and mitigations.
 ■ Cloud evaluation  When organizations evaluate the security features/capabilities of 
Azure services before onboarding/approving an Azure service(s) into the cloud service 
catalog.
 ■ Compliance requirements  When organizations need to meet compliance require-
ments in highly regulated industries like government, finance , and healthcare. These 
organizations need to certify their workload’s configurations in Azure to meet the secu-
rity specification s defined in a specific framework, such as CIS, NIST, or PCI. 
ASB was created with the idea of providing a canonical set of Azure-centric technical secu-
rity controls based on controls defined by the Center for Internet Security (CIS), the National 
Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), and the Payment Card Industry (PCI). When this 
chapter was written, the latest ASB version was 3 (v3), which considered CIS v8 and v7, NIST 
SP800-53 Rev4, and PCI-DSS v3.2.1. 
When it comes to leveraging Zero Trust principles to your landing zone, you must focus on 
the following three principles: 
 ■ Assume-breach  Assume-breach (or assume-compromise) shifts the overall thinking, 
strategy and architectures from creating a “safe network” to protecting assets on any-
where, including on an “open network.” This mindset shift helps you see the technical 
estate as a “graph” of connected nodes like attackers do, making security planning more 
effective.
 ■ Verify explicitly  Reduces each asset’s attack surface
 ■ Least-privilege access  Reduces the blast radius of a compromise
Zero Trust brings incredible benefits to security posture, productivity, and reducing friction 
between internal teams in organizations but it is also disruptive. Zero Trust changes how an 
organization operates, shifting technology architectures, technical capabilities, and people’s 
assumptions and mindsets about security.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security   CHAPTER 6 287
Create and configure an Azure Key Vault
Azure Key Vault serves as a cloud-based solution designed for the secure storage and retrieval 
of sensitive information, known as secrets. These secrets encompass many data types requiring 
stringent access control, including API keys, passwords, certificates, and cryptographic keys. 
The Key Vault service supports two primary types of containers: vaults and managed hard-
ware security module (HSM) pools. Vaults facilitate the storage of software and HSM-backed 
keys, secrets, and certificates, while managed HSM pools exclusively accommodate HSM-
backed keys. Follow the steps below to create a new Key Vault:
1.
 Navigate to the Azure portal by opening https://portal.azure.com .
2. In the search bar, type blueprint ; under Services, click Key Vaults  and click the Create 
button. The Create A Key Vault page appears, as shown in Figure 6-8.
FIGURE 6-8  Create A Key Vault
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance288
3. On the Basics tab, select the subscription in which you want to create your Key Vault 
and the resource group where it will reside. Type a name for your Key Vault, and select 
the appropriate pricing tier. In the Recovery Options  section, enter the number of days 
to retain deleted vaults (between 7 to 90 days).
4. Optionally, you can enable Purge Protection . Remember that once you select the 
Enable Purge Protection (Enforce A Mandatory Retention Period For Deleted 
Vaults and Vault Objects) , it cannot be changed. 
5.
 Click the Next button; the Access Configuration tab appears, as shown in Figure 6-9.
FIGURE 6-9 Access Configuration
6. On this page, you can change the permission model; the recommended setting— Azure 
Role-Based Access Control —is the default selection. You can select which resources 
to which you want to enable access. After making the selection, click the Next  button. 
The Networking  tab appears, as shown in Figure 6-10.
7. The first option on this tab— Public Access —is enabled by default. You should evalu-
ate this setting based on your business needs. You can also harden the public access 
by selecting which networks can access your Key Vault. Lastly, you can create a private 
endpoint to allow connections to this Key Vault. Once you finish making selections, click 
the Next button.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security   CHAPTER 6 289
FIGURE 6-10 Networking settings
8. On the Tags tab, you can optionally add tags to this Key Vault; once you do that, click 
the Next button, review your selections, and click the Create  button to finalize the 
setup. 
Now that the Key Vault is created and access has been allowed, clients can start accessing it. 
Azure Key Vault employs the Transport Layer Security (TLS) protocol to safeguard data during 
transit between the Key Vault and clients. Clients engage in a negotiation process to establish 
a TLS connection with Azure Key Vault. TLS offers robust authentication, message privacy, and 
integrity, ensuring detection of message tampering, interception, and forgery. Additionally, 
TLS promotes interoperability, provides algorithm flexibility, and facilitates straightforward 
deployment and usage.
If you need to make changes after creating the Key Vault, you can simply go to the Key Vault 
properties and click Access Configuration under Settings , as shown in Figure 6-11.

Recommend when to use a Dedicated Hardware Security 
Module (HSM)
Azure Dedicated HSM is a specialized Azure service designed for cryptographic key storage. 
Tailored to meet the most stringent security standards, Dedicated HSM is the optimal choice 
for customers requiring FIPS 140-2 Level 3-validated devices and complete control over  
the HSM appliance. Deployed across multiple Azure regions, HSM devices can be easily  
provisioned as a high-availability pair and configured for global failover assurance. 
 
Microsoft delivers the Dedicated HSM service utilizing Thales Luna 7 HSM model A790 
appliances, renowned for their superior performance and cryptographic integration capabili-
ties. Once provisioned, HSM devices are directly connected to the customer’s virtual network 
and can be accessed by on-premises applications and management tools through point-to-site 
or site-to-site VPN connectivity. Thales provides customers with the necessary software and 
documentation for configuring and managing HSM devices through their customer support 
portal. 
Some very specific scenarios will demand the use of HSM, as shown in Table 6-1.
TIP F IPS 140-2 Level 3 HSMs for Azure Key Vault has been available in GA since February 
2024 for the Premium SKU.

Configure access to Key Vault, including vault access 
policies and Azure Role-Based Access Control
The first step when configuring Key Vault access is to understand who should have access to 
your Key Vault from an administrative perspective and who can read the keys in your Key Vault. 
When planning your access, use the principle of least privilege to grant only the right level of 
access to only the users who need it.
TIP A zure Dedicated HSM is most suitable for “lift-and-shift” scenarios that require direct 
and sole access to HSM devices, such as when migrating applications from on-premises to 
Azure Virtual Machines. 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance292
Microsoft recommends using Azure Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) to manage Key Vault 
access. Azure RBAC operates on Azure’s control and data plane, enabling you to manage keys, 
secrets, and certificate permissions. The Azure RBAC model empowers users to define permis -
sions at various scope levels, including management groups, subscriptions, resource groups, or 
individual resources. 
Azure RBAC for Key Vault also enables users to establish distinct permissions for individual 
keys, secrets, and certificates. You can leverage built-in roles available in Azure that were tai-
lored for Key Vault and add users to these roles according to their need. Table 6-2 lists built-in 
roles and when to use it.

‘Azure role-based access control’ permission model.

can also use the access policy model, which is considered a legacy authorization system. One 
disadvantage of the access policy model compared to RBAC is that the access policy model 
operates on the data plane only.

Vault and click the Access Control (IAM) option. The Access Control (IAM) page appears, as

A Key Vault access policy dictates whether a specific security principal—a user, application, 
or user group—is authorized to execute various operations on Key Vault secrets, keys, and cer-
tificates. As mentioned before, the Key Vault access policy is considered legacy, but if you want 
to use this type of access control, you can follow the steps at https://aka.ms/AZ500KeyVaultAP . 
Manage certificates, secrets, and keys
When you create a Key Vault certificate, corresponding addressable key and secret entities are 
also generated with identical names. The Key Vault key facilitates key operations, while the Key 
Vault secret enables the retrieval of the certificate value as a secret. Additionally, a Key Vault 
certificate includes public X.509 certificate metadata. 
The certificates possess identifiers and versions. Furthermore, a particular version of the 
addressable key and secret, created alongside the Key Vault certificate version, is accessible 
within the Key Vault certificate response. 
After the Key Vault certificate is created, it can be obtained from the addressable secret 
along with the private key, available in either PFX or PEM format. The policy utilized for certifi -
cate creation must specify that the key is exportable. On the other hand, if the policy desig-
nates the key as nonexportable, the private key will not be included in the retrieved value when

This type of certificate has a public-private key pair and

can then be signed by your registration authority (RA)

A certificate with

provider
This approach has more steps to make the initial setup, 
which comprises establishing an issuer object. Once the 
issuer object is set up within your Key Vault, its name can 
be cited in the policy of the Key Vault certificate. When 
requesting the creation of a Key Vault certificate, a key 
pair is generated within the vault. Subsequently, the 
system interacts with the issuer provider service, utilizing 
the details from the referenced issuer object to obtain an 
x509 certificate. This certificate is fetched from the issuer 
service and combined with the key pair to finalize the 
creation of the KV certificate.
Creating a Key Vault certificate is an asynchronous process. When this operation is requested, 
a Key Vault certificate request is generated, and an HTTP status code of 202 (Accepted) is 
returned. The progress of the request can be monitored by continuously checking the status of 
the pending object created during this operation. The complete URI of the pending object is 
provided in the LOCATION header. Once the request to create a Key Vault certificate is finalized, 
the status of the pending object transitions from in progress to completed, and a new version of 
the Key Vault certificate is generated, becoming the current version.
When a Key Vault certificate is initially created, a corresponding addressable key and secret 
are also generated with matching names. If the the name is already in use, the operation will 
fail with an HTTP status code of 409 (conflict). Attributes for the addressable key and secret are 
derived from those of the Key Vault certificate. These entities are marked as managed keys and 
secrets, with their lifespan governed by Key Vault, and are thus read-only. It’s important to note 
that if a Key Vault certificate expires or is disabled, the associated key and secret become unus -
able. For the first operation to create a Key Vault certificate, a policy is mandatory. 
Subsequent create operations can include a policy to replace the existing one. In cases 
where no policy is provided, the service’s policy resource is used to create the next version of 
the Key Vault certificate. During the creation of a subsequent version, the current Key Vault 
certificate and its corresponding key and secret remain unaltered.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance296
Policy
A certificate policy outlines guidelines for creating and overseeing the lifecycle of a Key Vault 
certificate. When a certificate with a private key is imported into the Key Vault, the Key Vault 
service automatically generates a default policy by extracting information from the X.509 
certificate. 
On the other hand, when a Key Vault certificate is created from scratch, a policy must be 
provided, detailing the creation process for either the current or subsequent versions of the 
certificate. Once a policy is set up, it does not need to be supplied again for subsequent create 
operations targeting future versions. A single policy instance is utilized for all versions of a Key 
Vault certificate. To create or update a policy, you can use the 
Set-AzKeyVaultCertificatePolicy 
cmdlet. 
 
Once certificates are created, you can use them to secure communication and authentica -
tion. For example, you can use it to protect access to your intranet site or secure a cloud-based 
application. Using a chained certificate signed by a public Certificate Authority (CA) can also 
confirm the legitimacy of the entities possessing the certificates.
Secret
When discussing secrets in the context of Key Vault, you can simply think of a secret as a pass-
word you want to keep safe or a database connection string you want to keep secure. Having 
said that, if you are a developer and are building an app that uses Key Vault APIs to access a 
secret, you will see the secret as a string during 
accept and return operations. 
Key Vault secrets are internally stored and managed as sequences of octets (8-bit bytes), 
with a maximum size of 25 kilobytes each. The Key Vault service does not offer semantics for 
secrets; rather, it accepts the data, encrypts it, stores it, and issues a secret identifier (ID). This 
identifier can be utilized to retrieve the secret at a later point in time. It is important to mention 
that depending on the sensitivity of the data and the scenario’s requirement that you have to 
comply with, you should consider adding an extra layer of protection for the data by encrypt-
ing the data using a separate protection key prior to storage in Key Vault.
For scenarios where you want a more custom approach to interpret the type of secret, 
you can leverage the contentType field. You can designate the content type of a secret, which 
helps interpret the secret data upon retrieval. The maximum length allowed for this field is 
255 characters. Use this capability always when  you need to provide guidance on interpreting 
the secret data. One common scenario where this approach applies is when you need to store 
both passwords and certificates as secrets. In this case, you can utilize this field to distinguish 
between them. 
Access control for secrets stored in the Key Vault is administered at the level of the respec-
tive Key Vault housing those secrets. The access control policy for secrets is separate from the 
access control policy for keys within the same Key Vault. Users have the flexibility to establish 
TIP T o create or update a policy, you can use the Set-AzKeyVaultCertificatePolicy cmdlet. 
For examples of how to use this cmdlet, visit https://aka.ms/az500KVCP . 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 6.1: Plan, implement, and manage governance for security   CHAPTER 6 297
one or more vaults for storing secrets and are encouraged to ensure appropriate segmentation 
and management of secrets based on specific scenarios.
Keys
Key Vault offers automated cryptographic key rotation, enabling users to set up Key Vault to 
generate a new key version at defined intervals. This can be achieved by configuring a key 
rotation policy, which can be applied to each individual key. Microsoft recommends to rotate 
encryption keys at least once every two years to adhere to cryptographic best practices.
Key rotation involves creating a new version of an existing key with updated key material. 
Target services should utilize a versionless key URI to seamlessly access the latest key version. 
Your data encryption solution needs to store versioned key URIs alongside the data, ensuring 
that decryption/unwrapping operations point to the same key material used for encryption/
wrapping processes to prevent service disruptions. Currently, all Azure services adhere to this 
pattern for data encryption.
You can create a new key directly from your Key Vault by using the Keys option under 
Object in the properties of the Key Vault. The options available when you are creating a key are 
shown in Figure 6-14.
FIGURE 6-14 Creating a new key
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

During the key creation, you can define the key type (algorithm) used (RSA or EC), and  
the key size. Optionally you can set an activation date for this key and an expiration date.  
You can also use this page to configure the key rotation policy, which appears as not  
configured in Figure 6-14. Another way to configure the key rotation policy is by using  
Set-AzKeyVaultKeyRotationPolicy cmdlet.
If you need to enforce a key rotation policy, you can also use the Keys Should Have  
A Rotation Policy Ensuring That Their Rotation Is Scheduled Within The Specified  
Number Of Days After Creation Azure Policy, as shown in Figure 6-15.
FIGURE 6-15 Key rotation policy in Azure Policy
Configure backup and recovery of certificates, secrets,  
and keys 
Azure Key Vault offers automated features to ensure availability and prevent data loss. Only 
back up secrets if you have a compelling business reason. Doing so may present operational 
complexities, such as managing multiple logs, permissions, and backups as secrets expire or 
rotate. Key Vault guarantees availability during disaster scenarios and will autonomously  
redirect requests to a paired region without user intervention. 
For safeguarding against accidental or malicious deletion of secrets, configure soft-delete and 
purge protection features on your Key Vault. When this chapter was written, Key Vault did not 
support backing up an entire vault in a single operation. A problem that you may face trying to 
back up secrets is that the backup operation creates a point-in-time snapshot. However, secrets 
might renew during a backup operation, which may cause a mismatch of encryption keys. 
Another important point to consider when planning your backup strategy for Key Vault 
is that the Key Vault object that was backed up, such as a secret, key, or certificate, will be 
retrieved as an encrypted blob. This encrypted blob cannot be decrypted outside of Azure. To 
access usable data from this blob, you must restore it into a Key Vault located within the same 
Azure subscription and geographical region.
To start hunting, you just need to select the predefined query created for a specific scenario 
and click the Run Query  button in the right-hand pane. This pane shows a summary of the 
results. Click the View Results  button to see the full details of the query.

Chapter summary   CHAPTER 6 299
 
Thought experiment
In this thought experiment, demonstrate your skills and knowledge of the topics covered in this 
chapter. You can find answers to this thought experiment in the next section.
Governance Security at Tailwind Traders
You are one of the Cloud Security Administrators for Tailwind Traders, an online general store 
specializing in various home products. You received a set of security guidelines from your 
manager and the task to ensure these guidelines are enforced across multiple subscriptions 
in Azure. As part of this request, you cannot allow users to provision a new resource if the 
resource is not using some security best practices. Tailwind Traders’ development team must 
also secure communication between the web server and the clients. Because of the develop-
ment team’s business requirements, you must provide a solution that uses cryptographic keys 
stored in FIPS 140-2. With this information in mind, answer the following questions:
1.
 What should you use to enforce those security guidelines?
2. How do you prevent resources not using security best practices from being deployed?
3. What solution will you use to address the requirements of the development team?
Thought experiment answers
This section contains the solution to the thought experiment.
1. In this case, you need to use Azure Policy to enforce the security guidelines.
2. You should use the deny policy effect to avoid resources that are not using security best 
practices from being deployed. The deny attribute prevents a resource request that 
doesn’t match defined standards through a policy definition and fails the request.
3. You need to create a Key Vault and use an Azure Dedicated HSM to comply with the 
requirements. 
Chapter summary
 ■ Azure Policy allows you to create, assign, and manage a variety of policy definitions.
 ■ A policy assignment is a policy definition that takes place within a specific scope.  
A scope might range from an Azure management group to a resource group.

CHAPTER 6  Security governance300
 ■ An initiative definition is a collection of policy definitions tailored toward achieving a 
singular overarching goal. 
 ■ Azure Blueprints empowers cloud architects and central IT teams to establish a  
consistent set of Azure resources that embody and comply with an organization’s  
standards, patterns, and needs.
 ■ Azure Landing Zone is an environment to host Azure workloads pre-provisioned 
through code. It uses a set of defined cloud services and best practices to add  
foundational capability while providing cloud adoption teams with a well-managed 
environment to run their workloads.
 ■ Azure Key Vault is a cloud-based solution designed for the secure storage and retrieval 
of sensitive information, known as secrets. These secrets encompass many data types 
requiring stringent access control, including API keys, passwords, certificates, and  
cryptographic keys.
 ■ Azure Dedicated HSM is a specialized Azure service designed for cryptographic key 
storage. Tailored to meet the most stringent security standards, Dedicated HSM is the 
optimal choice for customers requiring FIPS 140-2 Level 3–validated devices and  
complete control over the HSM appliance.
 ■ When a Key Vault certificate is created, corresponding addressable key and secret  
entities are generated with identical names. The Key Vault key facilitates key operations, 
while the Key Vault secret allows the certificate value to be retrieved as a secret.
