CHAPTER 7
Microsoft Defender for Cloud
Microsoft Defender for Cloud gives organizations complete visibility and control over cloud 
workloads’ security in Azure, on-premises, or other cloud providers. By actively monitoring  
these workloads, Defender for Cloud enhances the overall security posture of the cloud 
deployment and reduces the exposure of resources to threats. Defender for Cloud also  
uses intelligent threat detection to assist you in protecting your environment from rapidly 
evolving cyberattacks.
Defender for Cloud also assesses the security of your hybrid cloud workload and provides 
recommendations to mitigate threats. In addition, it provides centralized policy management 
to ensure compliance with company or regulatory security requirements.
Skills covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 7.1: Manage security posture by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud
 ■ Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for 
Cloud
Skill 7.1: Manage security posture by using Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud
As enterprises start their journey to the cloud, they will face many challenges trying to adapt 
their on-premises tools to a cloud-based model. In a cloud environment with different 
workloads to manage, it becomes imperative to have ongoing verification and corrective 
actions to ensure that the security posture of those workloads is always at the highest quality 
possible. This section of the chapter covers the skills necessary to manage security posture by 
using Microsoft Defender for Cloud according to the Exam AZ-500 outline.
Identify and remediate security risks by using the 
Microsoft Defender for Cloud Secure Score and Inventory
When working in a cloud environment, monitoring the security state of multiple workloads 
can be challenging. How do you know if your security posture across all workloads is at the 
highest possible level? Are there any security recommendations that you are not meeting? 
These are hard questions to answer when you don’t have the right visibility and tools to  
manage the security aspects of your cloud infrastructure.
301
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud302
Microsoft Defender for Cloud reviews your security recommendations across all workloads, 
applies advanced algorithms to determine whether each recommendation is critical, and 
calculates your Secure Score based on the different risk factors identified for those workloads. 
Secure Score helps you to assess your workload security posture from a single dashboard. 
When you provision a new subscription in Azure and access Defender for Cloud, the default 
plan that will be used is the Microsoft Defender for Cloud, which will be using the Foundational 
Cloud Security Posture Management (CSPM) plan, which is free (no billing costs). This founda-
tional plan gives you access to the following features:
 ■ Security recommendations
 ■ Asset inventory
 ■ Secure score
 ■ Data exporting
 ■ Workflow automation
 ■ Tools for remediation
 ■ Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark
Follow the steps below to open Defender for Cloud and visualize your Secure Score: 
1. Navigate to the Azure portal at https://portal.azure.com .
2. In the search bar, type defender,  and under Services, click Microsoft Defender  
For Cloud.
3. On the Overview  page, in the Security Posture tile, click the Explore Your Security 
Posture hyperlink. Figure 7-1 shows the Security Posture page.

Google Cloud Provider (GCP). For each cloud, a separate Secure Score is shown. All the scores 
are averaged; in this case, the average Secure Score is 48 percent.

Assuming you want to improve your Secure Score in the Azure Cloud, you must focus on 
remediating recommendations that count points toward your Secure Score. The recommenda-
tions are aggregated in security controls, and to increase your Secure Score, all  recommenda-
tions within a security control must be remediated. To see the security controls, go back to 
the Overview  page in Defender for Cloud dashboard, and in the left navigation pane, click 
Recommendations ; in the navigation bar in the right corner, click the Switch To Classic View 
option. The Recommendations  page appears, as shown in Figure 7-2.
FIGURE 7-2 Security controls
In the Name column, the security controls are listed in order of impact on Secure Score 
(from the top down). In other words, the first security control ( Enable MFA) is the one that 
counts more points toward the Secure Score (see the number in the Max Score  column).  
Click the > symbol next to the Enable MFA  security control, and it will expand to show all 
recommendations that belong to this security control, as shown in Figure 7-3.
FIGURE 7-3 Recommendations part of Enable MFA security control
In Figure 7-3, notice the Current Score column, which represents the completion number 
for this security control—in this example, 6.84 out of 10 (in the Max Score column). Another 
important element of this figure is the Status  column, which shows the current status of the 
recommendations within the security control. In Figure 7-3, two recommendations are shown 
as Completed , meaning remediated. One is shown as Unassigned , meaning it has not yet

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud304
been assigned to an owner to remediate. The Unhealthy Resources  column has insights 
about the number of resources, and the icon represents the cloud provider. Also, the first two 
recommendations are in Azure, while the third one is in AWS.
Defender for Cloud also provides several filtering options that help you focus on  
recommendations and controls that are more important for your environment than others.  
The following filtering options are available:
 ■ Control status  lets you filter for security controls with the following status types: 
Active, Completed , or Not Applicable .
 ■ Recommendation status  filters for Completed , On Time, Overdue, or Unassigned  
recommendations.
 ■ Recommendation maturity  lets you decide to focus on GA  and/or Preview 
recommendations.
 ■ Contains exemption  helps you to filter for recommendations that do or do not contain 
resource exemptions.
 ■ Resource types  allow you to select recommendations that apply to one, several, or all 
types of resources. This filter will change according to the types of resources you have 
deployed in your environment.
 ■ Severity filters for recommendations with Low , Medium, or High severity. 
 ■ The Initiative filter lets you filter for recommendations that belong to a particular 
policy initiative, such as CIS, or a custom initiative you have added to your environment.
 ■ Defender for Cloud has added a MITRE ATT&CK Tactics and Techniques mapping to its 
recommendations. The Tactics  filter lets you filter for recommendations mapped to 
one, several, or all tactics.
 ■ The Environment  filter lets you filter for recommendations that belong to Azure, AWS, 
and/or GCP, depending on which environment you have connected to your Defender 
for Cloud estate.
 ■ Recommendation type  lets you filter for Built-In and Custom recommendations.
 ■ Insights lets you filter for recommendations with additional capabilities, such as Deny , 
Enforce, or Quick Fix.
 ■ The Resource group  filter helps you to focus on resources that belong to a particular 
resource group.
 ■ AWS account and GCP project  filters help you to only show recommendations that 
belong to a particular AWS account, or GCP connector.
While the overall approach to remediate recommendations is the same (open the recom-
mendation and follow the steps to remediate), not every recommendation provides the same 
experience. Some recommendations require you to perform manual steps to remediate the 
workload that is not healthy. Some others will allow you to select the unhealthy resource and 
apply the fix from the same dashboard. For example, the Auditing On SQL Server Should Be 
Enabled recommendation allows you to click the Fix  button once a resource is selected, as

Assess compliance against security frameworks and 
Microsoft Defender for Cloud
Governance might be one of the most important topics when it comes to security hygiene. You 
already learned that, with Azure Policy, you have a great tool to create guardrails in which your 
developers and resource administrators can move when it comes to resource creation. There 
might also be legal restrictions and regulatory standards that apply to your organization, such 
as PCI DSS, the Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard for organizations that handle 
credit cards, or ISO27001, an international standard on how to manage information security. 
Azure Policy contains several built-in initiative definitions, which can be used to determine 
your resources’ compliance toward the particular compliance standards, as shown in Figure 7-8.
FIGURE 7-8 Built-in Regulatory Compliance policies. 
You can assign these definitions directly to your management groups or subscriptions to see 
the results in the Azure Policy compliance dashboard. Alternatively, you can leverage Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud to assess your resources’ compliance with the standards that apply to your 
organization. 
Under the hood, Defender for Cloud leverages the built-in policy initiatives to automatically 
assess resource compliance toward a given compliance standard whenever possible. Azure 
Policy will only show resources as compliant or noncompliant regarding a particular policy or 
initiative definition. Still, parts of the compliance standards cannot automatically be assessed 
and, therefore, are not represented in the respective initiative definition. Defender for Cloud 
adds more context to these standards, so you do not only see assessment results, but also to 
which category these assessments belong.

FIGURE 7-9 Regulatory Compliance dashboard
Figure 7-9 shows the Defender for Cloud Regulatory Compliance dashboard. The PCI DSS 4 
standard is selected in this screenshot, and the compliance controls at the bottom of the page 
reflect this standard. If you open the compliance controls, you will see that these controls con -
tain several sub-controls each. Compliance controls can have three different states: the green 
circle with a checkmark shows that all resources within the scope are compliant regarding 
this particular control, whereas a red circle with a cross shows that resources are either non-
compliant or cannot automatically be assessed regarding this particular control. You will realize 
that some controls and sub-controls are grayed out, a third state. These controls are the ones 
that currently cannot automatically be assessed, so Defender for Cloud cannot decide whether 
a particular rule is met. For instance, this might happen if a compliance control is focused on 
processes rather than resource configurations. 
Moving a step deeper into the sub-controls, you will find the assessments that belong to the 
control. These assessments are based on the policies that are part of the respective Regulatory 
Compliance initiative definition. Figure 7-10 shows the 3.3. Requirement 03: Protect Stored

Add industry and regulatory standards to Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud
The Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark (MCSB) offers detailed best practices and recom-
mendations to enhance the security of workloads, data, and services across Azure and your 
multicloud environment. It concentrates on cloud-specific control areas, drawing insights from 
a comprehensive array of Microsoft and industry security guidelines. 
MCSB encompasses security guidance from the Cloud Adoption Framework, covering 
aspects like security strategy, roles and responsibilities, Azure Top 10 Security Best Practices, 
and reference implementations. Additionally, it incorporates Azure Well-Architected Frame-
work guidance for securing workloads on Azure, alongside industry standards and frameworks 
such as Amazon Web Services (AWS) Well-Architected Framework, Center for Internet Security 
(CIS) Controls, National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), and the Payment Card 
Industry Data Security Standard (PCI-DSS). 
MCSB is the default benchmark used in Defender for Cloud to generate the security  
recommendations, and it is accessible via the Regulatory Compliance dashboard, as shown in 
Figure 7-12.
As you can see in Figure 7-12, the MCSB comprises 12 assessments that range from network 
security to governance and strategy.

Add custom initiatives to Microsoft Defender for Cloud
You can add custom initiatives in Defender for Cloud by leveraging Azure Policy. This custom 
initiative will also show up in the Regulatory Compliance view, enabling you to get a more 
structured view of your policies. With a custom initiative in Regulatory Compliance, you can 
get a condensed view of only the policies you want to focus on. 
You should always try to enhance your Secure Score by focusing on the most impactful  
recommendations, as explained in this chapter, but if your governance concept demands  
further restrictions, you can create your own custom initiative. To do that, follow these steps:
1.
 Open the Azure portal and sign in with an account with Subscription Owner privileges.
2. In the Search bar, type Policy, then select Policy from the search results.
3. In the Authoring section of the Azure Policy blade, click the Definitions  option.
4. From the top menu, click + Initiative Definition ; the Initiative Definition  page 
appears, as shown in Figure 7-13.
5. Select the Initiative Location  and select the subscription. Remember that the initiative 
assignment is only available to resources at or below this location in the hierarchy.
6. Provide a Name and an informative Description , so others can easily figure out your 
new initiative’s purpose.
7. Under Category , click Create New.
8. Click the Next button, and the Policies  page appears; click the Add Policy 
Definition(s) button. Select all policy definitions you want to add to your custom  
initiative and click the Next button. 
9. In the Groups tab, you can organize policies within an initiative. Furthermore, groups 
reflect the settings in the Regulatory Compliance dashboard within Defender for Cloud. 
Create as many groups as you need within your initiative definition. Make sure to use 
informative names and descriptions, as this information is shown in the Regulatory 
Compliance dashboard and helps others understand how you have grouped your policy 
definitions. After you finish this configuration, click the Next  button.

Microsoft Defender for Cloud
Establishing connectivity between hybrid cloud and multicloud environments and Microsoft  
Defender for Cloud is essential for maintaining a cohesive security stance across varied IT 
infrastructures. Leveraging tools like Azure Arc–enabled servers for non-Azure machines, 
the Native Cloud Connector, and the Classic Connector enables the extension of Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud capabilities to non-Azure resources. 
This integration equips organizations with comprehensive monitoring, detection, and 
response capabilities against security threats. Here, we present an informative overview of the 
connection process and detailed prerequisites for successful implementation. To better

Skill 7.1: Manage security posture by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 313
Identify and monitor external assets by using Microsoft 
Defender External Attack Surface Management
Microsoft Defender External Attack Surface Management (Defender EASM) persistently 
detects and charts your digital attack surface, offering an external perspective of your online 
infrastructure. The intent is to look at your organization from the outside-in like an attacker 
would. The process happens in three major phases:
 ■ To perform the initial discovery, sensors and crawlers scan the Internet and your  
external, Internet-facing profile.
 ■ This initial scan will enable you to understand the unknown assets, shadow IT, and 
legacy services you still have operating online.
 ■ After gathering and rationalizing this information, Defender EASM builds an accurate 
and up-to-date catalog of your organization’s resources and assets.
This insight empowers security and IT teams to uncover unknowns, assess risks, neutralize 
threats, and broaden vulnerability and exposure management beyond the firewall. Leveraging 
vulnerability and infrastructure data, Attack Surface Insights highlights critical areas of concern 
for your organization. Microsoft employs proprietary discovery technology that recursively 
scans infrastructure, identifying connections to established legitimate assets to deduce 
their relationship with the organization and reveal previously undisclosed and unmonitored 
properties. 
These legitimate assets, termed discovery’ seeds,’ serve as the initial focus for Defender 
EASM, which traces robust connections to these entities, recursively exploring further connec-
tions to compile your comprehensive Attack Surface. The list of assets that are discoverable by 
Defender EASM includes:
 ■ Domains
 ■ Hostnames
 ■ Web pages
 ■ IP blocks
 ■ IP addresses
 ■ ASNs
 ■ SSL certificates
 ■ WHOIS contacts
The first step to configure Defender EASM is creating a new resource. To do that, you need 
to have Contributor  access to your Azure subscription. After validating that you have this 
access, follow the steps below to create a new Defender EASM resource:
1. Open the Azure portal and sign in with an account with Subscription Owner privileges.
2. In the search bar, type Defender EASM , then select Microsoft Defender EASM ; the 
Microsoft Defender EASM page appears, as shown in Figure 7-14.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud314

3. Click the Create button; the Create Microsoft Defender EASM Resource  page 
appears, as shown in Figure 7-15.
FIGURE 7-15 Creating a new resource
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

4. Select the desired Subscription  and the Resource Group .
5. Type the name for this instance and select the region where you want this resource to 
reside. After that, click the Review + Create button, followed by  Create .
6. After creating a resource, you can click it to open the Defender EASM dashboard. 
After the resource is created, you must create a discovery group. This discovery group will 
be the starting point to create your attack surface, which is built by using a discovery algorithm 
that starts with a set of initial identifiers called seeds. For example, to discover Contoso’s  
infrastructure, you can use the domain ( contoso.com) as the initial keystone seed. To create a 
discovery group, click the Discovery  option in the Manage section and click the + Add  
Discovery Group  button, as shown in Figure 7-16.

organizations. Figure 7-17 shows where you configure the seeds when creating a new discovery

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 317
FIGURE 7-18 Attack Surface Summary page
Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by 
using Microsoft Defender for Cloud
The first step to improving your security posture is to ensure that you are remediating security 
recommendations to disrupt potential attacks before they occur. However, you need to be 
prepared to quickly detect active attempts to compromise your cloud workloads; to do that, 
you need threat protection. This section of the chapter covers the skills necessary to configure 
and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud according to the Exam 
AZ-500 outline.
Enable workload protection services in Microsoft Defender 
for Cloud, including Microsoft Defender for Storage, 
Databases, Containers, App Service, Key Vault, and 
Resource Manager
Defender for Cloud uses advanced security analytics and machine-learning technologies to 
evaluate events across the entire cloud fabric. The security analytics include data from mul-
tiple sources, including Microsoft products and services, the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit 
(DCU), the Microsoft Security Response Center (MSRC), and external feeds. This is the core of 
Defender for Cloud threat detection, and on top of that, there will be different mechanisms of 
detection according to the workload.
Different workloads will have different threat landscapes, meaning that threat protection 
for each workload was custom-designed to address that specific workload ’s threat landscape. 
Threat protection will enable Defender for Cloud to trigger an alert once it identifies suspicious 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud318
activity. However, before a Defender for Cloud plan triggers an alert, it needs to be certain that 
this alert is a true-positive. The intent is to reduce false-positives and provide a high-quality 
alert level. Not all alerts will have the same level of information, as it depends on the threat 
type and the analytics for that threat. Alerts will also differ in the time it takes for them to be 
triggered.
Defender for Storage
Visibility and control are becoming critical with regard to the security hygiene of workloads. 
Exposed storage containers are a typical scenario targeted by threat actors that are utilizing 
custom scanners built specifically to identify public containers. Microsoft Defender for Storage 
is an Azure-native layer of security intelligence that detects unusual and potentially harmful 
attempts to access or exploit your storage accounts.
Defender for Storage can be enabled for data stored in Azure Blob Storage (Standard/ 
Premium StorageV2, Block Blobs), Azure Files, and Azure Data Lake Storage (ADLS) Gen2. You 
can enable Defender for Storage on the subscription level just like any other plan, but you can 
also enable it only on the storage accounts you want to protect. 
Defender for Storage also has malware scanning capabilities available. Malware Scanning in 
Defender for Storage safeguards your Azure Blob Storage from malicious content by conduct-
ing comprehensive malware scans on uploaded files in near real-time, leveraging Microsoft 
Defender Antivirus capabilities. This feature helps meet security and compliance requirements 
for managing untrusted content. As an agentless software as a service (SaaS) solution, it offers 
easy setup at scale, requires no maintenance, and supports automated response at scale.
Defender for Storage continually analyzes the telemetry stream generated by the Azure 
Blob Storage and Azure Files services. If a potential malicious activity is detected, a security 
alert is generated. Defender for Storage provides alerts based on different types of threats, as 
shown in the list below:
 ■ Unusual access to an account  Threats such as access from a TOR exit node.
 ■ Unusual behavior in an account  Behavior that deviates from a learned baseline, 
such as an unusual deletion of blobs or files. 
 ■ Hash reputation-based malware detection  As files are uploaded to the storage 
account, they are analyzed by the hash reputation system, which looks into Microsoft 
Threat Intelligence to determine if the file is suspicious. It is very important to under -
stand that this is not an antimalware feature since it only looks into the file hash and 
does not scan the file.
 ■ Unusual file uploads  The upload deviates from the normal pattern, such as the 
upload of cloud service packages and executable files.
 ■ Public visibility  Related to attempts to break in by using public scanners.
 ■ Phishing campaigns  Related to scenarios where a piece of content hosted on an 
Azure Storage account was identified as part of a phishing attack impacting Microsoft 
365 users.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 319
While our general recommendation is to keep Defender for Storage enabled across every 
type of storage account at the subscription level, some organizations that need to save cost 
might need to prioritize what is most critical to them and enabled only on the storage  
account level. 
There is also a misunderstanding that storage accounts behind private links don’t pose a  
risk and don’t need Defender for Storage enabled. In reality, several threats still threaten 
the storage account behind private links. The most prominent threats are malicious insiders, 
compromised identities, and lateral movement within the private network when a resource 
has been compromised, access keys or access tokens have been compromised, or if identity 
has been compromised and access privileges have been escalated. For this reason, using this 
rationale exclusively is not a recommended approach.
Before making this decision, it is important for your organization to perform a risk  
assessment that considers the following factors:
 ■ The type of information stored in the Storage account
 ■ The potential impact if this data is compromised 
 ■ Whether an attacker can leverage the storage account as an entry point into your 
organization
To enable any paid plan in Defender for Cloud, you need to open the environment settings 
in Defender for Cloud dashboard, select the subscription that you want to enable the plan, 
select Defender Plans , and then change the toggle to ON in the appropriate plan, which in 
this case, is Defender for Storage , as shown in Figure 7-19.
FIGURE 7-19 Enabling Defender for Storage

Once the plan is enabled, Defender for Storage starts monitoring the storage account, and 
upon identification of an attack that matches its threat detection analytics, it will trigger an 
alert in the Security Alert dashboard, as shown in Figure 7-20.

Defender for Databases
Threat protection for databases can be accomplished by enabling Defender for Databases, 
which contains different database (SQL, Open Source, and Cosmos DB) options to be enabled. 
One of the most utilized is Defender for SQL, a protection plan that helps you mitigate  
potential database vulnerabilities and detect anomalous activities that may indicate threats 
against SQL databases. Defender for SQL has evolved over the years and currently has two 
major plans: 
 ■ Defender for Azure SQL  Includes Azure SQL Database, Azure SQL Managed 
Instance, and Dedicated SQL pool in Azure Synapse.
 ■ Defender for SQL servers on machines  Includes SQL Server running on VMs in 
Azure, on-premises, or in another cloud provider.
Defender for SQL provides threat detection for anomalous activities indicating unusual and 
potentially harmful attempts to access or exploit databases. Figure 7-21 is an example of what a 
Defender for SQL alert looks like.
Another capability available on Defender for SQL is the built-in vulnerability assessment 
(VA). SQL VA scans your database and gives you a comprehensive list of items that must be 
addressed. SQL VA employs a knowledge base of rules that flag security vulnerabilities. It 
brings awareness of deviations from security best practices, such as misconfigurations, exces -
sive permissions, and unprotected sensitive data. You can access the assessment from the SQL  
Databases Should Have Vulnerability Findings Resolved security recommendation, as 
shown in Figure 7-22.

Another Defender for Database protection is available for Azure Cosmos DB. Azure Cosmos 
DB is a fully managed NoSQL database for modern, fast, and flexible app development, capa -
ble of having single-digit millisecond response times, automatic and instant scalability, and 
multiple SDKs and APIs to support a variety of nonrelational data models. Defender for Azure 
Cosmos DB monitors your Azure Cosmos DB accounts and detects various attack vectors, such 
as attacks originating from the application layer, SQL injections, suspicious access patterns, 
compromised identities, malicious insiders, and direct attacks on the database.

Defender for Containers
Defender for Containers provides hardening assessment protects for Azure Kubernetes Service 
(AKS), Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (EKS) in a connected Amazon Web Services (AWS) 
account, Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE) in a connected Google Cloud Platform (GCP)  
project, and other Kubernetes distributions (using Azure Arc–enabled Kubernetes). The plan 
can be enabled using the same dashboard as shown in Figure 7-19. 
After the plan is enabled, Defender for Container will also trigger additional recommenda-
tions to enable the sensor that needs to be installed in the cluster. These recommendations are:
 ■ Azure Kubernetes Service clusters should have a Defender profile enabled
 ■ Azure Arc–enabled Kubernetes clusters should have the Defender extension installed
 ■ Azure Kubernetes Service clusters should have the Azure Policy Add-on for Kubernetes 
installed
 ■ Azure Arc–enabled Kubernetes clusters should have the Azure Policy extension  
installed 
Just like any other Defender for Cloud paid plan, Defender for Containers brings security 
posture enhancements capabilities as well as threat detection for nodes and clusters. The 
vulnerability assessment performed by Defender for Containers appears in Microsoft Defender 
for Cloud dashboard as a security recommendation. Figure 7-23 shows how the vulnerability 
assessment appears as a recommendation in a container running in AWS.

Defender for App Service
Azure App Service is a service for hosting web applications, REST APIs, and mobile back ends. 
It enables you to develop in many languages, such as .NET, .NET Core, Java, Ruby, Node.js, 
PHP, or Python. Applications run and scale on both Windows and Linux. Defender for Cloud 
leverages the scale of the cloud to identify attacks on App Service applications; it focuses on 
emerging attacks while attackers are in the reconnaissance phase and identifies vulnerabilities 
across multiple websites hosted on Azure.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud324
Defender for App Service detects many threats by continuously monitoring the VM 
instance in which it is running and its management interface. It also monitors the requests and 
responses sent to and from your App Service apps and the underlying sandboxes and VMs.
Figure 7-25 shows an example of a dangling DNS alert, an analytic available in this plan to 
catch a DNS record pointing to a recently deleted App Service resource.
FIGURE 7-25 Dangling DNS alert triggered by Defender for App Service

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 325
Although this is a sample alert generated by the Sample Alert feature, it gives you an idea of 
the metadata available for this analytic. This attack usually occurs when a website is removed, 
but the custom domain from the DNS registrar is not. In this case, the DNS entry points to a 
nonexistent resource, which means the domain is vulnerable to a takeover. Although Defender 
for Cloud does not perform a scan DNS registrar for existing dangling DNS entries, it will still 
trigger an alert when an App Service website is decommissioned and its custom domain (DNS 
entry) is not deleted.

Defender for Key Vault uses machine learning to detect unusual and potentially harmful 
attempts to access or exploit Key Vault accounts. This plan is only available to enable on the 
subscription level.

gating and responding to it as outlined below. Since Microsoft Defender for Key Vault secures

Defender for Resource Manager
Azure Resource Manager (ARM) is Azure’s deployment and management service. ARM 
provides a management layer that allows you to create, update, and delete resources in your 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Azure account. These operations can be done via the Azure portal, PowerShell, Azure CLI, REST 
APIs, and client SDKs.
Threat actors targeting ARM will most likely use Toolkits such as Microbust to discover weak 
configurations and perform post-exploitation actions such as credential dumping. Defender 
for Resource Manager uses advanced security analytics to detect threats and trigger an alert 
when a suspicious activity occurs in the service layer. Also, Defender for Resource Manager can 
detect suspicious resource management operations, which include suspicious IP addresses, dis-

Defender for Servers
Defender for Servers provides threat detection and advanced defenses for your Windows and 
Linux machines across Azure, Amazon Web Services (AWS), Google Cloud Platform (GCP), and 
on-premises environments. Microsoft Defender for Servers features automatic, native integra-
tion with Microsoft Defender for Endpoint (MDE), offering access to vulnerability findings from 
Microsoft Defender vulnerability management. 
Defender for Servers applies known patterns to discover malicious behavior, which is called 
behavioral analysis. It uses statistical profiling to build a historical baseline, which means an 
alert may be triggered when Defender for Server detects deviations from established baselines 
that conform to a potential attack vector. The result will be externalized in the dashboard via a 
security alert. A security alert contains valuable information about what triggered the alert, the 
resources targeted, the source of the attack, and suggestions to remediate the threat. 
TIP T o learn more about Defender CSPM features and use case scenarios, download the free 
Microsoft CNAPP eBook at https://aka.ms/MSCNAPP .
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud328
Alerts generated by Defender for Server are also called Virtual Machine Behavioral Analysis 
(VMBA). This type of alert uses behavioral analytics to identify compromised resources based 
on analyzing the virtual machine (VM) event logs, such as process-creation events, in memory 
only (fileless attack), and login events. Defender for Servers also identifies suspicious activity 
in the network layer by collecting security information from your Azure Internet Protocol Flow 
Information Export (IPFIX) traffic and analyzing it to identify threats. An example of an alert 
that belongs to this category is the Suspicious Incoming RDP Network Activity from Multiple 
Sources alert. 
Defender for Servers has two tiers called Plan 1 (P1) and Plan 2 (P2). P1 was created for  
scenarios where you only need to deploy MDE using the native integration of Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud. In this plan, MDE licenses are charged per hour instead of per seat,  
lowering costs for protected VMs only when they are in use. MDE leverages the Defender for 
Cloud auto-provisioning ability to automatically deploy to all cloud workloads. Lastly, this 
plan also offers alerts and vulnerability data from MDE to appear in the Defender for Cloud 
dashboard. 
P2 does everything that P1 does, but it adds all other Defender for Servers capabilities,  
such as:
 ■ Threat detections for the operating system, network layer, and Control Plane
 ■ File integrity monitoring
 ■ Just-in-Time VM access
 ■ Integrated vulnerability assessment powered by Microsoft Threat Vulnerability  
Management (TVM)
 ■ Adaptive application control
 ■ Adaptive network hardening
 ■ Network map
 ■ Regulatory Compliance dashboard
 ■ 500MB allowance in the Log Analytics workspace for specific data type
When enabling Defender for Server for the first time, you can select if you want P1 or P2. 
Follow the steps below to see which plan you are currently using:
1. Open the Azure portal and sign in with a user with Security Admin  privileges.
2. In the left navigation pane, click Defender For Cloud.
3. In the Defender For Cloud  navigation pane under Management , click the  
Environment Settings  option.
4. Click the subscription for which you want to view the pricing settings.
5. On the Defender Plans  page, under Server Pricing , click the Change Plan option, and 
you will see the Plan Selection blade, as shown in Figure 7-28.
6. Once you finish reviewing, click the Confirm  button.

Manage and respond to security alerts in Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud
The cyber kill chain is a threat model described in 2011 by analysts from Lockheed Martin.  
The model was designed to help security teams and researchers organize their thinking about 
detecting and responding to threats. The model is now generally accepted and is part of the 
general lexicon of all security professionals.
The original cyber kill chain was divided into these phases:
1.
 Reconnaissance  During this phase, the attacker identifies the best targets.
2. Weaponization  Here, files are altered to make them weapons against a target system 
and are used to install malicious code.
IMPORTANT W hen you enable Defender for Servers on your subscription, its coverage does 
not automatically extend to your Log Analytics workspaces. You must enable Defender for 
Servers for each workspace individually. Note that Defender for Servers on workspaces  
supports only Plan 2. 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud330
3. Delivery At this point, weaponized files are placed on the target.
4. Exploitation  During this phase, weaponized files are detonated—that is, they’re run 
on the victim system.
5. Installation  At this point, a back door is installed on the compromised system, giving 
the attacker persistent access.
6. Command-and-control (C&C)  Here, malware on the compromised system  
communicates with a C&C system that gives the attacker access to the resources 
required to carry out their objective.
7. Actions on objectives  At this point, the attacker carries out their objectives, which 
might be predefined or might have evolved based on discovery. The cyber kill chain 
was defined when on-premises computing was the norm. Now that cloud computing is 
considered a better option, the cyber kill chain must be re-evaluated and updated.  
A reconfiguration of the cyber kill chain might include the following phases:
 ■ Active recon In this phase, the attacker uses numerous footprinting methods to 
identify the operating system or services being used, which will be the focus of the 
attack.
 ■ Delivery Because most public cloud assets lack a logged-on user, attackers will 
need to hack the system—typically by finding unpatched exploits that can be  
leveraged against the system or service.
 ■ Exploitation  This phase focuses on problems on the server side instead of the  
client side.
 ■ Persistence  Unlike client-side systems, which are rebooted often, server- and  
service-specific systems are rarely rebooted. The exploit code must remain in 
memory to remain persistent within the system. That is the focus of this stage.
 ■ Internal recon  Attacks against cloud-based systems are server-based. Therefore, 
rather than installing new tools, the attacker can simply use the server’s built-in tools 
to conduct the attack. This helps the attacker remain undiscovered, as installing new 
tools could trigger an alert.
Defender for Cloud has a type of alert called a security incident , which is raised in the 
dashboard whenever the system identifies multiple alerts that, when correlated with each 
other, indicate that those alerts belong to the same attack. A security incident uses the fusion 
capability to correlate the alerts that appear to be related. Each phase of the cyber kill chain 
individually indicates that something bad may be happening but cannot offer you complete 
certainty of the scenario. However, when you correlate these findings, you can have a better 
idea about the attack. A security incident appears in the alert dashboard in the format shown 
in Figure 7-29.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 331
FIGURE 7-29 Security incident with multiple alerts
Alert suppression
There are some scenarios in which you may want to dismiss an alert because you consider a 
false positive for your environment. A typical scenario is when organizations are going through 
a pentest exercise conducted by their red team, and some alerts start getting triggered, and 
they want to suppress to avoid noise and alert pollution. For those scenarios, you can leverage 
the alert suppression feature. 
Before configuring the alert suppression, you should identify the exact alert you want to 
suppress and how long the suppression rule should be active. Establishing an expiration date 
for the rule is important because you don’t want to be blind to this alert forever. Usually, those 
suppression scenarios happen for a reason, and for the most part, these reasons are happening 
due to a temporary circumstance. Follow these steps to configure an alert suppression rule:
1.
 Open the Azure portal and sign in with a user who has Security Admin  privileges.
2. In the left navigation pane, click Defender For Cloud.
3. Under General in the Defender for Cloud navigation pane, click the Security Alerts  
option.
4. Click the Suppression Rules  option in the Security Alerts dashboard; the  
Suppression Rules  page appears, as shown in Figure 7-30.
FIGURE 7-30 Suppression Rules page
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud332
5. Click the Create New Suppression Rule  option; the New Suppression Rule  blade 
appears, as shown in Figure 7-31.
FIGURE 7-31 Creating a new suppression rule
6. In the Rule Conditions  section, click the Subscription  dropdown option and select the 
subscription to which you want to apply this rule.
7. Under Alerts , select Custom , and in the dropdown, select the alert you want to sup-
press. For this example, select the Suspicious PHP Execution Detected sample alert.
8. Under the Entities  option, you can make the suppression more granular by specifying 
the specific fields from the alert that should match the suppressed rule. You can click the 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 333
plus sign button (+) to add multiple entities. Remember that when you do that, the  
suppression rule will only apply if both conditions are true. In order words, there is an 
AND between each entity field. For this example, leave this selection as is.
9. Type a name for this rule in the Rule Details  section under the Rule Name . The name 
cannot contain spaces. For this example, type PHPSuppression .
10. Under State, leave the default option, Enabled . 
11. Under Reason, select the most appropriate option in the dropdown. For this example, 
select Other, and under Comment , type Suppression for Red Team exercise.
12. Under Rule expiration , configure the rule for two months from the day you are 
configuring.
13. To validate the rule, click the Simulate button. The result will be shown right under the 
Test Your Rule option.
14. Click the Apply button to commit the change and create the rule.
The next time this alert is triggered, it will be automatically suppressed. It is important to 
mention that suppressed alerts are still available for you to see. You just need to change the 
filter in the Security Alerts  dashboard to see dismissed alerts. If you use the Continuous 
Export feature to export all alerts to the Log Analytics workspace, the suppressed alerts will 
also be available; you just need to run a query for dismissed alerts.
Responding to alerts
Aside from suppressing alerts that may be considered false-positives, you should always review 
the details and use the additional details to respond to alerts. When you open an alert and 
choose the Take Action tab, you will see the options available for that specific alert, as shown in 
Figure 7-32.
FIGURE 7-32 Take Action tab
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 7  Microsoft Defender for Cloud334
The options available on the Take Action tab will vary according to the alert. In Figure 7-32, 
the first section, Impact Resource Content , has no additional information because no logs 
were found for the affected resource within one day of this alert. In the Mitigate The Threat 
section are some guidelines of what can be done to mitigate this type of attack in the future. 
The Prevent Future Attacks section shows important security recommendations you should 
remediate. Closing the loop from detection to response is an important step. The process of 
working on an incident from detection to response will also reveal areas that can be improved. 
Use the lessons you learn to enhance your security posture. 
The following options are shown in the Prevent Future Attacks section:
 ■ Trigger Automated Response  Here, you can select an Azure Logic App you  
previously created to run once this alert is triggered. 
 ■ Suppress Similar Alerts  Here, you can leverage the alert suppression capability  
mentioned previously in this chapter to suppress this type of alert in the future.
 ■ Configure Email Notification Settings  Here, you can configure who’ll get emails 
regarding security alerts for this subscription.
Configure workflow automation by using Microsoft 
Defender for Cloud 
Workflow automation is a capability that helps you to automatically react on either a new alert, 
or on a new recommendation. It leverages Azure Logic Apps as the automation artifact, and 
within the Logic App, you are offered almost unlimited possibilities to automate processes. To 
enable automation, the workflow automation feature brings additional Trigger Types to Logic 
Apps:
 ■ The Recommendation  trigger will start a Logic App playbook in the following 
conditions:
 ■ A resource has been added to a recommendation as a result of an assessment.
 ■ A resource status has changed within a recommendation as a result of an assessment, 
while the resource status can be healthy, unhealthy, or not applicable.
 ■ A Logic App is manually triggered from a recommendation within Defender for 
Cloud. 
 ■ The Security Alert  trigger will start a Logic App playbook in the following conditions:
 ■ A new security alert is created in Microsoft Defender for Cloud.
 ■ From an alert, the Logic App is manually triggered.
 ■ The Regulatory Compliance Standards trigger is triggered when a compliance  
control’s status within a standard is changed. In this case, the status can be failed, 
passed, skipped, or unsupported.
The first trigger type will help you create several types of automation artifacts. For example, 
you could let the Logic App create a JIRA or ServiceNow ticket or notify stakeholders about 
new resource exemptions if a new recommendation is created. You could also auto-remediate 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 7.2: Configure and manage threat protection by using Microsoft Defender for Cloud   CHAPTER 7 335
or quarantine resources if they were part of a recommendation. Every time Defender for Cloud 
triggers the Logic App, it will send a bunch of information that you can use for further steps, 
including
 ■ Name of the assessment as a GUID
 ■ Assessment ID
 ■ The recommendation’s display name
 ■ Metadata information for the recommendation, including a description, remediation 
steps, and severity
 ■ Resource details, including the resource ID
 ■ Status code (healthy, unhealthy, not applicable)
 ■ A deep link to the assessment result in the recommendations blade
This information can then be used within the Logic App, either for storing it or notifying 
someone, but also to retrieve further information from other APIs, such as the different  
Microsoft Defender for Cloud REST API providers. 
If you want to auto-remediate a resource, the information about the resource and the 
assessment/recommendation help you determine the next steps. In the next section, you will 
find examples of manually and automatically triggered Logic Apps as reactions to recom -
mendations. Once you have built your Logic App, you need to create a workflow around it so 
Defender for Cloud can automatically trigger the Logic App without manual interaction. Follow 
these steps to create a workflow:
1.
 Open the Azure portal and sign in with a user who has Security Admin  privileges.  
Also, your account must also have write permissions for the target resource.
2. In the search bar, enter Defender, then open Microsoft Defender for Cloud  from the 
search results.
3. In the Management  section of the navigation pane, click Workflow Automation , 
and then click + Add Workflow Automation ; the Add Workflow Automation  page 
appears, as shown in Figure 7-33.
4. Enter a Name, and select the Subscription  and Resource Group  to store the workflow 
configuration.
5. From the Defender For Cloud Data Types dropdown list, you can choose between 
Security Alert , Recommendation , or Regulatory Compliance Standards . This  
setting will determine the trigger type for the workflow to react on. Notice that the 
blade will change depending on your selection. Select Recommendation.
6.
 Select the recommendations to react on, the severity, and the recommendation state to 
trigger your workflow.
7.
 Select the Subscription  in which you have created your Logic App and select the Logic 
App from the dropdown. Click Create .
8. Once the workflow automation has been created, it will trigger the Logic App you’ve 
selected whenever the trigger condition is 
true.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Threat Protection at Tailwind Traders
You are a security operation center (SOC) analyst for Tailwind Traders, an online general store 
specializing in various home products. You received a set of requests related to threat detec-
tion for workloads in Azure. One of the requirements is to ensure that you can identify threats 
in the Azure service layer and potential brute-force attacks against SQL databases. As part of 
this project, you must create an automated response if this type of attack appears to ensure 
IMPORTANT U ntil April 2024, you could select either Qualys or Defender Vulnerability 
Management as your vulnerability assessment source. However, Defender for Server’s  
vulnerability assessment solution powered by Qualys retired in May 2024. 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

that you automatically open a ticket on Jira and close the firewall to the IP address that origi-
nated the brute-force attack. With this information in mind, answer the following questions:
1. Which Defender for Cloud plans do you need to enable to identify attacks in the Azure 
service layer?
2.
 Which Defender for Cloud plans do you need to enable to identify brute-force attacks 
against an SQL database?

Thought experiment answers
This section contains the solution to the thought experiment.
1. You must enable Defender for Resource Manager to identify attacks in the service layer.
2. To identify brute-force attacks against SQL databases, you must enable Defender  
for SQL.
3.
 You should use Workflow Automation to automate your response to brute-force 
attacks. 
Chapter summary
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Cloud reviews your security recommendations across all  
workloads, applies advanced algorithms to determine whether each recommendation 
is critical, and calculates your Secure Score based on the risk factors identified for those 
workloads.
 ■ By leveraging the inventory capability in Defender for Cloud, you can obtain this  
visualization and better understand each resource status.
 ■ Governance might be one of the most important topics regarding security hygiene. 
You already learned that, with Azure Policy, you have a great tool to create guardrails in 
which your developers and resource administrators can move when it comes to resource 
creation. 
 ■ The Microsoft Cloud Security Benchmark (MCSB) offers detailed best practices and 
recommendations to enhance the security of workloads, data, and services across Azure 
and your multicloud environment.
 ■ You can add custom initiatives in Defender for Cloud by leveraging Azure Policy. This 
custom initiative will also show up in the Regulatory Compliance view, enabling you to 
get a more structured view of your policies.
 ■ Leveraging tools like Azure Arc–enabled servers for non-Azure machines, the Native 
Cloud Connector and the Classic Connector enable the extension of Microsoft Defender 
for Cloud capabilities to non-Azure resources.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Chapter summary   CHAPTER 7 339
 ■ Microsoft Defender External Attack Surface Management (Defender EASM) persistently 
detects and charts your digital attack surface, offering an external perspective of your 
online infrastructure.
 ■ Defender for Storage can be enabled for data stored in Azure Blob Storage (Standard/
Premium StorageV2, Block Blobs), Azure Files, and Azure Data Lake Storage (ADLS) 
Gen2.
 ■ Threat protection for databases can be accomplished by enabling Defender for  
Databases, which contains different database options that can be enabled, such as SQL, 
Open Source, and Cosmos DB.
 ■ Defender for Containers provides hardening assessment protects for Azure Kubernetes 
Service (AKS), Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (EKS) in a connected Amazon Web 
Services (AWS) account, Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE) in a connected Google Cloud 
Platform (GCP) project, and other Kubernetes distributions (using Azure Arc–enabled 
Kubernetes).
 ■ Defender for App Service detects many threats by continuously monitoring the VM 
instance in which it is running and its management interface. It also monitors the 
requests and responses sent to and from your App Service apps and the underlying 
sandboxes and VMs.
 ■ Defender for Key Vault uses machine learning to detect unusual and potentially harmful 
attempts to access or exploit Key Vault accounts.
 ■ Defender for Resource Manager uses advanced security analytics to detect threats and 
trigger an alert when a suspicious activity occurs.
 ■ Defender for Servers provides threat detection and advanced defenses for your  
Windows and Linux machines across Azure, Amazon Web Services (AWS), Google Cloud 
Platform (GCP), and on-premises environments.
 ■ Defender for Cloud has a type of alert called a security incident, which is raised in the 
dashboard whenever the system identifies multiple alerts that, when correlated with 
each other, indicate that those alerts belong to the same attack.
 ■ Workflow automation is a capability that helps you to automatically react on either a 
new alert, or on a new recommendation.
 ■ Vulnerability scan for VMs is part of Defender Vulnerability Management, which is 
included in Defender for Servers.

CHAPTER 8
Security Monitoring and 
Automation
The main goal of security operations is to maintain and restore the security assurances of 
the systems as adversaries attack them. The National Institute of Standards and Technol-
ogy (NIST) describes the security operations tasks in their Cybersecurity Framework: detect, 
respond, and recover. To be able to execute those functions in a cloud environment, you not 
only need the correct approach, but you also need to understand how the native tools work 
to provide you the data you need to limit the time and access an attacker can get to valuable 
systems and data.
Azure has native capabilities that you can leverage to continuously monitor the secu-
rity operations of your environment so you can quickly identify potential threats to your 
workloads.
Skill covered in this chapter:
 ■ Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring and automation solutions
Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring 
and automation solutions
Security monitoring is critical for any organization that wants to continue improving its secu-
rity posture, and there are native capabilities in Azure that can help you with that. Organiza-
tions need to empower their Security Operations Teams to better manage the influx of data 
and adopt a Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) and Security Orchestration, 
Automation, and Response (SOAR) solution. This section of this chapter covers the configu -
ration and management of security monitoring and automation solutions according to the 
Exam AZ-500 outline.
Monitor security events by using Azure Monitor
One common question about Azure Monitor is, “How do I enable it?” Azure Monitor is 
automatically enabled when you create a new Azure subscription. At that point, the Activity 
Log is active, and platform metrics are automatically collected. Another common question is, 
“Can Azure Monitor also monitor resources that are on-premises?“ Although Azure Monitor 
341
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation342
implies (by the name) that the resources are in Azure, it also collects data from virtual machines 
and applications in other clouds and on-premises to monitor.
For this reason, before making any sort of configuration in Azure Monitor, it is important to 
understand some foundational concepts of this platform. The section that follows covers some 
key principles. The diagram shown in Figure 8-1 helps you better understand the breadth of 
Azure Monitor and the different areas that it touches.
Application
Operating system
Azure resources
Azure subscription
Azure tenant
Application Insights
Data visualization
Data analysis
Response via alerts and operations
Integration with other services
These components can be in Azure, another cloud provider, or on-premises.
Logs
Metrics
Azure Monitor
FIGURE 8-1 Architecture diagram of an Azure Monitor solution
On the left side of Figure 8-1, you have the layers representing the components that will 
generate logs, which Azure Monitor can ingest. From the application and operating sys-
tem perspective, the machine can be physically located on-premises, in Azure, or another 
cloud provider. Aside from these data sources, you can also ingest data from different Azure 
resources, subscriptions, and the Azure tenant itself. This data is ingested into the Log Analyt-
ics Workspace, which is part of the Azure Monitor solution, and once the data is there, you 
can query it using Kusto Query Language, which uses schema entities that are organized in a 
hierarchy similar to SQL’s databases, tables, and columns.
The last three layers on the left side of the diagram shown in Figure 8-1 represent the three 
major layers in Azure where you can obtain logging information:
 ■ Azure Resources  Here, you can obtain resource logs, which have operations executed 
in the data plane level of Azure. An example of that would be getting a secret from 
Azure Key Vault. These logs are also referred to as diagnostic logs.
 ■ Azure Subscription  Here, you can obtain Activity Logs, which have operations 
executed in the Management Plane. You should review these logs when you need to 
determine the answer for the what  (what operation was made), who (who made this 
operation), and when (when this operation was made). For example, if a VM was deleted, 
you should go to Azure Activity Log to find out the answer of the what , who, and when 
regarding the delete VM operation.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring and automation solutions   CHAPTER 8 343
 ■ Azure Tenant Here, you can obtain the Azure Active Directory logs. In this layer, you 
have the history of sign-in activity and audit trail of changes made in the Azure Active 
Directory.
EXAM TIP
It is very important to understand those layers when studying for the AZ-500 exam because 
you may have scenarios where you will need to select the right option regarding where to 
look for specific information. For example, the Contoso administrator wants to identify the 
user who stopped the virtual machine two weeks ago. Where should they be searching for 
this information? If you answered “Azure Activity Log,” you are correct. In the Activity Log, 
you will find Management Plane operations and the identification of the what , who, and 
when an operation was performed.
Metrics are another type of information that can be ingested. Metrics are numerical values 
that describe some aspect of a system at a particular point in time. Telemetry, such as events, 
traces, and performance data, is stored as logs to be combined for analysis. This type of infor-
mation can be used when collecting security-related performance counters from multiple VMs 
and creating alerts based on certain thresholds.
Because Azure Monitor starts collecting data from a resource upon the creation of that 
resource, it is important to know where to look when you need information about those 
resources. Many resources summarize the relevant performance data for that resource, which 
is usually located on the resource’s Overview page. For example, the Monitoring/Insights  
option in an Azure Storage account provides details regarding transactions by storage type 
and API name, as shown in Figure 8-2.
FIGURE 8-2 Summary of storage account performance insights
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation344
You should use the Azure Activity Log to query logs executing operations in the  
Management Plane. Activity Log events are retained in Azure for 90 days before they are 
deleted, with no charge for entries during this period, regardless of volume. 
You can view the change history for certain events to see what changes occurred during that 
event. To do this, select an event from the Activity Log that you want to examine more closely. 
Then, click the Change History tab to view any changes to the resource up to 30 minutes before 
and after the event. As shown in Figure 8-3, any associated changes will be listed.
FIGURE 8-3 Change History
Suppose you need to correlate Activity Log data with other collected monitoring data or 
consolidate log entries from multiple Azure subscriptions and tenants into one location for 
analysis. In that case, you should consider exporting the Azure Activity Logs to a Log Analytics 
workspace. This option is available on the Activity Log  page when you click the Export  
Activity Logs  button, as shown in Figure 8-4.
FIGURE 8-4  Export Activity Logs option
Activity Log data in a Log Analytics workspace is stored in a table called AzureActivity, 
which you can retrieve using a log query in Log Analytics. The structure of this table varies 
based on the category of the log entry. Once the export is configured, you can run queries 
using Kusto Query Language (KQL) from the Logs  option, which integrates with the Log  
Analytics workspace, as shown in Figure 8-5.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

EXAM TIP
On the AZ-500 exam, you may be challenged to select the best option based on a  
business scenario. If the scenario requires the retention of the logs for more than 90 days 
for purposes such as audit, static analysis, or backup, then the ideal solution is to export the 
Activity Log to an Azure Storage account.
Configure data connectors in Microsoft Sentinel
Microsoft Sentinel is Microsoft’s cloud-native SIEM solution. It is the first SIEM solution built 
into a major public cloud platform. Microsoft Sentinel also contains a Security Orchestra-
tion and Automated Response (SOAR) capability. Microsoft Sentinel’s SOAR capability is fully 
customizable and allows security teams to write Playbooks that can (if desired) automate the 
entire response to a security event.
Microsoft Sentinel provides security teams with unprecedented visibility into their digital 
estate. The core capabilities of the solution include:
 ■ Data collection and storage across all users, devices, applications, and infrastructure, 
whether on-premises or in the cloud
 ■ Threat detection leveraging Microsoft’s analytics and threat intelligence
 ■ Investigation of threats by hunting for suspicious activities at scale
 ■ Rapid response to incidents leveraging built-in orchestration and automation of  
common tasks
You can use built-in connectors to ingest data into Microsoft Sentinel. Each data connector 
will have its own prerequisites, including the license type for the service you are trying to ingest 
data from. It is important to highlight that the following data sources are free to ingest:
 ■ Azure Activity Logs
 ■ Microsoft Sentinel Health
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation346
 ■ Office 365 Audit Logs, including all SharePoint activity, Exchange admin activity, and 
Teams
 ■ Security alerts from:
 ■ Microsoft Defender XDR
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Cloud
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Office 365
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Identity
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Endpoint
 ■ Alerts from:
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Cloud
 ■ Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps

Create and customize analytics rules in Microsoft Sentinel
The use of analytics can be extremely beneficial for creating custom alerts that will trigger 
indicators of compromise found in the systems. This is a powerful way to identify compromised 
systems without warning from other security controls (such as antimalware that relies on sig-
natures). While this is considered reactive work because the system was already compromised, 
you can also use analytics to identify whether a system is under attack. You can create alerts 
that use indicators of attack (IOA). 
By using analytics to create alerts based on an IOA, you can identify a potential attack in 
execution; for example, you can identify an attempt to elevate privileges to execute a built-in 
Windows tool, such as PowerShell, to download a piece of malware from a compromised site. 
In Microsoft Sentinel, the rules users create are called analytic rules. A rule comprises several 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation350
parts defining how the rule should trigger and how the incident should be handled. Follow 
these steps to access the Analytics dashboard:
1. Open the Azure portal and sign in as a user who has Microsoft Sentinel Contributor 
privileges.
2.
 In the search pane, type Microsoft Sentinel and click the Microsoft Sentinel icon when 
it appears.
3.
 Select the workspace on which Microsoft Sentinel is enabled.
4. In the left navigation pane, in the Configuration  section, click Analytics . The  
Microsoft Sentinel Analytics  blade appears, as shown in Figure 8-10.
FIGURE 8-10 Microsoft Sentinel Analytics blade
5. There are several components on the Analytics  blade. In the top pane, click the  
+ Create button and choose Scheduled Query Rule ; the Analytics Rule Wizard – 
Create A New Scheduled Rule page appears, as shown in Figure 8-11.
6. In the General section, the Name  field is the detection rule’s name and the incident’s 
display name that will be generated if triggered. It is important to use a descriptive 
name that will allow your security analysts to understand what the alert is about. You 
can further describe the incident by using the Description  field to provide more detail 
for your security analysts. The Tactics And Techniques  dropdown allows you to select 
the MITRE tactic(s)/technique(s) that the rule helps detect. The Severity  dropdown 
offers four options: High , Medium, Low, and Informational . You can use this setting 
to override the alert severity for a connected data source that sends alerts; also, you can 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring and automation solutions   CHAPTER 8 351
use this setting to set the severity for a created analytic. Severity should be used to help 
your security analysts prioritize and triage their responses and the incidents that are 
created. Lastly, you can set the Status  to either Enabled  or Disabled . Once you finish, 
click the Next: Set Rule Logic > button. The Set Rule Logic  tab appears, as shown in 
Figure 8-12.
FIGURE 8-11 Creating a new analytic rule
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation352
FIGURE 8-12 Configuring the rule logic
7. In the Rule Query  field, type the query that you want this rule to execute to validate it 
against your current data. Microsoft Sentinel will simulate the query against your data 
and show how many daily alerts would be generated using the logic settings. This allows 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring and automation solutions   CHAPTER 8 353
the analytics rule creator to test and adjust the rule before ever putting it into  
production. This can reduce the analysts’ workload and ensure they are only alerted on 
what matters. 
8.
 In the Alert Enhancement  section, you can define the entities returned as part of the 
data queried in the Rule Query Using Entity Mapping. Entities are important because 
they allow you to select which field from the data returned represents a user, host, 
 IP address, or other entity type. This information might be different column names 
across data sets, and the mapping allows you to normalize the data into entities.  
Entities are very important for incidents and investigations. Not all alert rules will have 
all entity types. For example, an alert rule based on firewall log data might only contain 
IP address entities. Mapping more entities when creating a rule will be useful when 
responding to incidents. Doing so will help the analysts understand which user or  
computer was involved or which IP address was used by the source machine. 
9.
 In Custom Details , you can surface event data as part of the alert properties. You can 
map event fields to alert properties using key-value pairs. 
10. In Alert Details , you can override or populate additional context into the alert rule’s 
general properties. For example, you could inject the Provider Name  into the Alert/
Incident name. 
11. The Query Scheduling  section is where you set how often to trigger the query and how 
far back in the data to query against. The Run Query Every  field defines how often you 
want to evaluate the query against your data. You might have a rule that runs every  
5 minutes or once every 24 hours. The Lookup Data From The Last value should be 
less than or equal to the Run Query Every Setting because you don’t want to query  
1 hour of data every 5 minutes. If you did this, the trigger would create multiple inci-
dents of the same alert. Both options can be between 5 minutes and 14 days. 
12.
 The Alert Threshold  section is where you set the number of results required for the 
rule to generate an incident. 
13. The Generate Alert When The Number Of Query Results  trigger supports the  
following operators: Is Greater Than , Is Fewer Than, Is Equal To, or Is Not Equal To. 
Then, you define the number for the threshold.
14. In the Event Grouping  section, you can configure if you want to group all events into 
one alert or separate each event into its own alert. 
15. In the Suppression  section, you can set the suppression to On  or Off. This option allows 
some basic suppression of the rule to prevent additional incidents from being created  
if the rule is triggered when you want it to be suppressed. If you select On , the Stop  
Running Query For field appears. You can set this to anywhere between 5 minutes  
and 24 hours. When you finish configuring these options, click the Next: Incident  
Settings > button. The Incident Settings tab appears, as shown in Figure 8-13.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation354
FIGURE 8-13 Incident Settings tab
16. First, you can choose whether to create an incident for each alert triggered by the rule. 
In most cases, you would create an incident for each alert, but there are cases where  
you might have two rules that create alerts and another rule that looks for both alerts  
created within a timespan to trigger an incident. These can be thought of as alert  
correlation rules. 
17.
 In the Alert Grouping section, you can group related alerts into the same incident.  
If disabled, each alert will be its own incident. If enabled, you choose the time limit for 
grouping, the grouping logic, and reopening incident options. In most cases, we recom-
mend enabling grouping and choosing Grouping Alerts Into A Single Incident If All 
The Entites Match option.

 Once you finish configuring this option, click the Next: Automated Response>  button. 
The Automated Response  tab appears, as shown in Figure 8-14.
19. The Automated Response  tab enables you to select a Playbook for alert automation or 
an automation rule for incident automation. This allows you to automate the response 
to alerts or incidents. This automation can run a query to gather more data and enrich

Evaluate alerts and incidents in Microsoft Sentinel
In today’s cyber landscape, the number of threats is increasing, which leads to an increasing 
number of alerts security teams need to respond to. Microsoft Sentinel can enrich alerts with 
data from other sources, investigate entities for more context, orchestrate across the organi-
zation, and act on incidents. Microsoft Sentinel can reduce the time to resolution for security 
incidents and allow security teams to focus on the most important alerts. Many, if not most, 
of these alerts conform to recurring patterns that can be addressed by specific and defined 
courses of action, which can be automated to allow the security operation center to focus on 
what matters most.
An incident in Microsoft Sentinel can be created through 
 ■ A scheduled analytics rule based on either one or more alerts or notable events
 ■ An incident created by one of the data connectors based on one or more alerts
 ■ A Fusion incident based on fused and correlated events
 ■ An incident based on a match with one or more threat indicators
 ■ An incident based on a hunting query
 ■ A manually created incident through the Security.Insights API, PowerShell, or the 
Azure portal
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

CHAPTER 8  Security Monitoring and Automation356
In Microsoft Sentinel, the rules can be categorized according to their detections:
 ■ Anomaly These are built-in rules that use machine learning (ML) against your data to 
detect specific types of threats. These rules have a few parameters and thresholds that 
can be configured, but Microsoft protects the ML models used. You can enable these 
rules in testing mode first to see how they would perform against your data, and when 
ready, you can move them to production.
 ■ Fusion This rule has no settings that can be configured. It can only be enabled or 
disabled in the workspace. If the alerts are part of the same attack, the fusion rule will 
correlate low-fidelity alerts across alert rules into a single high-fidelity incident.
 ■ Machine Learning behavioral  These rule templates are based on Microsoft  
proprietary ML algorithms. The ML is applied to specific data sources and events to 
detect anomalous behavior in the data.
 ■ Microsoft Security  Enables you to create an incident in Microsoft Sentinel from any 
existing security alert that comes from these solutions.
 ■ Near-real-time (NRT)  Allows you to run detections up to the minute. Because 
scheduled rules only allow a query to run as little as once every 5 minutes, NRT rules are 
hardcoded to run every 1 minute on the last minute of data.
 ■ Scheduled  A typical SIEM rule that runs on a timed basis and looks at data from a 
specific period of time. These rules trigger on a configured threshold.
 ■ Threat Intelligence (TI)  These rules match TI data from Threat Intelligence against 
Common Event Format (CEF), DNS, and Syslog data.
You can use analytic rule templates to create your own rule based on many available 
templates based on the detections above. To access these templates, click Analytics  in the 
Configuration section and then click the Rule Templates  tab. Select the template you want to 
use, and then the Create Rule  button will be available, as shown in Figure 8-15.
FIGURE 8-15 Rule Templates tab
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 8.1: Configure and manage security monitoring and automation solutions   CHAPTER 8 357
When you create an analytics rule, an access permissions token is applied and saved with 
the rule. This token ensures that the rule can access the workspace containing the queried 
data, maintaining access even if the rule’s creator loses access to that workspace. However, 
there’s an exception. For rules accessing workspaces in other subscriptions or tenants—such 
as those managed by a managed services provider—Microsoft Sentinel implements addi-
tional security measures to prevent unauthorized access to customer data. In these cases,  
the credentials of the user who created the rule are applied instead of an independent  
access token. Consequently, the rule ceases to function if the user loses access to the other 
subscription or tenant.
Configure automation in Microsoft Sentinel
Automation rules help you manage incidents in Microsoft Sentinel by automatically assigning 
them to the appropriate personnel, closing noisy incidents or known false positives, changing 
their severity, and adding tags. They also enable the execution of Playbooks in response to 
incidents or alerts. Playbooks are collections of procedures that can be executed from Micro-
soft Sentinel in response to an entire incident, an individual alert, or a specific entity. By being 
linked to an automation rule, they can automate and orchestrate your response and be config -
ured to run automatically when certain alerts are generated or when incidents are created or 
updated. 
When you create an analytic or automation rule, you can define a Playbook to trigger. The 
Playbook is automatically run when the analytic or the automation rule is triggered, and it fol-
lows the Playbook’s steps as you have configured them. Use the following steps to create and 
configure a Playbook automation.
1.
 Open the Azure portal and sign in as a user who has Microsoft Sentinel Contributor 
privileges.
2.
 In the search pane, type Microsoft Sentinel and click the Sentinel icon when it  
appears.
3.
 Select the workspace on which Microsoft Sentinel has been enabled.
4. In the left navigation pane, in the Configuration  section, click Automation.
5. Click the +Create dropdown and select the Playbook  With Incident Trigger.  
The Create Playbook  page appears, as shown in Figure 8-16.
6. Select the correct subscription from the Subscription  dropdown. Select the  
appropriate Resource Group . 
7. In the Playbook Name field, type the name for this Playbook. Click the Next:  
Connections >  button. The Connections  tab appears, as shown in Figure 8-17.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

FIGURE 8-17 Connections tab
